# Question bank audit — 2026-10-04

Reviewed 1798 questions blind with Claude Opus 5 (the reviewer never saw the answer key).

| Verdict | Count |
|---|---|
| ok | 1260 |
| wrong_key | 33 |
| ambiguous | 149 |
| needs_manual_review | 0 |
| minor | 356 |

Batch cost: about $10.21 (1798 questions, so about $0.0057 each).

## wrong_key (33)

### R&W · Command of Evidence (Quantitative) · easy · #27 (q00267)

> A scientist studying plant growth records the average number of days it takes for four different vegetable crops to reach full maturity from the time of planting. The scientist claims that lettuce matures more quickly than any other crop in the study.
> Table: Average Days to Maturity for Vegetable Crops
> | Crop       | Avg. Days to Maturity |
> |------------|-----------------------|
> | Lettuce    | 30                    |
> | Radishes   | 28                    |
> | Spinach    | 40                    |
> | Tomatoes   | 75                    |

**Q:** Which choice best uses data from the table to support the scientist's claim?

- A) Tomatoes take an average of 75 days to mature, the longest of any crop in the study.
- B) Radishes mature in 28 days, which is faster than lettuce.
- C) Lettuce matures in 30 days, faster than spinach at 40 days and tomatoes at 75 days.
- D) Spinach and tomatoes both take longer than lettuce to reach full maturity.

**Key:** D · **Reviewer:** C (medium)
- Also defensible: **D** — D makes exactly the same logical point as C (spinach and tomatoes take longer than lettuce), just without restating the numbers; since neither C nor D addresses radishes, the two choices are equivalent in how well they 'support' the claim, with C preferred only because it cites specific figures.
- no_correct_answer: The scientist's claim is factually contradicted by the table: radishes mature in 28 days, faster than lettuce's 30 days. No choice can legitimately support a false claim, so the item's premise is broken. A well-constructed SAT quantitative-evidence item requires the data to actually be consistent with the claim being supported.
- multiple_correct_answers: Choices C and D convey the identical supporting information (spinach and tomatoes both take longer than lettuce); D simply omits the explicit numbers. Both 'use data from the table' and support the claim to the same degree, making the intended distinction arbitrary.

### R&W · Command of Evidence (Quantitative) · medium-high · #18 (q00318)

> A linguist studying language acquisition in bilingual children claimed that children exposed to two languages from birth reach single-word vocabulary milestones at roughly the same age as monolingual children when vocabulary is measured across both languages combined, but that bilingual children tend to have smaller vocabularies in each individual language at those same ages. The linguist argued this finding challenges the assumption that bilingual upbringing delays overall language development.
> Table: Vocabulary Milestones in Bilingual and Monolingual Children (avg. age in months)
> | Milestone | Monolingual (age in months) | Bilingual – Language A only | Bilingual – Language B only | Bilingual – Combined |
> |---|---|---|---|---|
> | 50-word vocabulary | 22.1 | 26.4 | 25.8 | 22.3 |
> | 100-word vocabulary | 28.6 | 33.1 | 32.7 | 28.9 |
> | First word combinations | 19.4 | 22.6 | 21.9 | 19.7 |

**Q:** Which choice best uses data from the table to support the linguist's claim?

- A) Bilingual children reached the 50-word vocabulary milestone at 26.4 months in Language A and 25.8 months in Language B, both later than the 22.1 months recorded for monolingual children.
- B) Bilingual children reached all three milestones — 50-word vocabulary, 100-word vocabulary, and first word combinations — within 0.3 months of monolingual children when vocabulary was measured across both languages combined, yet consistently lagged behind monolinguals by 3 to 4.5 months when only one language was considered.
- C) The combined bilingual vocabulary milestone ages of 22.3, 28.9, and 19.7 months were all very close to the monolingual ages of 22.1, 28.6, and 19.4 months, suggesting that total language development is not delayed.
- D) For the 100-word vocabulary milestone, bilingual children in Language A averaged 33.1 months and in Language B averaged 32.7 months, both approximately 4 months behind monolinguals, while the combined bilingual measure of 28.9 months nearly matched the monolingual average of 28.6 months.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** D (medium)
- Also defensible: **B** — B is the apparently intended answer because it addresses both prongs of the claim across all three milestones; however, its stated gap range of '3 to 4.5 months' is inaccurate (Language B's first word combinations at 21.9 vs. 19.4 months is a gap of only 2.5 months), so it misstates the table data.
- factual_or_math_error: Choice B misstates the data: it claims the single-language lag is 'consistently... 3 to 4.5 months,' but the Language B first-word-combinations gap is 21.9 − 19.4 = 2.5 months, outside that range.
- multiple_correct_answers: Because B (the likely intended key) contains a data inaccuracy, D — which accurately cites both the single-language lag (~4 months) and the near-identical combined measure, thus supporting both parts of the linguist's claim — becomes an equally or more defensible answer.

### R&W · Command of Evidence (Quantitative) · medium-high · #20 (q00320)

> A sports physiologist studying endurance training argued that incremental increases in weekly training mileage are associated with systematic improvements in runners' VO₂ max scores, but that these gains plateau or diminish once weekly mileage exceeds approximately 70 miles. The physiologist suggested that training programs should be designed with this threshold in mind to maximize cardiovascular benefit without incurring the diminishing returns seen at higher mileage levels.
> Table: Weekly Training Mileage and Average VO₂ Max in Recreational Runners
> | Weekly Mileage Range (miles) | Number of Runners | Avg. VO₂ Max (mL/kg/min) |
> |---|---|---|
> | 20–30 | 38 | 44.2 |
> | 31–45 | 41 | 49.8 |
> | 46–60 | 37 | 54.6 |
> | 61–70 | 29 | 58.1 |
> | 71–85 | 24 | 58.9 |
> | 86+ | 18 | 59.2 |

**Q:** Which choice best uses data from the table to support the physiologist's claim?

- A) Runners in the highest mileage category (86+ miles/week) had an average VO₂ max of 59.2 mL/kg/min, only marginally higher than the 58.9 recorded for runners logging 71–85 miles per week.
- B) Average VO₂ max rose steadily from 44.2 mL/kg/min in the 20–30 miles group to 58.1 in the 61–70 miles group, a gain of nearly 14 points, but increased by only 1.1 points across the two highest mileage groups combined.
- C) Runners logging 46–60 miles per week had an average VO₂ max of 54.6 mL/kg/min, 9.9 points below the 58.1 recorded for runners in the 61–70 miles group, suggesting the greatest gains occur before the 70-mile threshold.
- D) The four groups below 70 miles per week showed an average VO₂ max gain of approximately 4.6 points per mileage bracket, while the two groups above 70 miles showed a combined gain of only 1.1 points.

**Key:** D · **Reviewer:** B (medium)
- Also defensible: **D** — D also addresses both parts of the claim (steady gains below 70 miles, negligible gain above), and its figures are consistent with the table (13.9 points over three brackets ≈ 4.6 per bracket; 1.1 points above 70). Its only weakness is the loose phrasing 'four groups ... per mileage bracket,' which invites dividing by four rather than by three.
- multiple_correct_answers: Both B and D supply data supporting the two components of the physiologist's claim (systematic gains up to ~70 miles, plateau beyond). B is cleaner because it quotes table values directly, while D requires a derived average whose wording is imprecise, but a careful reader could defend D.

### R&W · Command of Evidence (Quantitative) · medium-high · #24 (q00324)

> A materials scientist studying thermal insulation properties argued that aerogel composites retain a significantly lower thermal conductivity than conventional fiberglass and foam insulation materials across a range of temperatures. The scientist further claimed that this advantage becomes more pronounced at higher temperatures, making aerogel composites particularly well-suited for high-temperature industrial applications where conventional insulators perform poorly.
> Table: Thermal Conductivity (W/m·K) by Material and Temperature
> | Temperature (°C) | Aerogel Composite | Fiberglass | Polyurethane Foam |
> |---|---|---|---|
> | 25 | 0.014 | 0.040 | 0.026 |
> | 100 | 0.016 | 0.052 | 0.038 |
> | 200 | 0.019 | 0.071 | 0.059 |
> | 400 | 0.024 | 0.118 | N/A |
> | 600 | 0.031 | 0.174 | N/A |

**Q:** Which choice best uses data from the table to support the scientist's claim?

- A) At 600°C, aerogel composite had a thermal conductivity of 0.031 W/m·K, compared to 0.174 W/m·K for fiberglass — a difference of 0.143, far larger than the difference of 0.026 between the two materials at 25°C.
- B) Aerogel composite maintained lower thermal conductivity than both fiberglass and polyurethane foam at every temperature where all three materials were tested, while also showing the smallest absolute increase in conductivity from 25°C to 200°C (0.005 W/m·K, compared to 0.031 for fiberglass and 0.033 for foam).
- C) Fiberglass thermal conductivity rose from 0.040 W/m·K at 25°C to 0.174 W/m·K at 600°C, more than quadrupling, while aerogel composite rose from only 0.014 to 0.031 W/m·K over the same range — less than a 2.5-fold increase — showing that the aerogel's advantage grows substantially at higher temperatures.
- D) At 25°C, aerogel composite's thermal conductivity of 0.014 W/m·K was less than half that of polyurethane foam (0.026) and less than half that of fiberglass (0.040), demonstrating aerogel's superiority as an insulator even at ambient temperatures.

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** B (medium)
- Also defensible: **C** — C accurately uses the full temperature range (25°C–600°C) to show that fiberglass's conductivity rises far faster than the aerogel's, directly supporting the second part of the claim that the aerogel's advantage grows at high temperatures — the part most relevant to 'high-temperature industrial applications.' Its only shortfall is that it omits polyurethane foam (for which no high-temperature data exist).
- unclear_or_ambiguous: The claim has two parts (lower conductivity than BOTH conventional materials across temperatures, and a widening advantage at HIGH temperatures). B best covers both materials and both parts but limits its evidence to 25–200°C, while C covers the high-temperature range (up to 600°C) that the claim emphasizes but compares only to fiberglass. Reasonable reviewers could defend either, making the 'best' answer less than decisively determined.

### R&W · Command of Evidence (Quantitative) · hard · #14 (q00344)

> A sociologist studying intergenerational economic mobility in five metropolitan regions proposed that regions with higher public investment in early childhood education — measured as per-pupil spending in pre-K programs — show greater upward mobility rates among children born into the lowest income quartile. She further specified that this relationship holds independently of regional differences in median household income, and that higher-spending regions show higher mobility even when compared with higher-income regions that spend less on early education.
> Table: Early Childhood Education Spending and Upward Mobility by Region
> | Region | Pre-K Spending ($/pupil) | Median Household Income ($) | Upward Mobility Rate (%) |
> |--------|--------------------------|------------------------------|--------------------------|
> | Alton  | 9,400                    | 61,000                       | 38                       |
> | Breck  | 6,100                    | 74,500                       | 27                       |
> | Crowe  | 11,800                   | 58,200                       | 46                       |
> | Dural  | 4,700                    | 79,300                       | 21                       |
> | Edlen  | 8,200                    | 66,800                       | 33                       |

**Q:** Which choice best uses data from the table to support the sociologist's claim?

- A) Crowe, which spends the most per pupil on pre-K ($11,800) and has the lowest median household income ($58,200) of the five regions, also has the highest upward mobility rate (46%), suggesting that early education investment can compensate for lower regional wealth.
- B) Dural has the highest median household income ($79,300) but the lowest upward mobility rate (21%) and the lowest pre-K spending ($4,700), while Crowe has the lowest median income ($58,200) but the highest mobility rate (46%) and highest pre-K spending ($11,800), directly supporting the claim that early education spending predicts mobility independently of regional income.
- C) Regions with pre-K spending above $8,000 per pupil — Alton, Crowe, and Edlen — all had upward mobility rates above 33%, while the two regions spending below $7,000 — Breck and Dural — had mobility rates of 27% and 21% respectively, regardless of their higher median household incomes.
- D) The correlation between pre-K spending and upward mobility is evident across all five regions: as spending increases from $4,700 to $11,800, mobility rises from 21% to 46%, while median household income shows no consistent directional relationship with mobility rates.

**Key:** D · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- Also defensible: **A** — A is factually accurate about Crowe and addresses the 'compensates for lower wealth' idea, but it relies on a single region and so supports the claim less completely than B, which contrasts Crowe with the high-income, low-spending, low-mobility Dural.

### R&W · Inferences · medium-low · #19 (q00409)

> In many traditional hunter-gatherer societies, the division of labor between men and women was more flexible than popular accounts suggest. Archaeological and ethnographic evidence indicates that in some groups, women regularly participated in hunting, while men contributed to plant gathering. Researchers have found projectile points—tools associated with hunting—buried alongside women in several ancient grave sites. These findings challenge the long-held assumption that a strict, universal division of labor defined prehistoric human societies, suggesting instead that roles varied considerably across different groups and environments.

**Q:** Based on the passage, what can be inferred about the "long-held assumption" the researchers challenge?

- A) It was based on evidence from a wide variety of ancient cultures across multiple continents.
- B) It held that hunting and gathering roles were rigidly assigned by gender in all prehistoric societies.
- C) It was first proposed by archaeologists who studied the projectile points found in grave sites.
- D) It accurately described the labor divisions in some societies while overstating their universality.

**Key:** D · **Reviewer:** B (high)

### R&W · Words in Context · hard · #12 (q00582)

> Marine biologists studying bioluminescent organisms in the mesopelagic zone have
> long noted the phenomenon's dual utility: light produced by deep-sea creatures serves
> both as a lure for prey and as a form of counterillumination, matching the faint glow
> from the surface above and thereby obscuring the creature's silhouette from predators
> below. What is remarkable is how precisely calibrated this counterillumination can be —
> the organism adjusts its luminescent output almost continuously in response to changing
> ambient light levels.

**Q:** As used in the passage, "calibrated" most nearly means

- A) measured
- B) invented
- C) adjusted
- D) illuminated

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** C (high)

### R&W · Words in Context · hard · #19 (q00589)

> Philosophers of science have long debated whether theoretical elegance constitutes
> genuine evidence for a theory's truth, or whether it functions merely as a heuristic
> guide toward hypotheses worth testing. The concern is not trivial: if elegance is
> treated as epistemic rather than pragmatic, then aesthetic preference risks being
> laundered into a criterion of truth. A theory's beauty may indeed track deep structural
> features of reality, but this correlation, if it exists, stands in need of independent
> justification rather than being assumed.

**Q:** As used in the passage, "laundered" most nearly means

- A) disguised
- B) cleaned
- C) transformed
- D) smuggled

**Key:** D · **Reviewer:** C (medium)
- Also defensible: **D** — "Smuggled" captures the connotation of illegitimate introduction that the money-laundering metaphor carries, though "smuggled into a criterion of truth" does not fit the sentence's grammar as naturally as "transformed into."

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · medium-high · #20 (q00800)

> The aesthetic philosophy known as formalism holds that the value of a work of art lies exclusively in its formal properties — line, composition, rhythm, structure — rather than in any moral lesson it conveys or biographical context it reflects. For formalists, evaluating a painting by asking what it says about the artist's personal struggles is a category error. The work's meaning and worth emerge from within the work itself, from relationships among its internal elements.
> Passage 2:
> Purely formalist readings often produce interpretations that are elegant but curiously bloodless. Art does not arrive in a vacuum: it is made by particular people in particular historical circumstances, and those circumstances shape not only what artists choose to depict but how formal choices themselves acquire meaning. A stark composition may read as serenity in one cultural context and menace in another. Divorcing formal analysis from cultural and biographical context does not produce purity — it produces selective blindness.
> Correct Answer: D
> Passage 1:
> The aesthetic philosophy known as formalism holds that the value of a work of art lies exclusively in its formal properties — line, composition, rhythm, structure — rather than in any moral lesson it conveys or biographical context it reflects. For formalists, evaluating a painting by asking what it says about the artist's personal struggles is a category error. The work's meaning and worth emerge from within the work itself, from relationships among its internal elements.
> Passage 2:
> Purely formalist readings often produce interpretations that are elegant but curiously bloodless. Art does not arrive in a vacuum: it is made by particular people in particular historical circumstances, and those circumstances shape not only what artists choose to depict but how formal choices themselves acquire meaning. A stark composition may read as serenity in one cultural context and menace in another. Divorcing formal analysis from cultural and biographical context does not produce purity — it produces selective blindness.

**Q:** The author of Passage 2 would most likely respond to the formalist claim in Passage 1 that a work's meaning emerges "from relationships among its internal elements" by arguing that:

- A) Internal formal elements are less important than moral content in determining the lasting value of a work of art
- B) Formalist methodology is useful for analyzing music and literature but fails when applied to visual art
- C) The very meaning of formal elements such as composition and structure is partly determined by the cultural and historical context in which they are encountered
- D) Formalist analysis is too abstract to be understood by audiences without specialized training in art theory

**Key:** D · **Reviewer:** C (high)
- factual_or_math_error: The stated correct answer is D, but D is unsupported: Passage 2 never mentions abstraction or audience training. The textually supported answer is C, which directly paraphrases Passage 2's claim that formal choices acquire meaning through cultural/historical context (e.g., a stark composition reading as serenity in one context and menace in another). The answer key appears to be mislabeled.

### R&W · Transitions · easy · #21 (q00861)

> Monarch butterflies migrate thousands of miles each year between their summer habitats in North America and their winter homes in Mexico. This journey requires enormous amounts of energy. _______, monarchs feed heavily on milkweed and other nectar-rich plants before beginning their migration, building up fat reserves that will fuel their long flight south.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text with the most logical transition?

- A) Nevertheless
- B) In contrast
- C) To prepare
- D) As a result

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** D (high)
- Also defensible: **C** — "To prepare" expresses purpose and is logically coherent (the butterflies feed in order to prepare for migration), though it is somewhat redundant with "before beginning their migration" and is not a standard transition word.
- multiple_correct_answers: Choice C ("To prepare") also yields a logical sentence, which weakens the uniqueness of the intended causal answer D ("As a result"); a cleaner distractor would avoid a purpose phrase that fits the context.

### R&W · Transitions · medium-low · #2 (q00872)

> The Impressionist painters of nineteenth-century France were initially dismissed by the official art establishment, which rejected their work from major exhibitions. ________, figures like Monet, Renoir, and Degas organized their own independent shows, building an audience outside the institutions that had excluded them.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text with the most logical transition?

- A) As a result
- B) Similarly
- C) Nevertheless
- D) In particular

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** A (high)

### R&W · Transitions · medium-low · #23 (q00893)

> Many cities have invested heavily in expanding their public bicycle-sharing programs, citing benefits such as reduced traffic congestion and lower carbon emissions. _______, ridership data from several programs show that the majority of users are tourists and recreational riders rather than daily commuters. This pattern suggests that bicycle sharing may not be reducing car trips as significantly as city planners had hoped.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text with the most logical transition?

- A) Consequently,
- B) Furthermore,
- C) Nevertheless,
- D) In contrast,

**Key:** D · **Reviewer:** C (medium)

### R&W · Transitions · medium-high · #7 (q00907)

> The works of philosopher Simone Weil were largely unknown during her lifetime; she published little, circulated manuscripts only among small circles of friends, and died of tuberculosis at thirty-four in 1943. ________, her writings on labor, attention, and affliction are now considered foundational texts in twentieth-century political and religious thought.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text with the most logical transition?

- A) As a result
- B) Similarly
- C) Posthumously
- D) In contrast

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** D (high)
- Also defensible: **C** — "Posthumously" is factually consistent (her fame came after death), so a student could defend it, though it is a time adverb rather than a logical transition and is redundant with 'now'; it also fails to signal the needed contrast.

### R&W · Transitions · hard · #8 (q00938)

> Classical economic theory holds that markets in competitive equilibrium are efficient: prices reflect all available information, resources flow to their highest-valued uses, and no reallocation could make anyone better off without making someone else worse off. ________, this efficiency result depends on a set of idealized conditions — perfectly competitive markets, complete information, no externalities, no public goods — that are rarely if ever fully satisfied in real economies, which is why market failures are a central subject of applied economics rather than a theoretical curiosity.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text with the most logical transition?

- A) In fact
- B) Similarly
- C) This conclusion, however, holds only when
- D) As a result

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** A (medium)
- Also defensible: **C** — Its semantic content ('however... holds only when') is the contrast the passage needs, so a grader focused on meaning rather than grammar might mark it correct — but as written it cannot be inserted into the sentence grammatically.
- formatting: Choice C is not a transition but a partial clause ('This conclusion, however, holds only when'), which produces an ungrammatical sentence when inserted: '...holds only when, this efficiency result depends on...'. It appears to be a leftover from a rewritten version of the item, and it unfairly duplicates/obscures the contrast function the blank requires.
- unclear_or_ambiguous: The logical relationship is concessive/contrastive, but no clean contrast transition (e.g., 'However' or 'Nevertheless') is offered; the keyed answer must be 'In fact,' which SAT items more typically use for intensification, making the intended relationship ambiguous.

### R&W · Transitions · hard · #9 (q00939)

> Feminist philosophers have argued that the Western philosophical canon has systematically privileged certain ways of knowing — formal reasoning, abstraction, universality — and devalued others — care, particularity, emotion — in ways that map onto historically gendered divisions between masculine and feminine. ________, some scholars have cautioned that efforts to valorize "women's ways of knowing" as an alternative risk reinforcing the very gender essentialism that feminist philosophy aims to dismantle, by assuming that care and emotion are naturally or uniquely feminine rather than the product of social assignment.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text with the most logical transition?

- A) Furthermore
- B) In response to this critique, however
- C) For example
- D) This position has itself been challenged, however, by

**Key:** D · **Reviewer:** B (high)

### R&W · Transitions · hard · #10 (q00940)

> Political theorists have long distinguished between "negative liberty" — freedom from interference by others — and "positive liberty" — the actual capacity to pursue one's ends, which may require resources, education, or social conditions that the state must actively provide. Defenders of negative liberty argue that positive liberty is not really liberty at all but a rationalization for state expansion. ________, this objection assumes that the absence of coercion is sufficient to make a person free, ignoring the extent to which poverty, ignorance, or social exclusion can constrain choice as effectively as any direct prohibition.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text with the most logical transition?

- A) In fact
- B) Similarly
- C) The problem with this objection, however, is that
- D) As a result

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** A (medium)
- unclear_or_ambiguous: Choice C is not a transitional word/phrase (as SAT transition items require) but a full clause, and inserting it produces the redundant, non-standard sentence "The problem with this objection, however, is that this objection assumes..." Its presence muddies the item, since it expresses exactly the contrastive meaning the blank needs while being grammatically unusable. The ideal transition ('However') is absent, leaving 'In fact' as a somewhat weak but best available contrastive/corrective option.

### R&W · Boundaries · easy · #29 (q01107)

> Volunteers gathered at the community center early on Saturday morning. They organized supply boxes and loaded trucks with donations. By nine o'clock, the team was ready to head out, the coordinator gave a brief speech thanking everyone for their time and effort before they departed.

**Q:** Which choice most effectively corrects the punctuation error in the passage?

- A) By nine o'clock, the team was ready to head out. The coordinator gave a brief speech thanking everyone for their time and effort before they departed.
- B) By nine o'clock the team was ready to head out, the coordinator gave a brief speech thanking everyone for their time and effort before they departed.
- C) By nine o'clock, the team was ready to head out; the coordinator gave a brief speech, thanking everyone for their time and effort before they departed.
- D) By nine o'clock, the team was ready to head out, the coordinator gave a brief speech, thanking everyone for their time and effort, before they departed.

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** A (high)

### R&W · Boundaries · medium-low · #1 (q01109)

> The documentary was praised for its cinematography _______ critics noted that its narrative structure felt disjointed and difficult to follow.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text so that it conforms to the conventions of Standard Written English?

- A) , however
- B) ; however,
- C) , but
- D) although

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- Also defensible: **C** — A comma plus the coordinating conjunction 'but' correctly joins two independent clauses and conveys the same contrast; this is fully grammatical.
- Also defensible: **D** — 'Although' is a subordinating conjunction that makes the second clause dependent; 'The documentary was praised for its cinematography although critics noted...' is grammatically acceptable without a comma.
- multiple_correct_answers: B (semicolon + conjunctive adverb), C (comma + coordinating conjunction 'but'), and D (subordinating conjunction 'although') all produce grammatically correct sentences expressing the same contrast. Only A (comma splice with 'however') is clearly wrong, so the item has no single best answer.

### R&W · Boundaries · medium-low · #3 (q01111)

> Researchers have long studied the effects of sleep deprivation on cognitive function _______ a new study suggests that even mild, chronic sleep restriction — defined as fewer than seven hours per night — significantly impairs working memory.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text so that it conforms to the conventions of Standard Written English?

- A) ,
- B) , and
- C) ; a
- D) :

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- Also defensible: **C** — If the stray 'a' in the choice is a typo and the option is simply a semicolon, it would also correctly join the two independent clauses, making two keys correct.
- formatting: Choice C is written as '; a' even though the article 'a' already appears in the passage after the blank ('_______ a new study'), producing 'a a new study.' The option appears to be a mis-typed semicolon choice.
- multiple_correct_answers: Read as a plain semicolon (the apparent intent of C), both B (', and') and C would correctly link the two independent clauses.

### R&W · Boundaries · medium-low · #7 (q01115)

> Amara spent three years developing her startup _______ she had to balance building the business with working full-time to cover her living expenses.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text so that it conforms to the conventions of Standard Written English?

- A) ,
- B) ; at the same time,
- C) , during which time
- D) . During which time

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** B (medium)
- Also defensible: **C** — ", during which time she had to balance..." is also grammatically standard: the comma plus the relative phrase creates a nonrestrictive clause whose antecedent is the time period "three years," producing a correctly punctuated sentence with essentially the same meaning.
- multiple_correct_answers: Both B (semicolon + transition joining two independent clauses) and C (comma + nonrestrictive relative clause 'during which time') yield grammatically acceptable sentences. The only clear errors are A (comma splice) and D (sentence fragment), so the item does not isolate a single best answer.

### R&W · Boundaries · medium-low · #10 (q01118)

> The committee reached a unanimous decision after hours of debate _______ all five members agreed that the proposed budget cuts were too severe.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text so that it conforms to the conventions of Standard Written English?

- A) ,
- B) ;
- C) , so
- D) :

**Key:** D · **Reviewer:** B (medium)
- Also defensible: **D** — A colon is also conventionally correct here: the first clause is an independent clause, and the second clause elaborates on/explains what the unanimous decision was, which is exactly the function of a colon.
- multiple_correct_answers: Both the semicolon (B) and the colon (D) produce grammatically and rhetorically acceptable sentences. The second clause ('all five members agreed...') directly explains the 'unanimous decision,' which is a standard colon context, so there is no principled basis for ruling out D. Well-constructed SAT items do not offer both a semicolon and a colon when the second part is an independent clause that elaborates on the first.

### R&W · Boundaries · medium-high · #10 (q01148)

> The economist's argument is elegant in theory _______ it assumes perfect information, rational actors, and the absence of externalities — conditions that rarely obtain in real markets.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text so that it conforms to the conventions of Standard Written English?

- A) , but
- B) ;
- C) , yet
- D) , because

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** B (medium)
- Also defensible: **A** — A comma plus the coordinating conjunction 'but' correctly joins two independent clauses and expresses the contrast implied by the em-dash clause; it is grammatically unimpeachable.
- Also defensible: **C** — ', yet' is functionally identical to ', but' — a comma plus coordinating conjunction joining two independent clauses with a contrastive sense — so it is equally correct.
- multiple_correct_answers: Choices A (', but') and C (', yet') are synonymous coordinating conjunctions preceded by a comma; both correctly join the two independent clauses, so neither can be eliminated on conventions grounds. Choice B (semicolon) is also a correct joining of two independent clauses, and even D (', because') is punctuationally legal. Under Standard English conventions at least three options are defensible, leaving no single best answer.
- unclear_or_ambiguous: Because the item cannot be resolved by punctuation rules alone, students must guess at intended meaning (contrast vs. causation vs. neutral juxtaposition), which is not a conventions-based distinction tested by Boundaries questions.

### R&W · Boundaries · medium-high · #24 (q01162)

> The novelist Toni Morrison once described her writing process as one of radical attentiveness, she believed that language must be handled with a precision that honors both the story being told and the community from which that story emerges. For Morrison, every word choice carried moral weight; careless prose was not merely an aesthetic failure but an ethical one. This conviction shaped her editorial work at Random House as profoundly as it shaped her fiction.

**Q:** Which choice most effectively corrects the boundary error in the passage?

- A) The novelist Toni Morrison once described her writing process as one of radical attentiveness; she believed that language must be handled with a precision that honors both the story being told and the community from which that story emerges.
- B) The novelist Toni Morrison once described her writing process as one of radical attentiveness, believing that language must be handled with a precision that honors both the story being told and the community from which that story emerges.
- C) The novelist Toni Morrison once described her writing process as one of radical attentiveness. She believed that language must be handled with a precision that honors both the story being told and the community from which that story emerges.
- D) The novelist Toni Morrison once described her writing process as one of radical attentiveness — she believed that language must be handled with a precision that honors both the story being told and the community from which that story emerges.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** A (high)
- Also defensible: **C** — Splitting into two sentences with a period fully eliminates the comma splice and is grammatically correct.
- Also defensible: **B** — Converting the second clause into a participial phrase ('believing...') removes the independent clause entirely, making the comma correct.
- Also defensible: **D** — A single dash can legitimately join two independent clauses, so this also corrects the splice.
- multiple_correct_answers: The original error is a comma splice, and all four choices correct it (semicolon, participial modifier, period, dash). No choice introduces a new grammatical error, so there is no single defensible 'most effective' answer; the question lacks the usual SAT design where only one option is conventionally correct.
- unclear_or_ambiguous: 'Most effectively corrects' gives no criterion for choosing among four equally grammatical revisions, leaving the intended answer a matter of style preference rather than Standard English conventions.

### R&W · Boundaries · medium-high · #25 (q01163)

> The phenomenon known as "decision fatigue" suggests that the quality of human judgment deteriorates after a long session of decision-making. Researchers studying parole board hearings in Israel found that prisoners appearing early in the day received favorable rulings far more often than those appearing later, the judges, it seemed, defaulted to denial — the safer, lower-effort option — as their mental resources depleted throughout the day.

**Q:** Which choice most effectively corrects the boundary error in the passage?

- A) Researchers studying parole board hearings in Israel found that prisoners appearing early in the day received favorable rulings far more often than those appearing later. The judges, it seemed, defaulted to denial — the safer, lower-effort option — as their mental resources depleted throughout the day.
- B) Researchers studying parole board hearings in Israel found that prisoners appearing early in the day received favorable rulings far more often than those appearing later; the judges, it seemed, defaulted to denial — the safer, lower-effort option — as their mental resources depleted throughout the day.
- C) Researchers studying parole board hearings in Israel found that prisoners appearing early in the day received favorable rulings far more often than those appearing later, and the judges, it seemed, defaulted to denial — the safer, lower-effort option — as their mental resources depleted throughout the day.
- D) Researchers studying parole board hearings in Israel found that prisoners appearing early in the day received favorable rulings far more often than those appearing later: the judges, it seemed, defaulted to denial — the safer, lower-effort option — as their mental resources depleted throughout the day.

**Key:** D · **Reviewer:** B (medium)
- Also defensible: **A** — Splitting into two sentences with a period fully and correctly resolves the comma splice; nothing is grammatically wrong with it.
- Also defensible: **D** — A colon is acceptable here because the second independent clause explains/elaborates on the finding in the first.
- Also defensible: **C** — Comma plus the coordinating conjunction 'and' is a standard, grammatically correct way to join two independent clauses.
- multiple_correct_answers: The underlying error is a comma splice, and all four choices (period, semicolon, comma + coordinating conjunction, colon) eliminate it in a grammatically acceptable way. A well-constructed SAT Boundaries item includes only one punctuation option that produces a conventionally correct sentence; here no choice can be shown to be uniquely best on grounds of Standard English conventions.
- unclear_or_ambiguous: The prompt asks which choice 'most effectively corrects the boundary error,' implying a stylistic/rhetorical judgment the SAT does not test in Boundaries items, leaving students to guess among several conventionally correct fixes.

### R&W · Boundaries · medium-high · #28 (q01166)

> For decades, economists assumed that consumers behave as rational actors who evaluate choices systematically to maximize personal utility. Behavioral economists challenged this view by documenting predictable patterns of irrational decision-making, the most influential of these researchers, Daniel Kahneman and Amos Tversky, demonstrated through a series of elegant experiments that human judgment is systematically distorted by cognitive biases, heuristics, and framing effects that even well-informed individuals cannot fully override.

**Q:** Which choice most effectively corrects the boundary error in the passage?

- A) Behavioral economists challenged this view by documenting predictable patterns of irrational decision-making; the most influential of these researchers, Daniel Kahneman and Amos Tversky, demonstrated through a series of elegant experiments that human judgment is systematically distorted by cognitive biases, heuristics, and framing effects that even well-informed individuals cannot fully override.
- B) Behavioral economists challenged this view by documenting predictable patterns of irrational decision-making, and the most influential of these researchers, Daniel Kahneman and Amos Tversky, demonstrated through a series of elegant experiments that human judgment is systematically distorted by cognitive biases, heuristics, and framing effects that even well-informed individuals cannot fully override.
- C) Behavioral economists challenged this view by documenting predictable patterns of irrational decision-making: the most influential of these researchers, Daniel Kahneman and Amos Tversky, demonstrated through a series of elegant experiments that human judgment is systematically distorted by cognitive biases, heuristics, and framing effects that even well-informed individuals cannot fully override.
- D) Behavioral economists challenged this view by documenting predictable patterns of irrational decision-making. The most influential of these researchers, Daniel Kahneman and Amos Tversky, demonstrated through a series of elegant experiments that human judgment is systematically distorted by cognitive biases, heuristics, and framing effects that even well-informed individuals cannot fully override.

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** A (high)
- Also defensible: **B** — A comma plus the coordinating conjunction 'and' is a grammatically correct way to join the two independent clauses, eliminating the splice.
- Also defensible: **C** — A colon is acceptable because the second clause elaborates on/specifies the preceding general statement about behavioral economists.
- Also defensible: **D** — Splitting the clauses into two sentences with a period fully corrects the comma splice and is stylistically unobjectionable.
- multiple_correct_answers: All four choices eliminate the comma splice in a grammatically acceptable way (semicolon, comma + coordinating conjunction, colon, and period). SAT Boundaries items must have exactly one punctuation option that is conventionally correct; here no choice can be ruled out on convention, and 'most effectively' gives no objective criterion for preferring the semicolon.
- unclear_or_ambiguous: The prompt asks which choice 'most effectively corrects' the error, implying a stylistic judgment the SAT does not test; a student has no principled basis for choosing among four correct fixes.

### R&W · Boundaries · medium-high · #29 (q01167)

> The 1905 Russian Revolution, though ultimately suppressed by Tsar Nicholas II, fundamentally altered the political landscape of the empire. Nicholas reluctantly issued the October Manifesto, which promised civil liberties and established a legislative assembly called the Duma. Revolutionary leaders greeted the concessions with deep suspicion, they argued that the Tsar had granted just enough reform to forestall genuine transformation while preserving the essential structures of autocratic power.

**Q:** Which choice most effectively corrects the boundary error in the passage?

- A) Revolutionary leaders greeted the concessions with deep suspicion. They argued that the Tsar had granted just enough reform to forestall genuine transformation while preserving the essential structures of autocratic power.
- B) Revolutionary leaders greeted the concessions with deep suspicion: they argued that the Tsar had granted just enough reform to forestall genuine transformation while preserving the essential structures of autocratic power.
- C) Revolutionary leaders greeted the concessions with deep suspicion; they argued that the Tsar had granted just enough reform to forestall genuine transformation while preserving the essential structures of autocratic power.
- D) Revolutionary leaders greeted the concessions with deep suspicion, arguing that the Tsar had granted just enough reform to forestall genuine transformation while preserving the essential structures of autocratic power.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** C (high)
- Also defensible: **A** — Splitting into two sentences with a period fully and correctly eliminates the comma splice; it is grammatically flawless.
- Also defensible: **B** — A colon after an independent clause can properly introduce an elaboration, and the second clause explains the leaders' suspicion, so this is also grammatically acceptable.
- Also defensible: **D** — Converting the second clause into a participial phrase ('arguing that...') removes the splice entirely and is arguably the most concise, idiomatic revision.
- multiple_correct_answers: All four choices successfully repair the comma splice in Standard English (period, colon, semicolon, and participial-phrase reduction are each valid). A genuine SAT Boundaries item includes exactly one punctuation-valid option; here there is no basis in the text for preferring one, so the item cannot be reliably scored.
- unclear_or_ambiguous: The phrase 'most effectively corrects' invites stylistic judgment rather than a conventions-based determination, which is not how SAT Boundaries questions are designed.

### R&W · Boundaries · hard · #13 (q01181)

> The Meiji Restoration of 1868 transformed Japan with breathtaking speed, within three decades, a feudal society governed by hereditary samurai lords had become an industrialized nation-state with a modern military, a constitutional government, and a centralized bureaucracy. Historians have debated whether this transformation should be understood as a genuine revolution or as a conservative modernization — one that adopted Western technologies and institutions precisely in order to preserve certain traditional hierarchies of power rather than dismantle them.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text with the most logical and grammatically correct use of sentence boundaries?

- A) The Meiji Restoration of 1868 transformed Japan with breathtaking speed; within three decades, a feudal society governed by hereditary samurai lords had become an industrialized nation-state with a modern military, a constitutional government, and a centralized bureaucracy.
- B) The Meiji Restoration of 1868 transformed Japan with breathtaking speed, within three decades a feudal society governed by hereditary samurai lords had become an industrialized nation-state with a modern military, a constitutional government, and a centralized bureaucracy.
- C) The Meiji Restoration of 1868 transformed Japan with breathtaking speed: within three decades, a feudal society governed by hereditary samurai lords had become an industrialized nation-state with a modern military, a constitutional government, and a centralized bureaucracy.
- D) The Meiji Restoration of 1868 transformed Japan with breathtaking speed — within three decades a feudal society governed by hereditary samurai lords had become an industrialized nation-state, with a modern military, a constitutional government, and a centralized bureaucracy.

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** A (medium)
- Also defensible: **C** — A colon after an independent clause introducing an elaboration ('within three decades...') is also fully grammatical and idiomatic here, making C equally correct in Standard English.
- Also defensible: **D** — An em dash can likewise join an independent clause to an elaborating independent clause; the only quibble is the arguably unnecessary comma before 'with a modern military,' which is nonetheless defensible as a mild appositive-style pause.
- multiple_correct_answers: Choices A (semicolon), C (colon), and arguably D (em dash) all correctly resolve the comma splice and are grammatically and logically acceptable. Real SAT Boundaries items ensure only one punctuation mark works; here the second clause both stands alone and elaborates on the first, so semicolon, colon, and dash are all valid.
- formatting: The passage is presented with the target sentence already punctuated (as a comma splice) rather than with a blank or underlined portion, so the task of 'completing the text' is unclear; the answer choices restate the entire sentence.

### R&W · Boundaries · hard · #17 (q01185)

> The concept of the sublime, as theorized by Edmund Burke and later by Immanuel Kant, refers to an aesthetic experience provoked by vastness or power so overwhelming that it exceeds the mind's capacity to comprehend or contain it, mountains, storms, and infinite expanses of ocean were the canonical examples. Unlike the beautiful, which produces pleasure through harmony and proportion, the sublime is characterized by a mixture of terror and exaltation — the recognition of one's own smallness in the face of forces that dwarf human scale.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text with the most logical and grammatically correct use of sentence boundaries?

- A) . . . that it exceeds the mind's capacity to comprehend or contain it; mountains, storms, and infinite expanses of ocean were the canonical examples.
- B) . . . that it exceeds the mind's capacity to comprehend or contain it, mountains, storms, and infinite expanses of ocean were the canonical examples.
- C) . . . that it exceeds the mind's capacity to comprehend or contain it: mountains, storms, and infinite expanses of ocean were the canonical examples.
- D) . . . that it exceeds the mind's capacity to comprehend or contain it — mountains, storms, and infinite expanses of ocean were the canonical examples.

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** A (medium)
- Also defensible: **C** — A colon after a complete independent clause is acceptable when what follows explains or illustrates it; here the second clause supplies the canonical examples, so the colon is grammatically and logically defensible.
- Also defensible: **D** — An em dash can also correctly join two independent clauses, with the second amplifying the first; nothing in the sentence rules it out.
- multiple_correct_answers: Three of the four options (semicolon, colon, em dash) correctly join two independent clauses, and all three are logical here since the second clause illustrates the first. Only B (comma splice) is clearly wrong, so the item lacks a single best answer. A properly constructed SAT boundaries item would include only one viable punctuation mark.

### R&W · Boundaries · hard · #20 (q01188)

> In her landmark study of infant attachment, psychologist Mary Ainsworth identified what she called the "secure base phenomenon," the tendency of securely attached infants to use their caregiver as a fixed point from which to explore an unfamiliar environment. When the caregiver was present and emotionally available, infants ventured confidently into new territory. When the caregiver departed, even briefly, exploration ceased and distress behaviors emerged — behaviors that Ainsworth interpreted not as signs of weakness but as biologically adaptive responses calibrated to restore proximity to a protective adult.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text with the most logical and grammatically correct use of sentence boundaries?

- A) In her landmark study of infant attachment, psychologist Mary Ainsworth identified what she called the "secure base phenomenon," the tendency of securely attached infants to use their caregiver as a fixed point from which to explore an unfamiliar environment.
- B) In her landmark study of infant attachment, psychologist Mary Ainsworth identified what she called the "secure base phenomenon": the tendency of securely attached infants to use their caregiver as a fixed point from which to explore an unfamiliar environment.
- C) In her landmark study of infant attachment, psychologist Mary Ainsworth identified what she called the "secure base phenomenon" — the tendency of securely attached infants to use their caregiver as a fixed point from which to explore an unfamiliar environment.
- D) In her landmark study of infant attachment, psychologist Mary Ainsworth identified what she called the "secure base phenomenon"; the tendency of securely attached infants to use their caregiver as a fixed point from which to explore an unfamiliar environment.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** A (medium)
- Also defensible: **B** — A colon after an independent clause correctly introduces the appositive explanation of the quoted term; this is fully grammatical and idiomatic.
- Also defensible: **C** — A single em dash after an independent clause also correctly sets off the appositive definition; grammatically acceptable.
- multiple_correct_answers: Choices A (comma appositive), B (colon), and C (em dash) are all grammatically correct ways to attach the noun-phrase appositive to a complete independent clause. Only D (semicolon joining a clause to a fragment) is wrong, so the item has three defensible answers.
- formatting: The passage already contains the full sentence identical to choice A, with no blank or underlined portion indicating what is to be completed. The stem asks to 'complete the text,' but nothing is omitted.

### R&W · Form, Structure, and Sense · hard · #5 (q01293)

> The commission's report on urban housing, which was completed after eighteen months of fieldwork and reviewed by an independent panel of experts before _______ released, identified several systemic failures in zoning enforcement that had allowed unsafe conditions to persist for years.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text so that it conforms to the conventions of Standard Written English?

- A) it was
- B) being
- C) it being
- D) having been

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** A (high)
- Also defensible: **B** — "before being released" is a fully idiomatic prepositional phrase with a gerund whose implied subject (the report) matches the subject of the relative clause: "...reviewed by an independent panel of experts before being released..." This is standard and conventional English, so it is also correct.
- multiple_correct_answers: Both A ("before it was released") and B ("before being released") produce grammatically standard, idiomatic sentences with the same meaning. The item needs to be revised (e.g., by changing the structure so a gerund phrase would dangle) to leave only one defensible answer.

### Math · Algebra · medium-low · #36 (q01394)

**Q:** If h(x) = x^2 + 2x - 3, for which value of x is h(x) = 0?

- A) x = -1 only
- B) x = 3 only
- C) x = 1 or x = -3
- D) x = -3 or x = 1

**Key:** D · **Reviewer:** C (high)
- Also defensible: **D** — Choice D lists exactly the same solution set as choice C (x = 1 and x = -3), merely in a different order, so it is equally correct.
- multiple_correct_answers: Choices C ('x = 1 or x = -3') and D ('x = -3 or x = 1') are logically identical solution sets; both are correct, so the item has no unique best answer. One of them should be replaced with a genuine distractor (e.g., 'x = -1 or x = 3').

### Math · Data Analysis · medium-low · #3 (q01521)

**Q:** Using the Pet Preference by Gender table, how many more males dislike cats than females who dislike cats?

**Key:** 15 · **Reviewer:** N/A (high)
- formatting: The question refers to a 'Pet Preference by Gender' table, but no table (or any data) is included in the item. Without the data, no numeric answer can be computed.
- no_correct_answer: Because the referenced data set is absent, the item has no determinable answer.

### Math · Geometry · hard · #9 (q01767)

**Q:** Using the same cone (radius 5, slant height 13), what is the total surface area, in terms of π?

**Key:** 90π · **Reviewer:** 90 (high)
- formatting: The question asks for an answer "in terms of π," but SAT student-produced response boxes accept only numeric values (integers, decimals, or fractions). A student cannot enter 90π; the intended entry would have to be 90, which conflicts with the instruction.
- unclear_or_ambiguous: The stem begins "Using the same cone," referring to a prior question that is not provided. Although the needed dimensions (radius 5, slant height 13) are restated, the dangling reference makes the item non-self-contained.
- difficulty_mismatch: Direct application of the surface-area formula S = πr² + πrl with given radius and slant height (no need to find the height) is an easy/medium task, not 'hard.'

## ambiguous (149)

### R&W · Central Ideas and Details · hard · #7 (q00097)

> Standard cosmological models predict that dwarf galaxies, because of their relatively low stellar masses, should exhibit steep, highly concentrated spikes of dark matter at their galactic centers—a phenomenon known as the "cusp" profile. However, high-resolution spectroscopic observations of nearby dwarf spheroidal galaxies consistently reveal a drastically different reality: the dark matter distribution is flat and uniformly distributed across a broad central core. This persistent mismatch, termed the "cusp-core problem," has ignited intense theoretical debates. Some astrophysicists argue that the predictive models are correct but fail to account for "baryonic feedback"—the energetic processes of supernova explosions that can physically push dark matter outward from the center. Others contend that the anomaly exposes a fundamental flaw in the cold dark matter model itself, suggesting that dark matter particles may interact via self-repulsive forces that naturally smooth out galactic centers.

**Q:** Which choice best states the main idea of the text?

- A) High-resolution observations have disproven the existence of dark matter in dwarf spheroidal galaxies, forcing a complete abandonment of cosmological models.
- B) The cusp-core problem describes a contradiction between predicted and observed dark matter concentrations in dwarf galaxies, inspiring competing astrophysical explanations.
- C) Supernova explosions are the universally accepted explanation for the flat dark matter cores observed at the center of dwarf galaxies.
- D) Cold dark matter models are completely flawless when applied to large spiral galaxies, but become inaccurate due to the absence of baryonic matter in dwarf systems.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- difficulty_mismatch: Labeled 'hard,' but the three distractors are eliminated by obvious absolute/extreme language ('disproven,' 'universally accepted,' 'completely flawless'), making the item much easier than a hard-tier Central Ideas question.
- factual_or_math_error: Minor: the passage attributes the predicted cusp profile to dwarf galaxies' 'relatively low stellar masses,' but in standard CDM theory cusps are a generic prediction of structure-formation simulations, not a consequence of low stellar mass. This does not affect the answer.

### R&W · Command of Evidence (Textual) · medium-low · #11 (q00161)

> Marine biologists have long studied the feeding habits of the blue whale, the largest animal on Earth. Despite its enormous size, the blue whale survives almost entirely on krill, tiny shrimp-like crustaceans that measure only a few centimeters in length. During feeding season, a single blue whale may consume up to four tons of krill per day. The whale uses a process called filter feeding, in which it takes in massive gulps of water and then forces the water out through baleen plates, trapping the krill inside its mouth. This highly efficient system allows the whale to meet its enormous caloric needs.

**Q:** Which choice best describes the main evidence the author provides to support the claim that blue whales have an efficient feeding system?

- A) The author notes that blue whales are the largest animals on Earth, implying that a large body requires a powerful feeding method.
- B) The author explains that blue whales consume up to four tons of krill per day, which demonstrates the system's productivity.
- C) The author describes how baleen plates trap krill after water is expelled, showing how the process captures large quantities of food.
- D) The author points out that krill are only a few centimeters long, suggesting that consuming them in bulk is the only viable strategy.

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (medium)
- Also defensible: **B** — The four-tons-per-day figure is quantitative evidence of how much food the system delivers, which a reader could reasonably call the main support for the claim of efficiency.
- unclear_or_ambiguous: Both B and C are supportable. The claim sentence ('This highly efficient system allows the whale to meet its enormous caloric needs') directly follows the description of baleen filter feeding, favoring C, but the stated intake of four tons of krill per day is explicit quantitative evidence of the system's effectiveness, favoring B. The item does not clearly distinguish mechanism-based evidence from output-based evidence.

### R&W · Command of Evidence (Textual) · medium-low · #12 (q00162)

> The practice of crop rotation, in which farmers alternate the types of crops grown in a field each season, has been used for centuries to maintain soil health. When the same crop is grown repeatedly in one location, it depletes specific nutrients from the soil, eventually reducing yields. By rotating crops — for example, alternating nitrogen-fixing legumes with nitrogen-consuming grains — farmers can restore nutrient levels naturally. Studies have shown that fields using crop rotation produce higher yields over time compared with fields planted with a single crop year after year. This method also reduces the buildup of pests and diseases that tend to target specific plant species.

**Q:** Which choice identifies the evidence the author uses to support the claim that crop rotation benefits soil health?

- A) The author explains that farmers have used crop rotation for centuries, suggesting the practice has proven reliable over time.
- B) The author notes that nitrogen-fixing legumes can restore nutrients depleted by nitrogen-consuming grains.
- C) The author states that crop rotation reduces pest buildup, which indirectly improves the conditions in which crops grow.
- D) The author cites studies showing that rotated fields produce higher yields than single-crop fields over time.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** D (low)
- Also defensible: **B** — The legume/grain example is the passage's most direct support for the specific claim about soil health (nutrient restoration), whereas yields are only an indirect indicator of soil quality; a reviewer could reasonably call this the author's supporting evidence.
- multiple_correct_answers: Both D (cited studies on yields — the explicit 'Studies have shown' evidence) and B (the nitrogen-fixing legume example, which bears most directly on soil nutrient health) are defensible answers. The question would need to specify whether it wants empirical data or the mechanism tied to soil health to have a single best answer.
- unclear_or_ambiguous: The stem asks for 'the evidence' (singular) in a passage that offers several distinct supports (historical use, mechanism example, studies, pest reduction), without a criterion for choosing among them.

### R&W · Command of Evidence (Textual) · medium-low · #22 (q00172)

> Coral reefs, sometimes called the rainforests of the sea, support an extraordinary diversity of marine life despite covering less than one percent of the ocean floor. Reefs provide habitat, feeding grounds, and nurseries for roughly twenty-five percent of all marine species. However, rising ocean temperatures have triggered mass coral bleaching events, in which corals expel the symbiotic algae that provide them with nutrients and color, leaving them white and vulnerable. Without the algae, corals can survive for a time but will eventually die if temperatures do not return to normal. Scientists have recorded increasingly frequent and severe bleaching events over the past three decades, threatening the survival of reef ecosystems worldwide.

**Q:** Which choice best states the textual evidence the author provides to show that coral bleaching endangers reef ecosystems?

- A) The author notes that coral reefs cover less than one percent of the ocean floor, making them especially fragile compared to other marine habitats.
- B) The author explains that bleached corals expel their symbiotic algae and will eventually die if water temperatures remain elevated.
- C) The author states that reefs support roughly twenty-five percent of all marine species, so their decline would have widespread consequences.
- D) The author points out that bleaching events have become more frequent and severe over the past three decades.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (medium)
- Also defensible: **D** — The passage's final sentence explicitly ties increasingly frequent and severe bleaching events to 'threatening the survival of reef ecosystems worldwide,' so D also directly quotes evidence the author supplies for the danger.
- unclear_or_ambiguous: Both B (bleached corals eventually die) and D (bleaching events increasingly frequent and severe, explicitly described in the text as 'threatening the survival of reef ecosystems') serve as evidence that bleaching endangers reefs. The prompt does not distinguish between the causal mechanism of harm and the trend/scale of harm, so two choices are defensible.

### R&W · Command of Evidence (Textual) · medium-low · #30 (q00180)

> The Amazon rainforest plays a critical role in regulating the global climate by absorbing vast quantities of carbon dioxide from the atmosphere. Trees store carbon in their trunks, roots, and leaves, effectively removing it from the air and slowing the accumulation of greenhouse gases. When forests are cleared through logging or burning, this stored carbon is released back into the atmosphere, contributing to climate change. Researchers have warned that deforestation in the Amazon has already reached a point at which some areas are emitting more carbon than they absorb, shifting portions of the forest from a carbon sink to a carbon source. This transformation has alarmed scientists who study climate systems.

**Q:** Which choice best describes the textual evidence the author uses to support the claim that deforestation in the Amazon contributes to climate change?

- A) The author states that trees store carbon in their trunks, roots, and leaves, showing that forests contain significant reserves of carbon.
- B) The author explains that clearing forests releases stored carbon back into the atmosphere, adding to greenhouse gas accumulation.
- C) The author notes that researchers warn some Amazon areas now emit more carbon than they absorb, reversing the forest's climate function.
- D) The author describes the Amazon as critical to global climate regulation because of the large volume of carbon dioxide it absorbs.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (medium)
- Also defensible: **C** — The researchers' finding that some Amazon areas now emit more carbon than they absorb is Amazon-specific empirical support for the claim; since B arguably just restates the claim's causal assertion rather than offering evidence for it, C can be read as the stronger 'evidence.'
- unclear_or_ambiguous: The sentence paraphrased in B ('clearing forests releases stored carbon... contributing to climate change') is essentially the claim itself, so labeling it 'evidence' blurs the claim/evidence distinction and makes C (the researchers' finding) a competitive answer.

### R&W · Command of Evidence (Textual) · medium-high · #4 (q00184)

> A literary historian hypothesized that the nineteenth-century novelist Claire Dupin authored an anonymous, controversial political pamphlet mocking a local politician in 1842, despite her public denials at the time. To evaluate authorship, stylistic analysts conducted a comprehensive computational stylometric profile, comparing the syntax, sentence length distributions, and rare vocabulary frequencies of the anonymous pamphlet with Dupin's known novels and private journals from that exact era.

**Q:** Which choice provides the most direct textual evidence to support the stylometric analysts' hypothesis?

- A) The anonymous pamphlet contains an idiosyncratic pattern of semicolons and a specific sequence of archaic adjectives that are perfectly mirrored in Dupin's private letters and novels written during the autumn of 1842.
- B) In an 1843 journal entry, Dupin wrote, "The anonymous pamphlet attacking the politician is brilliant, and whoever wrote it possesses an artistic mind far superior to my own."
- C) Historical publishing records from 1842 show that the anonymous pamphlet was printed using the exact same type of printing press that Dupin's publisher used for her novels.
- D) The local politician targeted by the pamphlet had previously published a harsh, highly public review criticizing the plot structure of Dupin's first major novel.

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (high)
- unclear_or_ambiguous: The question attributes the hypothesis to 'the stylometric analysts,' but the passage states the hypothesis was the literary historian's; the analysts merely conducted the comparison. This mismatch is a minor imprecision that does not change the answer.
- difficulty_mismatch: The correct choice is the only one mentioning stylistic/linguistic features, making it transparently correct for a 'medium-high' item; distractors are weak (B is contrary evidence, C and D are circumstantial and obviously non-stylometric).

### R&W · Command of Evidence (Textual) · hard · #2 (q00212)

> In modern cosmology, the prevailing Lambda-CDM model characterizes dark energy as a "cosmological constant"—a static, unaltering vacuum energy density inherent to space that accelerates cosmic expansion at a perfectly uniform rate across time. However, proponents of alternative "quintessence" frameworks argue that dark energy is not a static constant but rather a dynamic, evolving scalar field whose energy density and accelerating influence fluctuate over cosmic epochs. To adjudicate between these paradigms, astrophysicists analyzed precision high-redshift data from Type Ia supernovae to map the rate of cosmic acceleration across the universe's 13.8-billion-year history.

**Q:** Which choice, if true, would most directly support the dynamic "quintessence" framework over the traditional cosmological constant model?

- A) The supernova data revealed that the rate of cosmic acceleration was significantly slower 8 billion years ago than it is today, demonstrating a clear variation in dark energy's influence across different historical epochs.
- B) The data confirmed that the energy density of dark energy remains precisely fixed at 10 to the power of minus 29 grams per cubic centimeter across all measured spatial coordinates and time intervals.
- C) Cosmological observations indicated that the rate of cosmic expansion is perfectly balanced by the gravitational attraction exerted by baryonic matter and static dark matter structures.
- D) Computer models showed that if dark energy were completely dynamic, the structural formation of early galaxies would have been delayed by several billion years.

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (high)
- factual_or_math_error: The passage mischaracterizes the cosmological constant model as producing 'a perfectly uniform rate' of acceleration across time; in actual Lambda-CDM, the acceleration rate changes over time (matter density dilutes) even though vacuum energy density is constant. This scientific inaccuracy is needed to make choice A work, though it does not create answer ambiguity within the passage's own framing.

### R&W · Command of Evidence (Textual) · hard · #16 (q00226)

> Proponents of universal basic income (UBI) often argue that by decoupling subsistence from employment, UBI would empower workers to refuse exploitative labor conditions and thereby force employers to improve wages and working environments. Critics counter that UBI, if funded through value-added taxation, would effectively transfer wealth from wage earners to capital owners, since the tax burden would fall disproportionately on consumption rather than on accumulated assets. A further complication arises from the labor supply question: if UBI reduces labor force participation, it may shrink the tax base upon which the program depends, creating a fiscal feedback loop that renders the program unstable over time without continuous adjustment of benefit levels or tax rates.

**Q:** Which choice identifies a tension within the passage's presentation of UBI that neither proponents nor critics, as described, fully address?

- A) The passage mentions that UBI could improve working conditions but does not specify which industries would benefit most from workers' increased bargaining power
- B) The passage presents the labor supply concern as a structural instability but does not resolve whether this instability would necessarily undermine the worker empowerment benefits that proponents cite
- C) The passage criticizes VAT funding but does not propose an alternative funding mechanism that would avoid the regressive redistribution problem
- D) The passage acknowledges that UBI might reduce labor participation but does not consider whether reduced participation could itself be a desirable social outcome

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- Also defensible: **D** — Also points to something the passage leaves unexamined (whether reduced labor participation might be desirable), but it identifies an omission rather than a tension between the two positions presented, so B remains clearly better.
- other: The task ('identifies a tension ... that neither proponents nor critics fully address') is an analysis/inference prompt rather than a Command of Evidence (Textual) item, which on the Digital SAT asks which quotation or detail would most effectively support/illustrate/weaken a stated claim. Skill label and item format are mismatched, though the question itself has one clearly best answer.

### R&W · Command of Evidence (Quantitative) · easy · #23 (q00263)

> A botanist measures the average height of sunflowers grown under four different light conditions in a greenhouse experiment. She claims that sunflowers grown under full sunlight grow taller on average than those grown under any other light condition tested.
> Table: Average Sunflower Height by Light Condition
> | Light Condition    | Avg. Height (cm) |
> |--------------------|-----------------|
> | Full Sunlight      | 182             |
> | Partial Sunlight   | 155             |
> | Artificial Light   | 140             |
> | Low Light          | 98              |

**Q:** Which choice best uses data from the table to support the botanist's claim?

- A) Sunflowers in low light grew an average of only 98 cm, the shortest of any condition.
- B) Sunflowers grown in full sunlight averaged 182 cm, taller than those in partial sunlight at 155 cm, artificial light at 140 cm, and low light at 98 cm.
- C) Partial sunlight produced taller sunflowers than both artificial light and low light conditions.
- D) There is a 42-cm difference between partial sunlight and low light conditions.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- factual_or_math_error: Choice D misstates the data: the difference between partial sunlight (155 cm) and low light (98 cm) is 57 cm, not 42 cm. Although D is a distractor either way, SAT distractors are normally arithmetically accurate statements that simply fail to support the claim.

### R&W · Command of Evidence (Quantitative) · medium-low · #19 (q00289)

> A behavioral economist studied the average amount (in dollars) that participants in four income brackets donated to charity annually. The economist claimed that individuals in the $40,000–$59,999 income bracket donated a greater share of their income to charity than those in any other bracket, suggesting that middle-income earners are disproportionately generous relative to their earnings.
> Table: Average Annual Charitable Donations by Income Bracket
> | Income Bracket | Avg. Annual Donation ($) | Avg. Income in Bracket ($) | Donation as % of Income |
> |---|---|---|---|
> | Under $40,000 | 320 | 28,000 | 1.14% |
> | $40,000–$59,999 | 980 | 49,000 | 2.00% |
> | $60,000–$99,999 | 1,450 | 76,000 | 1.91% |
> | $100,000 and over | 4,200 | 145,000 | 2.90% |

**Q:** Which choice best uses data from the table to support the economist's claim?

- A) The $100,000 and over bracket had the highest average annual donation at $4,200, more than four times that of the $40,000–$59,999 bracket.
- B) The $40,000–$59,999 bracket donated an average of $980 annually, more than the under $40,000 bracket's $320.
- C) Individuals in the $40,000–$59,999 bracket donated 2.00% of their income to charity, a higher share than the under $40,000 bracket (1.14%) and the $60,000–$99,999 bracket (1.91%).
- D) Average donation amounts increased with income level, rising from $320 in the lowest bracket to $4,200 in the highest bracket.

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (high)
- no_correct_answer: The economist's claim is actually contradicted by the table: the $100,000-and-over bracket donates 2.90% of income, a greater share than the $40,000–$59,999 bracket's 2.00%. Therefore no option can fully support the claim; C only shows the bracket exceeds two of the three other brackets, deliberately omitting the highest-income bracket. On a real SAT item, the intended answer would fully support the stated claim, so the stimulus claim should be revised (e.g., limited to brackets under $100,000) or the data adjusted. C is nonetheless clearly the best of the four choices since it is the only one addressing share of income.

### R&W · Command of Evidence (Quantitative) · medium-high · #6 (q00306)

> A public health researcher tracked the percentage of fully immunized children across four distinct urban districts (Districts 1, 2, 3, and 4) following three consecutive annual funding increases for mobile vaccination clinics. Anti-program critics asserted that expanding mobile clinic funding fails to change real-world outcomes because urban families face insurmountable cultural and structural barriers to access. The researcher argues that the funding has had a powerful cumulative impact, but only in districts where clinics operate on weekends. Note: Mobile clinics operated on weekends exclusively in Districts 1 and 3.
> Table: Child Immunization Rates (%) Over Three Years
> | District Name | Baseline Year | Year 1 (Post-Funding) | Year 2 (Post-Funding) | Year 3 (Post-Funding) |
> |---------------|---------------|------------------------|------------------------|------------------------|
> | District 1    | 45%           | 58%                    | 72%                    | 88%                    |
> | District 2    | 52%           | 54%                    | 53%                    | 55%                    |
> | District 3    | 38%           | 52%                    | 68%                    | 85%                    |
> | District 4    | 60%           | 61%                    | 60%                    | 62%                    |

**Q:** Which choice best uses data from the table to support the researcher's argument?
