- A) District 4 maintained the highest initial baseline immunization rate at 60% and finished the three-year study at 62%.
- B) Districts 1 and 3 experienced dramatic, multi-year immunization increases, climbing from 45% to 88% and 38% to 85% respectively, while weekday-only Districts 2 and 4 remained completely flat.
- C) District 2 started with a 52% immunization rate and showed minor fluctuations, dipping to 53% before finishing at 55%.
- D) All four urban districts achieved immunization rates exceeding 50% by the end of the third post-funding evaluation cycle.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- factual_or_math_error: Choice B describes Districts 2 and 4 as having 'remained completely flat,' but the table shows modest increases (52%→55% and 60%→62%). The overstatement makes the correct answer slightly inaccurate relative to the data, though it is still clearly the only choice that supports the researcher's claim.

### R&W · Command of Evidence (Quantitative) · medium-high · #8 (q00308)

> An archeologist analyzed the relative frequencies of imported ceramic styles found across three distinct structural sectors (Palace Complex, Marketplace Plaza, and Artisan Quarter) of an ancient Mesopotamian city to evaluate its economic hierarchy. The researcher claimed that while elite administrative sectors imported luxury pottery uniformly from foreign kingdoms, the commercial marketplace was highly dynamic, showing a sudden shift from local earthenware to imported maritime goods over a two-decade timeline.
> Table: Luxury Ceramic Counts (% of Total Sector Artifacts)
> | Urban Sector     | Year 1 Excavation Strata | Year 10 Excavation Strata | Year 20 Excavation Strata |
> |------------------|--------------------------|---------------------------|---------------------------|
> | Palace Complex   | 65%                      | 68%                       | 67%                       |
> | Marketplace Plaza| 12%                      | 45%                       | 82%                       |
> | Artisan Quarter  | 8%                       | 10%                       | 11%                       |

**Q:** Which choice best uses data from the table to support the archeologist's claim?

- A) The Palace Complex consistently maintained high luxury ceramic frequencies, tracking between 65% and 68% across the entire 20-year strata profile.
- B) The commercial Marketplace Plaza experienced a massive surge in luxury ceramics, climbing from 12% in Year 1 to 45% in Year 10, and peaking at 82% in Year 20.
- C) Luxury ceramics in the Artisan Quarter remained extremely low and rigid, failing to cross above 11% across all layers.
- D) Year 20 strata excavations revealed that luxury pottery made up the absolute majority of items recovered from both the Palace and Marketplace sectors.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (medium)
- Also defensible: **A** — The archeologist's claim has two parts, and A accurately supports the first part (elite administrative sector importing luxury pottery uniformly: 65–68% with little variation). A reviewer could argue it legitimately supports the claim, though it does not address the claim's more distinctive assertion about the dynamic marketplace.
- unclear_or_ambiguous: Because the claim is compound (palace uniformity AND marketplace dynamism), two choices (A and B) each accurately support one half of it; the item would be cleaner if the claim were single-pronged or if one option combined both trends. B is still the best answer since the 12%→45%→82% surge is the claim's most distinctive, testable element, but the two-part claim invites defensible disagreement.

### R&W · Command of Evidence (Quantitative) · medium-high · #13 (q00313)

> An urban sociologist studying public transit usage argued that cities with higher population density tend to have greater rates of public transit ridership as a percentage of total commuters. The sociologist suggested that this relationship holds across a range of city sizes, and that density—rather than city population alone—is the more reliable predictor of transit adoption among commuters.
> Table: Population Density and Transit Ridership in Selected Cities
> | City | Population (millions) | Density (people/km²) | Transit Ridership (% of commuters) |
> |---|---|---|---|
> | City F | 0.8 | 1,200 | 18% |
> | City G | 2.1 | 1,850 | 27% |
> | City H | 1.4 | 2,600 | 39% |
> | City I | 3.3 | 3,400 | 48% |
> | City J | 1.1 | 4,100 | 61% |

**Q:** Which choice best uses data from the table to support the sociologist's claim?

- A) City I, with a population of 3.3 million, had a higher transit ridership rate (48%) than City F, which had a population of only 0.8 million and a ridership rate of 18%.
- B) City J, despite having a smaller population than Cities G and I, had the highest transit ridership rate at 61%, corresponding to its highest density of 4,100 people/km².
- C) Cities with densities above 2,600 people/km² — City H, City I, and City J — all recorded transit ridership rates of 39% or higher, while cities below that density recorded rates of 27% or lower.
- D) Transit ridership increased from 18% in City F to 61% in City J, a rise of 43 percentage points, as both population and density varied across the five cities.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- Also defensible: **C** — It accurately shows the positive density–ridership association, but it ignores population entirely, so it does not support the key part of the claim that density rather than population is the better predictor; B is clearly better.

### R&W · Command of Evidence (Quantitative) · medium-high · #19 (q00319)

> A soil scientist examining agricultural land use argued that fields rotated between legume and cereal crops show progressively increasing soil nitrogen levels over successive growing seasons, whereas fields planted continuously with cereal crops show a gradual decline in soil nitrogen over the same period. The scientist contended that after four seasons, the nitrogen difference between rotated and non-rotated fields becomes agronomically significant.
> Table: Avg. Soil Nitrogen Content (kg/hectare) by Season and Cropping Strategy
> | Growing Season | Rotated Fields | Continuously Cereal Fields |
> |---|---|---|
> | Baseline (Season 0) | 142 | 141 |
> | Season 1 | 149 | 137 |
> | Season 2 | 158 | 131 |
> | Season 3 | 166 | 126 |
> | Season 4 | 175 | 119 |

**Q:** Which choice best uses data from the table to support the scientist's claim?

- A) By Season 4, rotated fields had an average soil nitrogen content of 175 kg/hectare compared to 119 kg/hectare for continuously cereal fields — a difference of 56 kg/hectare, up from a near-identical baseline of 142 and 141 kg/hectare.
- B) Rotated fields showed an increase of 33 kg/hectare in soil nitrogen from Season 0 to Season 4, while continuously cereal fields showed a decrease of 22 kg/hectare over the same period.
- C) Continuously cereal fields lost soil nitrogen in every recorded season, declining from 141 kg/hectare at baseline to 119 kg/hectare by Season 4, a total loss of 22 kg/hectare.
- D) Between Season 2 and Season 4, rotated fields gained 17 kg/hectare of soil nitrogen while continuously cereal fields lost 12 kg/hectare, demonstrating that the divergence continued in the later seasons.

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (high)
- Also defensible: **B** — B accurately reports both the increase in rotated fields and the decline in continuous cereal fields, supporting the trend portion of the claim, though it does not address the magnitude of the gap after four seasons as directly as A.

### R&W · Command of Evidence (Quantitative) · medium-high · #26 (q00326)

> A conservation biologist studying predator reintroduction effects argued that following the reintroduction of wolves to a protected grassland ecosystem, elk populations declined to sustainable levels while native vegetation in riparian zones — areas near rivers and streams — recovered measurably. The biologist claimed that by five years post-reintroduction, riparian vegetation coverage had increased substantially compared to the year of reintroduction, consistent with trophic cascade effects driven by altered elk grazing behavior.
> Table: Ecosystem Metrics Before and After Wolf Reintroduction
> | Year (Relative to Reintroduction) | Wolf Population | Elk Population | Riparian Vegetation Coverage (%) |
> |---|---|---|---|
> | Year 0 (reintroduction) | 14 | 2,840 | 31 |
> | Year 1 | 18 | 2,690 | 33 |
> | Year 2 | 23 | 2,510 | 37 |
> | Year 3 | 27 | 2,280 | 42 |
> | Year 4 | 31 | 2,090 | 48 |
> | Year 5 | 34 | 1,920 | 54 |

**Q:** Which choice best uses data from the table to support the biologist's claim?

- A) The wolf population grew from 14 at reintroduction to 34 by Year 5, an increase of more than 140%, suggesting the ecosystem could sustain a substantially larger predator population over time.
- B) By Year 5, elk population had declined from 2,840 to 1,920 — a reduction of 920 animals — while riparian vegetation coverage had increased from 31% to 54%, a gain of 23 percentage points over five years.
- C) Riparian vegetation coverage rose in each year following reintroduction, from 31% at Year 0 to 54% at Year 5, while elk population declined in each corresponding year, consistent with the trophic cascade the biologist described.
- D) From Year 3 to Year 5, riparian vegetation coverage increased from 42% to 54%, a gain of 12 percentage points in just two years, while elk population fell from 2,280 to 1,920 during the same period.

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (low)
- Also defensible: **B** — B also accurately compares Year 0 to Year 5 for both elk population (2,840 to 1,920) and riparian vegetation (31% to 54%), which is precisely the comparison the biologist's claim specifies; it is fully supportive and factually correct, making it equally defensible.
- multiple_correct_answers: Choices B and C both accurately use Year 0 vs. Year 5 data to support the claim that vegetation increased substantially while elk declined. C merely adds that the changes were year-by-year monotonic; B gives the same endpoint comparison with exact magnitudes. Neither contains an error or irrelevance, so there is no clear single best answer.

### R&W · Command of Evidence (Quantitative) · medium-high · #29 (q00329)

> A sociologist studying income and charitable giving argued that the relationship between household income and charitable donation amounts is not linear; rather, the lowest and highest income groups donate a larger share of their income to charity than middle-income groups do. This pattern, sometimes called the "U-shaped giving curve," the sociologist argued, reflects different motivational and social mechanisms operating at the economic extremes compared to the middle of the income distribution.
> Table: Charitable Giving as a Share of Household Income by Income Bracket
> | Annual Household Income Bracket | Avg. Annual Donation ($) | Avg. % of Income Donated |
> |---|---|---|
> | Under $25,000 | 610 | 4.3% |
> | $25,000–$49,999 | 940 | 2.7% |
> | $50,000–$74,999 | 1,180 | 1.9% |
> | $75,000–$99,999 | 1,560 | 1.7% |
> | $100,000–$149,999 | 2,340 | 1.8% |
> | $150,000–$249,999 | 4,120 | 2.1% |
> | $250,000 and above | 9,740 | 4.8% |

**Q:** Which choice best uses data from the table to support the sociologist's claim?

- A) Households earning under $25,000 donated an average of $610 annually — far less in absolute terms than households earning $250,000 or more, which donated $9,740, yet the lower-income group donated a larger share of income (4.3%) than any middle-income bracket.
- B) The lowest-income bracket (under $25,000) donated 4.3% of income and the highest bracket ($250,000 and above) donated 4.8%, while the middle-income brackets — ranging from $25,000 to $149,999 — each donated between 1.7% and 2.7%, forming a U-shaped pattern in donation rates across the income distribution.
- C) The percentage of income donated declined from 4.3% for the lowest bracket to a low of 1.7% for households earning $75,000–$99,999, after which it rose again, reaching 4.8% for the highest bracket.
- D) Middle-income households earning between $50,000 and $149,999 donated a smaller share of income (between 1.7% and 1.9%) than either the lowest-income households (4.3%) or the wealthiest households (4.8%), consistent with the U-shaped pattern the sociologist described.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (medium)
- Also defensible: **C** — C accurately traces the decline from 4.3% to a minimum of 1.7% and the subsequent rise to 4.8%, which is itself a complete and accurate description of the U-shaped giving curve the sociologist posits.
- Also defensible: **D** — D accurately contrasts middle brackets ($50,000–$149,999 at 1.7%–1.9%) with both extremes (4.3% and 4.8%), directly supporting the claim; its only shortcoming is omitting the $25,000–$49,999 bracket.
- multiple_correct_answers: Choices B, C, and D are all factually accurate readings of the table and all directly support the sociologist's U-shaped-giving claim. Well-constructed SAT quantitative-evidence items make distractors either inaccurate or irrelevant to the claim; here the only differentiator is comprehensiveness (B covers all brackets), which makes the 'best' judgment subjective.

### R&W · Command of Evidence (Quantitative) · hard · #1 (q00331)

> The following text is adapted from a research report on avian foraging ecology.
> Biologists investigating the competitive exclusion principle frequently study the hunting strategies of predatory birds in overlapping territories. A long-standing hypothesis posits that when two closely related raptor species—the Northern Harrier and the Red-tailed Hawk—share a habitat, they divide resources via temporal segregation, meaning one species hunts primarily during early dawn hours while the other concentrates its activity during peak afternoon heat, thereby minimizing direct physical encounters. Ecologist Elena Vance designed a study to evaluate this hypothesis by recording active hunts across multiple field sites.
> Table: Average Number of Active Hunts Observed per Hour
> | Raptor Species    | Early Dawn (5 AM - 7 AM) | Midday (11 AM - 1 PM) | Late Afternoon (4 PM - 6 PM) |
> |-------------------|---------------------------|------------------------|------------------------------|
> | Northern Harrier  | 14.2                      | 2.1                    | 15.8                         |
> | Red-tailed Hawk   | 3.5                       | 18.4                   | 4.2                          |

**Q:** Which choice best uses data from the table to complete the evaluation of the temporal segregation hypothesis? The data in the table ________ the temporal segregation hypothesis because the Northern Harrier's active hunting peaked during early dawn (14.2) and late afternoon (15.8) while dropping to a minimum at midday (2.1), which perfectly alternated with the Red-tailed Hawk's hunting profile, which ________

- A) support; reached its maximum intensity during midday at 18.4 hunts per hour, precisely when the Harrier's activity was lowest.
- B) weaken; remained low during early dawn (3.5) and late afternoon (4.2), indicating that the two species consistently avoided hunting at the same times.
- C) refute; peaked at 18.4 hunts per hour during midday, demonstrating that both raptor species are highly active during the exact same intervals of the day.
- D) support; exhibited a completely uniform distribution of 18.4 hunts per hour across all measured periods of the daytime tracking cycle.

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (high)
- unclear_or_ambiguous: The stated hypothesis specifies that one species hunts at early dawn and the other during 'peak afternoon heat,' but the data show the Harrier peaking at BOTH dawn and late afternoon while the Hawk peaks at midday. Strictly, the specific pattern described in the hypothesis is not what the data show, even though broad temporal segregation is supported; this creates a small mismatch between passage and intended answer.
- difficulty_mismatch: Labeled 'hard,' but the three distractors are each self-contradictory or factually inconsistent with the table (B pairs 'weaken' with evidence of avoidance; C and D misstate the numbers), making the item considerably easier than a hard-tier quantitative evidence question.

### R&W · Command of Evidence (Quantitative) · hard · #7 (q00337)

> In materials engineering, the fracture toughness of ceramic matrices can be enhanced by embedding carbon nanotube (CNT) alignments. A common manufacturing assumption is that fracture toughness increases linearly with the volume percentage of embedded nanotubes. Mechanical engineer Sarah Lin hypothesized that this linear model oversimplifies the composite's structural limits. She asserted that while increasing CNT volume up to 2.0% steadily reinforces the ceramic matrix, adding any volume beyond that threshold causes nanotube agglomeration (clumping), which introduces internal structural defects that severely degrade fracture toughness back toward baseline levels.
> Table: Average Fracture Toughness (MPa·m¹/²)
> | Ceramic Matrix Base Type | 0.0% CNT (Baseline) | 2.0% CNT (Optimal) | 5.0% CNT (Excessive) |
> |--------------------------|---------------------|--------------------|----------------------|
> | Alumina Ceramic          | 3.4                 | 6.8                | 3.6                  |
> | Zirconia Ceramic         | 5.2                 | 9.5                | 5.5                  |
> | Silicon Carbide          | 4.1                 | 8.2                | 4.3                  |

**Q:** Which choice best uses data from the table to support Lin's hypothesis over the linear manufacturing assumption?

- A) Zirconia Ceramic possessed the highest baseline fracture toughness at 5.2 MPa·m¹/² and reached an optimal toughness of 9.5 MPa·m¹/² when infused with 2.0% CNT alignments.
- B) Across all three ceramic base types, fracture toughness doubled when CNT content rose from 0.0% to 2.0%, but crashed abruptly back down to near-baseline levels (3.6, 5.5, and 4.3) when CNT content reached an excessive 5.0%.
- C) Infusing Silicon Carbide with 5.0% CNT resulted in an average fracture toughness of 4.3 MPa·m¹/² which was slightly higher than its unreinforced 0.0% baseline value of 4.1 MPa·m¹/².
- D) The fracture toughness of Alumina Ceramic grew by exactly 3.4 points during the initial reinforcement phase, demonstrating a highly predictable growth trajectory.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- factual_or_math_error: Choice B says toughness 'doubled' across all three matrices from 0.0% to 2.0% CNT, but Zirconia rose from 5.2 to 9.5 (about 1.8x, not double). The choice is still clearly the best support for Lin's hypothesis, but the data description is imprecise, which real SAT answer choices generally avoid.

### R&W · Command of Evidence (Quantitative) · hard · #11 (q00341)

> A marine biologist studying coral reef fish communities hypothesized that reef structural complexity — measured as rugosity, a dimensionless index from 0 (flat) to 3 (highly complex) — is a stronger predictor of fish species richness than reef area alone. She surveyed six reef sites, recording rugosity, area (in hectares), and total fish species count. Her analysis suggested that sites with rugosity scores above 2.0 consistently supported more species than larger but flatter reefs, even when the larger reefs had substantially greater area. She concluded that conservation efforts prioritizing structural complexity over sheer size would better protect biodiversity in reef ecosystems under increasing environmental stress.
> Table: Reef Site Characteristics and Fish Species Richness
> | Site | Rugosity | Area (ha) | Fish Species |
> |------|----------|-----------|--------------|
> | A    | 2.4      | 3.1       | 87           |
> | B    | 1.1      | 9.8       | 41           |
> | C    | 2.7      | 2.6       | 103          |
> | D    | 1.8      | 7.4       | 58           |
> | E    | 2.1      | 4.0       | 79           |
> | F    | 0.9      | 11.2      | 34           |

**Q:** Which choice best uses data from the table to support the marine biologist's claim?

- A) Site C, with the highest rugosity (2.7) and smallest area (2.6 ha), supported more fish species (103) than Site B, which had nearly four times the area (9.8 ha) but a rugosity of only 1.1, supporting just 41 species.
- B) Site F, with the largest area (11.2 ha) and the lowest rugosity (0.9), supported the fewest fish species (34), demonstrating that large reef areas with low complexity cannot sustain diverse fish communities.
- C) Sites A, C, and E all had rugosity scores above 2.0 and supported between 79 and 103 fish species, whereas Sites B, D, and F had lower rugosity scores and smaller species counts despite having greater average area.
- D) Site D had a rugosity of 1.8 and supported 58 species across 7.4 ha, while Site E had a rugosity of 2.1 and supported 79 species across only 4.0 ha, showing that modest increases in rugosity can substantially increase species richness.

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (high)
- Also defensible: **A** — A is factually accurate and illustrates high rugosity/small area beating low rugosity/large area, but it rests on a single pair rather than supporting the claim of a consistent pattern, so C is better.

### R&W · Command of Evidence (Quantitative) · hard · #16 (q00346)

> A climate scientist analyzing urban heat island effects across six cities proposed that the intensity of the heat island effect — defined as the difference in average summer temperature between a city's urban core and its surrounding rural areas — scales more strongly with the proportion of impervious surface coverage than with total city population. She maintained that cities with higher impervious surface coverage consistently exhibit stronger heat island effects than comparably or more populous cities with lower coverage, and that population alone is a poor predictor of the phenomenon.
> Table: Urban Heat Island Characteristics for Six Cities
> | City       | Population (millions) | Impervious Surface (%) | Urban-Rural Temp. Difference (°C) |
> |------------|----------------------|------------------------|-----------------------------------|
> | Harwick    | 1.2                  | 71                     | 4.8                               |
> | Larssen    | 3.4                  | 48                     | 3.1                               |
> | Molvane    | 0.9                  | 78                     | 5.3                               |
> | Nethby     | 2.7                  | 55                     | 3.6                               |
> | Orfield    | 4.1                  | 42                     | 2.7                               |
> | Prestwick  | 1.5                  | 66                     | 4.2                               |

**Q:** Which choice best uses data from the table to support the climate scientist's claim?

- A) Molvane, with the highest impervious surface coverage (78%) and the smallest population (0.9 million), records the largest urban-rural temperature difference (5.3°C), while Orfield, the most populous city (4.1 million) but with the lowest coverage (42%), records the smallest difference (2.7°C).
- B) The three cities with impervious surface coverage above 65% — Harwick (71%), Molvane (78%), and Prestwick (66%) — have urban-rural temperature differences of 4.8°C, 5.3°C, and 4.2°C respectively, all exceeding the differences recorded by the three cities with lower coverage (2.7°C–3.6°C), despite the lower-coverage cities having substantially larger average populations.
- C) Across all six cities, urban-rural temperature differences increase as impervious surface coverage increases, ranging from 2.7°C in Orfield (42% coverage) to 5.3°C in Molvane (78% coverage), while population figures show no consistent ordering relative to temperature differences.
- D) Larssen and Nethby, both mid-sized cities by population (3.4 and 2.7 million), have impervious surface coverages of 48% and 55% and temperature differences of 3.1°C and 3.6°C respectively, illustrating that even among similarly populated cities, higher coverage correlates with a greater heat island effect.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- Also defensible: **A** — A accurately cites the two extreme cases (Molvane and Orfield) and genuinely supports the claim, but it rests on only one pair of cities and so does not establish the 'consistently' element of the scientist's claim as fully as B does.

### R&W · Command of Evidence (Quantitative) · hard · #18 (q00348)

> A conservation biologist studying wolf reintroduction in a mountain ecosystem argued that the presence of wolves reduces overgrazing by elk, leading to measurable recovery of streamside vegetation, and that this vegetative recovery in turn increases stream bank stability — as indicated by declining rates of annual soil erosion near monitored stream segments. She predicted that regions with established wolf packs would show both greater vegetation density and lower erosion rates than regions without wolves, and that these differences would be more pronounced in areas where wolves had been present longer.
> Table: Wolf Presence, Vegetation Density, and Erosion Rates by Region
> | Region    | Years Wolf Pack Present | Streamside Vegetation Density (%) | Annual Erosion Rate (tons/km) |
> |-----------|------------------------|-----------------------------------|-------------------------------|
> | Aldervane | 0                      | 34                                | 18.4                          |
> | Brockmere | 3                      | 51                                | 13.7                          |
> | Crowfall  | 6                      | 63                                | 9.2                           |
> | Duskpine  | 0                      | 37                                | 17.1                          |
> | Elloway   | 9                      | 74                                | 6.5                           |
> | Fernglen  | 12                     | 83                                | 4.1                           |

**Q:** Which choice best uses data from the table to support the conservation biologist's claim?

- A) Fernglen, where wolves have been present for 12 years, has the highest vegetation density (83%) and the lowest erosion rate (4.1 tons/km), while Aldervane and Duskpine, where wolves are absent, average 35.5% vegetation density and 17.75 tons/km erosion — differences consistent with the biologist's prediction.
- B) Across all six regions, vegetation density increases and erosion rates decrease in tandem with the number of years a wolf pack has been present, with wolf-free regions averaging approximately 17.8 tons/km erosion compared to 4.1 tons/km in the region with the longest wolf presence.
- C) As years of wolf presence increase from 0 to 12, vegetation density rises progressively from roughly 35–37% to 83%, while annual erosion rates fall from 17–18 tons/km to 4.1 tons/km — a pattern that holds monotonically across all six regions and is consistent with the biologist's prediction that differences are more pronounced with longer wolf presence.
- D) The two wolf-free regions (Aldervane and Duskpine) are similar to each other in both vegetation density (34% and 37%) and erosion rate (18.4 and 17.1 tons/km), suggesting that baseline conditions are comparable, which strengthens the inference that differences observed in wolf-present regions reflect the influence of wolf presence rather than pre-existing environmental variation.

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (medium)
- Also defensible: **B** — B's statement is also factually accurate (vegetation rises and erosion falls in step with years of wolf presence) and supports the claim, though it cites less complete data and does not explicitly document the progressive gradation the biologist predicted.
- multiple_correct_answers: Choice B is a factually accurate and supportive reading of the table, differing from C mainly in thoroughness rather than correctness; C is better because it explicitly documents the monotonic gradation addressing the 'more pronounced with longer presence' part of the prediction, but the distinction is one of degree.
- other: Minor imprecision in C: Aldervane's density is 34%, slightly outside the stated 'roughly 35-37%' range (though hedged by 'roughly').

### R&W · Command of Evidence (Quantitative) · hard · #20 (q00350)

> A transportation engineer studying highway toll pricing argued that dynamic pricing — adjusting toll rates in real time based on traffic volume — reduces average travel time more effectively than fixed toll rates, and that the difference in travel-time reduction between the two pricing systems becomes more substantial as traffic volume increases. She compared average travel times under both systems during four different traffic-volume conditions on a monitored highway corridor, where lower average travel time indicates better traffic flow management.
> Table: Average Travel Time (minutes) by Pricing System and Traffic Volume
> | Traffic Volume (vehicles/hr) | Fixed Toll Avg. Travel Time (min) | Dynamic Toll Avg. Travel Time (min) | Time Saved by Dynamic Pricing (min) |
> |------------------------------|-----------------------------------|-------------------------------------|--------------------------------------|
> | 800                          | 18.2                              | 17.6                                | 0.6                                  |
> | 1,200                        | 22.7                              | 20.9                                | 1.8                                  |
> | 1,600                        | 31.4                              | 26.8                                | 4.6                                  |
> | 2,000                        | 47.3                              | 36.1                                | 11.2                                 |

**Q:** Which choice best uses data from the table to support the transportation engineer's claim?

- A) At 2,000 vehicles per hour, the fixed toll system produced an average travel time of 47.3 minutes compared to 36.1 minutes under dynamic pricing — an 11.2-minute advantage for dynamic pricing and the largest single gap recorded across all volume conditions.
- B) Dynamic pricing reduced average travel time at every traffic volume level tested, saving 0.6 minutes at 800 vehicles per hour and 11.2 minutes at 2,000 vehicles per hour, with the time savings increasing consistently as volume rose.
- C) The time saved by dynamic pricing grew from 0.6 minutes at 800 vehicles per hour to 1.8, 4.6, and 11.2 minutes at successively higher volume levels, demonstrating that dynamic pricing's travel-time advantage over fixed tolls becomes substantially more pronounced as traffic volume increases.
- D) Under fixed toll pricing, average travel times increased sharply as volume rose — from 18.2 minutes at 800 vehicles per hour to 47.3 minutes at 2,000 vehicles per hour — while dynamic pricing constrained travel times more effectively, reaching only 36.1 minutes at the highest volume, a difference of 11.2 minutes.

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (medium)
- Also defensible: **B** — B also addresses both parts of the engineer's claim: it states dynamic pricing lowered travel time at every volume level (the comparative advantage) and that the savings increased consistently as volume rose (the growing difference). Its only weakness relative to C is that it cites just the two endpoint values rather than all four savings figures, so a reviewer could reasonably accept it as correct.
- multiple_correct_answers: Choices B and C both accurately use table data to support both components of the engineer's two-part claim (greater effectiveness of dynamic pricing and a widening gap as volume rises). C is marginally stronger because it documents all four savings values, but B's only shortcoming is citing endpoints rather than every data point, which is a thin basis for distinguishing a single best answer.

### R&W · Command of Evidence (Quantitative) · hard · #23 (q00353)

> An agricultural economist investigated whether irrigation water use efficiency — defined as crop yield per cubic meter of water applied — differs systematically across three irrigation technologies: flood, sprinkler, and drip. He hypothesized that drip irrigation would show not only the highest average efficiency but also the least variability across crop types, making it the most reliably efficient system. He collected yield and water use data across four staple crops using each technology and computed efficiency values for each crop-technology pairing.
> Table: Irrigation Water Use Efficiency (kg of crop per m³ of water)
> | Crop | Flood | Sprinkler | Drip |
> |---|---|---|---|
> | Wheat | 0.6 | 1.1 | 1.8 |
> | Maize | 0.5 | 1.0 | 2.1 |
> | Cotton | 0.4 | 0.8 | 1.5 |
> | Soybean | 0.7 | 1.3 | 1.9 |

**Q:** Which choice best uses data from the table to support the economist's claim?

- A) Drip irrigation produced the highest efficiency value for every crop in the table, with values ranging from 1.5 kg/m³ for cotton to 2.1 kg/m³ for maize, a range of only 0.6 kg/m³, while flood irrigation ranged from 0.4 to 0.7 kg/m³ — a narrower absolute range but across substantially lower values.
- B) Drip irrigation achieved the highest efficiency for all four crops, with values ranging from 1.5 to 2.1 kg/m³, a spread of 0.6 kg/m³, compared to sprinkler irrigation's range of 0.5 kg/m³ (0.8 to 1.3), demonstrating that drip is both the most efficient and — given its higher absolute values — the most reliably efficient system across crop types.
- C) Flood irrigation was the least efficient technology for every crop, confirming that modernizing away from flood systems would improve agricultural water use regardless of the crop grown.
- D) Sprinkler irrigation represented a consistent improvement over flood irrigation across all four crops, with efficiency gains ranging from 0.1 to 0.6 kg/m³, suggesting that intermediate technology upgrades can substantially improve water use efficiency.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (low)
- Also defensible: **A** — A also accurately reports that drip is highest for every crop and makes the same relative-variability point (flood's narrower absolute range occurs across much lower values), so it can be read as equally supportive of the economist's claim; a reviewer could prefer it because it confronts flood, the technology with the smallest absolute spread.
- multiple_correct_answers: Choices A and B present essentially the same evidence and the same logical bridge (drip's larger absolute spread is offset by its much higher absolute values, i.e., lower relative variability). B differs only in explicitly stating the conclusion and comparing to sprinkler rather than flood, so there is no clearly single best answer.
- unclear_or_ambiguous: The claim's 'least variability' component is not supported in absolute terms by the data (ranges: flood 0.3, sprinkler 0.5, drip 0.6). Supporting it requires an unstated relative-variability (coefficient-of-variation) interpretation, which the question never signals, making the intended reasoning ambiguous.

### R&W · Command of Evidence (Quantitative) · hard · #26 (q00356)

> A conservation ecologist argued that reintroducing apex predators to degraded grassland ecosystems does not uniformly restore plant diversity; rather, the benefit depends critically on the baseline vegetation density at the time of reintroduction. In areas with very low initial vegetation density, she predicted, predator reintroduction would have a stronger positive effect on plant species richness than in areas already approaching natural vegetation levels, because in the former case predators suppress herbivore overgrazing that is actively preventing recovery. She compared plots with varying initial vegetation density before and after wolf reintroduction over a five-year period.
> Table: Plant Species Richness Before and After Wolf Reintroduction by Initial Vegetation Density
> | Initial Vegetation Density | Species Richness (Before) | Species Richness (After) | Change |
> |---|---|---|---|
> | Very Low (<20% cover) | 11 | 28 | +17 |
> | Low (20–39% cover) | 19 | 31 | +12 |
> | Moderate (40–59% cover) | 24 | 29 | +5 |
> | High (≥60% cover) | 31 | 33 | +2 |

**Q:** Which choice best uses data from the table to support the ecologist's claim?

- A) Plots with very low initial vegetation density gained 17 species after wolf reintroduction, while plots with high initial density gained only 2 species, demonstrating that the effect of predator reintroduction on plant diversity is inversely related to baseline vegetation density.
- B) All four vegetation density categories showed increases in plant species richness after wolf reintroduction, confirming that apex predator reintroduction reliably enhances plant diversity across ecosystem conditions.
- C) Species richness in very low density plots nearly tripled after wolf reintroduction — rising from 11 to 28 — while high-density plots saw only a marginal increase from 31 to 33, suggesting that the magnitude of recovery is linked to how far a plot is from its natural species richness potential.
- D) Low-density plots (20–39% cover) gained 12 species after reintroduction, a larger gain than moderate-density plots (5 species) but smaller than very low-density plots (17 species), confirming a monotonically decreasing relationship between initial vegetation density and reintroduction benefit.

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (high)
- Also defensible: **D** — D also cites accurate data showing larger gains at lower initial densities, which is consistent with the ecologist's claim; however, it cites only three of the four categories while asserting a 'monotonically decreasing relationship' and does not make the key contrast with plots already near natural vegetation levels, so A is clearly better.

### R&W · Command of Evidence (Quantitative) · hard · #27 (q00357)

> A behavioral economist studying consumer decision-making under uncertainty hypothesized that loss aversion — the tendency to weigh potential losses more heavily than equivalent gains — intensifies as the magnitude of the potential loss increases, but that this intensification plateaus at high-loss magnitudes, producing a diminishing-sensitivity pattern. She conducted an experiment in which participants were offered a series of gambles, each with a 50% chance of a loss and a 50% chance of an equal gain. She recorded the minimum gain amount participants required to accept each gamble, then computed a loss aversion coefficient (required gain divided by potential loss) for each loss magnitude.
> Table: Loss Aversion Coefficients by Potential Loss Magnitude
> | Potential Loss ($) | Mean Required Gain ($) | Loss Aversion Coefficient |
> |---|---|---|
> | 10 | 14 | 1.40 |
> | 50 | 82 | 1.64 |
> | 100 | 171 | 1.71 |
> | 500 | 880 | 1.76 |
> | 1,000 | 1,790 | 1.79 |

**Q:** Which choice best uses data from the table to support the behavioral economist's claim?

- A) The loss aversion coefficient rose from 1.40 at a $10 potential loss to 1.79 at a $1,000 potential loss, an overall increase of 0.39, while the coefficient increased by only 0.03 between the $500 and $1,000 conditions, suggesting that the rate of intensification slows substantially at higher magnitudes.
- B) Participants required a mean gain of $1,790 to accept a $1,000 gamble but only $14 to accept a $10 gamble, demonstrating that loss aversion causes people to demand disproportionately large compensation as potential losses grow larger.
- C) The loss aversion coefficient was above 1.0 for every potential loss magnitude in the study, confirming that participants consistently overweighted losses relative to equivalent gains across all conditions tested.
- D) Between the $10 and $50 conditions, the loss aversion coefficient increased by 0.24 — the largest single-step increase in the table — while subsequent steps produced progressively smaller increments, consistent with a pattern of intensification that diminishes as loss magnitude grows.

**Key:** D · **Reviewer:** D (low)
- Also defensible: **A** — A also accurately cites the data (overall rise of 0.39 from 1.40 to 1.79, and only a 0.03 rise between the $500 and $1,000 conditions) and explicitly supports both parts of the hypothesis — intensification with loss size and a slowdown at high magnitudes. It is essentially as supportive as D, differing only in using two comparisons rather than the full sequence of increments.
- multiple_correct_answers: Both A and D cite accurate table data and support both components of the economist's claim (increasing loss aversion plus diminishing sensitivity at high loss magnitudes). D describes the whole sequence of shrinking increments and A contrasts the overall rise with the minimal top-end rise, but neither contains an error or omission that clearly disqualifies it, so the 'best answer' distinction is not defensible.

### R&W · Inferences · easy · #19 (q00379)

> Sunflowers always face the direction of the sun as it moves across the sky, a behavior called solar tracking or heliotropism. Young sunflowers move their stems throughout the day to follow the sun from east to west and then reset overnight to face east again. Mature sunflowers, however, stop tracking the sun and remain facing east permanently. Researchers have found that east-facing mature flowers warm up faster in the morning, which attracts more pollinating insects early in the day.

**Q:** Based on the passage, what can be reasonably inferred about east-facing mature sunflowers?

- A) East-facing mature sunflowers produce more seeds than west-facing ones.
- B) East-facing mature sunflowers may be more successful at attracting pollinators than west-facing ones.
- C) East-facing mature sunflowers stop growing once they face east permanently.
- D) East-facing mature sunflowers receive the same amount of sunlight as young sunflowers.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- factual_or_math_error: Minor internal inconsistency: the opening sentence asserts sunflowers 'always face the direction of the sun as it moves across the sky,' which is contradicted two sentences later by the statement that mature sunflowers stop tracking and remain facing east. This does not affect which choice is correct, but the overgeneralized 'always' should be softened.

### R&W · Words in Context · hard · #17 (q00587)

> Researchers investigating the resilience of urban ecosystems have found that certain
> bird species demonstrate remarkable plasticity in their foraging behaviors, adopting
> novel food sources and nesting materials with what one ornithologist described as
> unsettling ease. This behavioral flexibility, once considered exceptional, is now
> understood as an adaptive trait differentially selected for in environments where
> resource availability changes rapidly. The species best suited to cities are not
> merely tolerant of disruption — they appear to thrive on it.

**Q:** As used in the passage, "plasticity" most nearly means

- A) resilience
- B) adaptability
- C) intelligence
- D) malleability

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- Also defensible: **D** — "Malleability" is a literal dictionary synonym of plasticity, but it connotes being passively shaped by outside forces, whereas the passage describes birds actively adopting novel resources and is explicitly restated as 'behavioral flexibility,' which matches 'adaptability.'

### R&W · Words in Context · hard · #18 (q00588)

> The novelist's late work was greeted with what her admirers could only call a
> disappointed silence. Critics who had championed her elliptical early prose found
> themselves disarmed by the new novel's directness — its willingness to explain, to
> comfort, and even, in its final pages, to console. This was not the writer who had
> trained her readers to distrust closure. The kindness of her ending felt, to those
> same readers, like a kind of betrayal.

**Q:** As used in the passage, "disarmed" most nearly means

- A) offended
- B) unsettled
- C) silenced
- D) persuaded

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (medium)
- Also defensible: **C** — The opening reference to a 'disappointed silence' makes 'silenced' a tempting reading, though 'disarmed' denotes being stripped of one's critical defenses (disconcerted) rather than literally rendered mute.

### R&W · Words in Context · hard · #21 (q00591)

> Throughout the eighteenth century, the coffeehouse served as the central node of what
> Habermas would later theorize as the bourgeois public sphere: a space in which private
> individuals came together to exercise public reason. Yet historians have grown
> increasingly skeptical of this idealized account. The coffeehouse was also a place of
> commercial transaction, rumor, slander, and speculation — not merely a salon of
> rational deliberation. To describe it as a forum for reasoned discourse is to suppress
> its more turbulent energies in the service of a tidy theoretical narrative.

**Q:** As used in the passage, "suppress" most nearly means

- A) silence
- B) ignore
- C) contradict
- D) conceal

**Key:** D · **Reviewer:** D (medium)
- Also defensible: **B** — "Ignore" also fits the sense that the idealized account leaves the coffeehouse's turbulent aspects out of account; a reader could reasonably read 'suppress' as 'disregard' rather than 'actively hide.'
- unclear_or_ambiguous: Both 'conceal' and 'ignore' are defensible readings of 'suppress' here. The phrase 'in the service of a tidy theoretical narrative' tilts toward deliberate hiding ('conceal'), but the distinction from passive 'ignore' is fine enough that strong students could be split.

### R&W · Words in Context · hard · #22 (q00592)

> The transition from oral to written culture, as Walter Ong argued, was not merely
> a change of medium but a fundamental restructuring of cognition itself. Writing
> enabled — and demanded — a kind of analytical detachment unavailable to purely oral
> cultures, in which knowledge remained inseparable from the living voice that carried
> it. The chirographic mind could externalize thought, inspect it at leisure, and subject
> it to the kind of linear, sequential reasoning that oral discourse, always in motion,
> could never sustain.

**Q:** As used in the passage, "sustain" most nearly means

- A) endure
- B) maintain
- C) support
- D) extend

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (medium)
- Also defensible: **C** — "Support" is a common synonym for "sustain," and "oral discourse could never support linear reasoning" reads naturally; however, the clue "always in motion" points to the idea of keeping something going over time, which "maintain" captures more precisely.

### R&W · Words in Context · hard · #28 (q00598)

> Critics of deliberative democracy have argued that its model of rational consensus
> is premised on an exclusion it cannot acknowledge: the prior silencing of voices
> deemed irrational, emotional, or insufficiently articulate. Deliberation, on this
> account, is not a neutral procedure but a mechanism that launders existing power
> relations into the language of reason. The very norms that govern which contributions
> count as legitimate already encode the preferences of those whose discourse style
> was used to write them.

**Q:** As used in the passage, "encode" most nearly means

- A) reflect
- B) express
- C) conceal
- D) embed

**Key:** D · **Reviewer:** D (medium)
- Also defensible: **A** — "Reflect" is a plausible paraphrase since the norms do mirror the preferences of the powerful, but it loses the sense of the preferences being built into the structure of the norms, which 'encode' conveys.

### R&W · Text Structure and Purpose · medium-low · #8 (q00638)

> For decades, economists largely dismissed the idea that raising the minimum wage causes significant unemployment. The standard concern — that higher wages force employers to hire fewer workers — seemed logical in theory but difficult to confirm empirically. A landmark 1994 study by Card and Krueger compared fast-food employment in two neighboring states before and after one raised its minimum wage and found no significant job losses. Their methodology has since become a model for similar research, and subsequent studies across multiple countries have generally reinforced their finding.

**Q:** What is the main purpose of the text?

- A) To argue that the minimum wage should be raised in all countries
- B) To describe how empirical research challenged a theoretical economic concern
- C) To criticize earlier economists for failing to study employment data
- D) To explain the methodology of the Card and Krueger study in detail

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- factual_or_math_error: The opening sentence states that 'For decades, economists largely dismissed the idea that raising the minimum wage causes significant unemployment,' which both inverts the actual history (the standard view before the 1990s accepted the disemployment prediction) and creates an internal inconsistency: if the idea had already been dismissed, the Card–Krueger evidence would not be overturning a prevailing theoretical expectation. The rest of the passage ('seemed logical in theory but difficult to confirm empirically') and the keyed answer both presuppose that the concern was taken seriously. This does not make the answer ambiguous, but the premise should be reworded (e.g., 'economists largely accepted').

### R&W · Text Structure and Purpose · hard · #11 (q00701)

> The historian's task, Walter Benjamin once suggested, is not to reconstruct the past as it actually was but to seize hold of a memory as it flashes up at a moment of danger. This formulation is deliberately paradoxical: it implies that historical understanding is less a matter of accumulating verified facts than of recognizing the political urgency latent in particular images of the past. Benjamin distrusted the smooth, continuous narratives that professional historians produced, arguing that such narratives inevitably served the interests of those already in power by naturalizing the present order of things. The true historian, for Benjamin, was a kind of redemptive figure who could interrupt that continuity and allow suppressed possibilities to speak.

**Q:** The primary purpose of the passage is to

- A) challenge Benjamin's claim that professional historians produce narratives that serve those in power
- B) explain the paradoxical logic underlying Benjamin's conception of the historian's role
- C) argue that Benjamin's redemptive model of history is more politically effective than conventional historiography
- D) contrast Benjamin's view of historical memory with the standard archival methods of professional historians

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- Also defensible: **D** — The passage does set Benjamin's view against the 'smooth, continuous narratives' of professional historians, so a reader could see contrast as the organizing move; however, that contrast is a supporting detail within the explanation, so B is clearly better.

### R&W · Text Structure and Purpose · hard · #29 (q00719)

> In moral philosophy, Bernard Williams's critique of utilitarianism targeted not its conclusions — which he occasionally found reasonable — but its structure. What Williams found objectionable was the utilitarian demand that agents adopt an impartial, calculative stance toward all outcomes, including outcomes that implicate their own commitments and projects. To be required to treat one's deepest attachments as merely contingent inputs to an impersonal calculation was, for Williams, to be alienated from the very sources of one's identity as a moral agent. The agent who complies fully with the utilitarian demand has not achieved moral purity; she has achieved a kind of integrity-destroying transparency to the moral point of view, becoming, in Williams's famous phrase, "one thought too many" away from what it means to be a person at all.

**Q:** The author's primary purpose in the passage is to

- A) defend Williams's critique of utilitarianism by demonstrating that impartiality is psychologically impossible for normal moral agents
- B) explain the specific structural objection Williams raised against utilitarianism and the conception of moral agency underlying it
- C) compare Williams's critique of utilitarianism with other philosophical attacks on impartialist moral theories
- D) argue that utilitarianism's requirement of impartiality leads to conclusions that are not merely theoretically deficient but practically harmful

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- factual_or_math_error: The passage garbles Williams's famous "one thought too many" remark, which comes from his discussion of a man deliberating about saving his wife, not from a claim about being "'one thought too many' away from what it means to be a person." The phrasing is semantically incoherent, though it does not prevent students from identifying the purpose.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · easy · #8 (q00728)

> Bilingual education — teaching children academic subjects in both their home language and the target language — is more effective than English-only immersion for developing long-term language proficiency. Students who receive bilingual instruction not only achieve English fluency at comparable rates but also maintain and develop their home language, which supports cognitive flexibility and cultural identity.
> Passage 2:
> English immersion programs have shown strong results in helping non-native speakers integrate quickly into English-speaking academic environments. For students whose goal is rapid English proficiency for academic and professional purposes, structured immersion can be the most efficient path, particularly when delivered with strong instructional support.

**Q:** Based on the texts, the authors of Passage 1 and Passage 2 would most likely agree that:

- A) Bilingual education is always superior to immersion programs
- B) The most effective approach to language instruction depends in part on students' goals
- C) English-only immersion should be eliminated from public schools
- D) Students who receive bilingual instruction always outperform immersion students academically

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: The first passage is not labeled (only 'Passage 2' is marked), so the two texts are not clearly delineated; SAT convention also uses 'Text 1'/'Text 2.'
- unclear_or_ambiguous: Minor: Passage 1 never explicitly concedes that effectiveness depends on student goals — it flatly claims bilingual education is more effective — so the agreement in choice B is inferred rather than directly supported for Text 1, though it remains the only defensible option.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · medium-high · #24 (q00804)

> Proponents of the "great man" theory of history argue that pivotal historical transformations are best explained by reference to exceptional individuals whose vision, will, and capability allow them to shape events in ways that ordinary people cannot. Napoleon, Lincoln, and Gandhi are offered as exemplars: their personal qualities, it is claimed, were not merely symptomatic of broader forces but were themselves causal — capable of redirecting the course of events in ways that a different individual in the same position could not have achieved.
> Passage 2:
> Structural historians counter that the great man account inverts the relationship between individuals and the forces that produce them. Exceptional leaders emerge when social, economic, and ideological conditions have already prepared the ground for transformation; they succeed because the moment makes them possible, not the reverse. Had Napoleon not risen, the pressures of post-revolutionary France would have elevated someone else capable of capitalizing on identical conditions. The individual is the variable the historical forces select for, not the independent cause.

**Q:** Based on the passages, which of the following positions would the author of Passage 2 most likely critique as an example of the error described in Passage 1?

- A) The claim that the French Revolution was caused primarily by economic inequality and fiscal crisis rather than by the ideology of the Enlightenment
- B) The claim that Abraham Lincoln's personal moral convictions were a decisive factor in his decision to issue the Emancipation Proclamation rather than a mere reflection of political pressures he faced
- C) The claim that industrialization was driven by technological innovation arising from many independent inventors working within a broader cultural climate of empiricism
- D) The claim that democratic institutions tend to be more stable in societies with strong traditions of civic participation than in those without

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: The first passage is not labeled "Passage 1" (only "Passage 2" is labeled), which is a minor deviation from the standard paired-passage presentation.
- unclear_or_ambiguous: The phrase "the error described in Passage 1" is slightly imprecise: Passage 1 advances the great man theory; the characterization of it as an error comes from Passage 2. The intent is still recoverable, so it does not create a second correct answer.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · hard · #24 (q00834)

> Rewilding — the large-scale restoration of ecosystems through the reintroduction of apex predators — has gained considerable scientific support as a strategy for reversing biodiversity loss. Proponents point to the reintroduction of wolves in Yellowstone as evidence that a single keystone species can trigger trophic cascades, reshaping vegetation patterns, riverbank stability, and the abundance of dozens of other species. For advocates, rewilding offers a rare case of ecological intervention that works with, rather than against, natural processes.
> Passage 2:
> The enthusiasm for rewilding sometimes rests on a romanticized conception of nature as a self-regulating system tending toward equilibrium. Ecologists have increasingly questioned whether the Yellowstone wolf reintroduction produced effects as dramatic and as clearly causal as popular accounts suggest. Some studies indicate that vegetation recovery near riverbeds was driven largely by drought cycles and human management decisions rather than predator-induced shifts in elk grazing behavior — a phenomenon called the "landscape of fear."

**Q:** Based on the passages, the author of Passage 2 would most likely argue that the author of Passage 1 has made which of the following errors?

- A) Overlooking the legal and political obstacles that have historically prevented rewilding programs from being implemented at large scales
- B) Treating one contested case study as though it provides conclusive proof for a broader ecological principle
- C) Failing to acknowledge that apex predators often destabilize ecosystems rather than restore them to equilibrium
- D) Confusing the goals of biodiversity conservation with those of ecosystem restoration, which are not always compatible

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: The first passage lacks a "Passage 1:" header while the second is labeled "Passage 2:", which is inconsistent with College Board presentation of paired texts.
- unclear_or_ambiguous: The appositive placement in Passage 2 ("— a phenomenon called the 'landscape of fear'") sits immediately after the clause about drought cycles and human management, so it reads as naming the wrong referent; the term properly applies to predator-induced shifts in elk grazing behavior.

### R&W · Transitions · easy · #8 (q00848)

> The first passenger railroad in the United States opened in 1830, connecting Baltimore and Ohio. ________, by 1860, the country had over 30,000 miles of track, more than all of Europe combined.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text with the most logical transition?

- A) Instead
- B) In contrast
- C) Within just three decades
- D) On the other hand

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (high)
- factual_or_math_error: The Baltimore and Ohio Railroad's 1830 opening connected Baltimore to Ellicott's Mills, Maryland — it did not connect Baltimore to Ohio in 1830 (it reached the Ohio River in 1853). The phrasing is factually misleading, though this does not affect the transition task.
- other: Minor redundancy: 'Within just three decades, by 1860...' is slightly awkward/repetitive, since the time span is already conveyed by the dates. Still clearly the only logical option.

### R&W · Transitions · easy · #29 (q00869)

> Many schools have replaced traditional textbooks with tablets and laptops in the classroom. Digital devices allow students to access up-to-date information and interactive learning materials. _______, some teachers report that students become easily distracted by games and social media when using personal devices during lessons.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text with the most logical transition?

- A) Furthermore
- B) For instance
- C) Even so
- D) However

**Key:** D · **Reviewer:** D (high)
- Also defensible: **C** — "Even so" is also a concessive contrast transition that fits the relationship between the benefits of digital devices and the drawback of distraction; it is functionally interchangeable with "However" in this sentence.
- multiple_correct_answers: Both C ("Even so") and D ("However") express the required contrast and are equally logical here, so there is no single best answer. A well-constructed item would include only one contrast transition among the options.

### R&W · Transitions · medium-low · #16 (q00886)

> Constructing tall buildings in earthquake-prone regions requires engineers to balance structural strength with flexibility. Rigid structures may resist moderate shaking but are vulnerable to catastrophic failure during severe tremors. _______, modern skyscrapers in cities like Tokyo and San Francisco are designed to sway gently during earthquakes, absorbing seismic energy rather than resisting it outright.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text with the most logical transition?

- A) In contrast
- B) Nonetheless
- C) For this reason
- D) Similarly

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (high)
- Also defensible: **A** — The third sentence does describe swaying buildings as opposed to rigid ones, so a contrast reading is tempting; however, the logic is primarily causal (rigid structures fail, so modern designs sway), making 'For this reason' clearly better.

### R&W · Transitions · medium-high · #1 (q00901)

> Proponents of charter schools argue that they improve educational outcomes by introducing competition: when families can choose between schools, traditional public schools are forced to improve in order to retain students. ________, the research on whether competition has actually improved outcomes in traditional public schools near charters has been mixed, with some studies finding modest gains and others finding no significant effect.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text with the most logical transition?

- A) Furthermore
- B) In other words
- C) The evidence for this claim, however, is
- D) As predicted

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (high)
- formatting: Choice C is not a transition word/phrase but a sentence fragment ('The evidence for this claim, however, is'). Inserted into the blank it produces an ungrammatical sentence: 'The evidence for this claim, however, is, the research on whether competition ... has been mixed.' SAT transition items offer parallel transition words/phrases as choices.
- no_correct_answer: The logical relationship required is contrast (proponents' claim vs. mixed research). The only grammatically well-formed choices — 'Furthermore' (addition), 'In other words' (restatement), and 'As predicted' (confirmation) — all express the wrong relationship, while the only contrastive option (C) is grammatically broken. A proper option such as 'However' or 'Nevertheless' is missing.

### R&W · Transitions · medium-high · #2 (q00902)

> The Mars rovers have transmitted vast quantities of data about the planet's geology, atmosphere, and surface chemistry. ________, interpreting this data requires sustained collaboration between geologists, chemists, atmospheric scientists, and engineers across institutions on multiple continents — a logistical challenge that can slow the pace of discovery.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text with the most logical transition?

- A) As a result
- B) Consequently
- C) Making sense of this data, however, requires
- D) Similarly

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (low)
- Also defensible: **B** — If one reads the relationship as cause-and-effect (the sheer volume of data makes interpretation labor-intensive), 'Consequently' is logical — but it is exactly synonymous with choice A, so neither can be the intended key.
- formatting: Choice C is not a transition word/phrase like the others; it is a rewritten clause ('Making sense of this data, however, requires'). Inserted into the blank it produces redundant, ungrammatical text: 'Making sense of this data, however, requires, interpreting this data requires sustained collaboration...'. The choices are not parallel and the item does not follow the standard SAT transitions format.
- multiple_correct_answers: Choices A ('As a result') and B ('Consequently') are synonymous; if a cause-effect reading is accepted, both would be equally correct, which is impermissible on a single-best-answer item.
- no_correct_answer: The logical relationship signaled by the contrast ('a logistical challenge that can slow the pace of discovery') calls for a contrast transition such as 'However,' which is not offered as a properly formatted choice.

### R&W · Transitions · medium-high · #9 (q00909)

> The nineteenth-century realist novel claimed to represent the world as it actually was — objectively, comprehensively, without authorial intrusion. ________, scholars have shown that this very claim to objectivity was itself a rhetorical construction: realist techniques such as the omniscient narrator and detailed social description produced an effect of transparency that concealed, rather than eliminated, the author's shaping hand.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text with the most logical transition?

- A) In other words
- B) Scholars have since shown, however, that
- C) Similarly
- D) As a result

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: Choice B is not parallel in form with the other options: inserting it into the blank produces the redundant, ungrammatical string "Scholars have since shown, however, that, scholars have shown that this very claim..." The choice apparently was meant to replace both the transition and the clause opener, but the stem retains "scholars have shown that."
- no_correct_answer: The context requires a contrast transition (e.g., 'However' or 'Nevertheless'). A ('In other words'), C ('Similarly'), and D ('As a result') all misstate the relationship, and B cannot be substituted grammatically as written, so no option completes the sentence correctly.

### R&W · Transitions · hard · #6 (q00936)

> Proponents of "rewilding" argue that reintroducing apex predators to ecosystems from which they have been absent can restore ecological balance by triggering cascading effects throughout the food web. ________, this reasoning assumes that ecosystems exist in stable equilibrium states to which they naturally return — an assumption that contemporary ecology has largely abandoned in favor of models that treat ecosystems as dynamic, non-equilibrium systems subject to multiple possible trajectories.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text with the most logical transition?

- A) Furthermore
- B) This argument, however, implicitly rests on
- C) For example
- D) Consequently

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- no_correct_answer: The context requires a contrast transition (e.g., 'However' or 'Nevertheless'), but none of the one-word options (Furthermore, For example, Consequently) signals contrast. The only choice with the correct logical relationship, B, is a full clause fragment that cannot be inserted into the blank grammatically: 'This argument, however, implicitly rests on, this reasoning assumes that...' is ungrammatical and redundant.
- formatting: Choice B is not parallel in form to the other choices (it is a clause rather than a transition word/phrase), indicating the answer set was constructed incorrectly; a proper 'However,' option is missing.

### R&W · Transitions · hard · #12 (q00942)

> The historian Natalie Zemon Davis famously argued that early modern pardon tales — accounts submitted to royal courts by individuals seeking clemency — functioned not merely as legal documents but as literary performances shaped by cultural narratives. Petitioners, she contended, drew on available story structures to frame their actions as sympathetic, inevitable, or accidental. Critics have since pointed out that Davis may have overstated the degree to which petitioners exercised narrative agency, noting that scribes and notaries often mediated and reformulated the accounts before submission. _______ the question of whose voice actually animates these documents remains genuinely contested.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text with the most logical transition?

- A) Accordingly,
- B) Thus,
- C) In either case,
- D) To that end,

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (medium)
- unclear_or_ambiguous: "In either case" is only loosely logical here: the final sentence asserts that the question remains contested, which reads more like a consequence of the scholarly disagreement than a claim true regardless of which view holds. The item relies on elimination (A and B are near-synonyms, D is a purpose transition), so C is the intended and best answer, but its logic is somewhat strained.

### R&W · Transitions · hard · #18 (q00948)

> Classical economists held that wages in a competitive labor market would naturally tend toward a level that reflects workers' marginal productivity — the additional output attributable to one more unit of labor. Monopsony theory complicates this account by demonstrating that when a single employer (or a small number of employers) dominates a local labor market, workers face reduced competition for their services and therefore accept wages below their marginal product. Empirical work over the past two decades has found widespread evidence of monopsonistic wage suppression even in markets with nominally many employers. _______ the competitive wage model, long treated as the appropriate baseline for labor market analysis, may be a special case rather than the general rule.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text with the most logical transition?

- A) By the same token,
- B) Conversely,
- C) If so,
- D) In sum,

**Key:** D · **Reviewer:** D (medium)
- Also defensible: **C** — Because the final sentence is hedged ("may be"), a reader could treat the empirical findings as a proposition whose truth is being supposed, making "If so" a plausible conditional inference marker; it is weaker, though, since the prior sentence reports findings as established rather than hypothesized.

### R&W · Transitions · hard · #28 (q00958)

> Scholars of colonial Caribbean literature have drawn attention to the complex ways in which formerly enslaved writers navigated the constraints of a publishing world controlled overwhelmingly by metropolitan European houses. To gain readership and evade censorship, some authors adopted stylistic conventions — sentimental prose, providential narrative arcs, testimonials framed as moral improvement stories — that superficially aligned with the expectations of white audiences. Closer reading reveals, however, that these same conventions were frequently subverted from within: embedded critiques, strategic silences, and ironic reversals that would have been legible primarily to readers sharing the authors' cultural codes. _______ what looks like accommodation in these texts often encodes a sustained, if necessarily covert, form of resistance.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text with the most logical transition?

- A) Nevertheless,
- B) To clarify,
- C) Thus,
- D) For this reason,

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (medium)
- Also defensible: **D** — "For this reason" is also a conclusive/causal transition and reads acceptably: because the conventions are subverted from within, apparent accommodation encodes resistance. It is functionally near-synonymous with "Thus" in this context, though "Thus" better marks an inference drawn from the cited evidence rather than a single stated cause.
- multiple_correct_answers: Choices C ("Thus") and D ("For this reason") are both conclusion-signaling transitions and are close enough in meaning here that the item lacks a single clearly best answer; College Board items normally avoid including two synonymous conclusive transitions.

### R&W · Transitions · hard · #29 (q00959)

> Marine biologists studying coral reef resilience have noted a troubling feedback dynamic: as water temperatures rise, thermally stressed corals expel the symbiotic algae that give them both color and roughly ninety percent of their nutritional energy. This bleaching process weakens corals' immune responses and growth rates, leaving them more susceptible to infection and physical damage from storm events. Reefs that bleach repeatedly at short intervals cannot complete the recovery cycles needed to restore full structural complexity. _______ even if global temperature increases were halted tomorrow, reefs that have experienced frequent bleaching events in recent decades would remain compromised for generations.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text with the most logical transition?

- A) Still,
- B) Incidentally,
- C) In other words,
- D) By extension,

**Key:** D · **Reviewer:** D (medium)
- Also defensible: **C** — 'In other words' is somewhat tempting since the final sentence restates the failure-to-recover idea, but it adds a new conditional claim (halting warming) and a time scale (generations), so it is an extension rather than a paraphrase.

### R&W · Rhetorical Synthesis · medium-high · #7 (q01026)

> • A longitudinal study tracked 1,200 adults over twenty years.
> • Participants who reported a strong sense of purpose in life had a 15% lower risk of death during the study period than those with low purpose scores.
> • The association held after controlling for age, physical health, income, and social connection.
> • Researchers noted the finding does not establish causation — purposeful living may correlate with other health-protective behaviors.
> • A follow-up analysis found that the protective effect was stronger in participants over age 65.

**Q:** The student wants to present the study's core finding with appropriate methodological precision and note the most important qualification researchers included. Which choice most effectively uses the notes to accomplish this goal?

- A) Adults who reported a strong sense of purpose in life had a 15% lower risk of death over a twenty-year period — an association that remained significant after controlling for major confounds, though researchers cautioned it does not establish a causal relationship.
- B) A twenty-year longitudinal study found that sense of purpose is associated with lower mortality risk, even after accounting for factors like age, income, and physical health, suggesting it may be an independent predictor of longevity.
- C) Researchers found that purposeful living was associated with a 15% reduction in mortality risk over twenty years, with especially strong effects in adults over 65 — a finding they noted could reflect purposeful people engaging in more health-protective behaviors generally.
- D) A longitudinal study of 1,200 adults found that purpose in life predicted longevity over a twenty-year period, reinforcing the growing body of evidence that psychological wellbeing has measurable effects on physical health outcomes.

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (high)
- Also defensible: **C** — C also cites the 15% figure and gestures at the confounding caveat (health-protective behaviors), but it omits the explicit non-causation statement and adds the secondary age-65 finding, so it is less precise about the key qualification.

### R&W · Rhetorical Synthesis · hard · #1 (q01049)

> • Historian Saidiya Hartman developed the concept of "critical fabulation" in her 2008 work Lose Your Mother.
> • Critical fabulation involves combining archival research with imaginative speculation to reconstruct the lives of enslaved people who left few or no written records.
> • Hartman argues that traditional historical methods, reliant on archives, reproduce the silences and distortions imposed by slavery — enslaved people appear only as property, if they appear at all.
> • She uses critical fabulation not to invent freely but to follow the constraints of what the archive permits while filling gaps that would otherwise render these lives invisible.
> • The approach has been praised for its ethical attention to marginalized lives and criticized for blurring the line between history and fiction.

**Q:** The student wants to define critical fabulation, explain why Hartman argues it is necessary, and acknowledge the debate it has generated. Which choice most effectively uses the notes to accomplish this goal?

- A) Saidiya Hartman's "critical fabulation" combines archival research with constrained imaginative speculation to recover the lives of enslaved people who appear in records only as property or not at all; Hartman argues this is necessary because traditional archives reproduce slavery's silences, a method that has been praised for its ethical attention to marginalized lives while being criticized for blurring history and fiction.
- B) In Lose Your Mother, Saidiya Hartman argues that the archives produced under slavery systematically distorted or erased the lives of enslaved people, making conventional historical methods insufficient for recovering those lives in their full humanity.
- C) Critical fabulation, as developed by Hartman, has been both praised and criticized: supporters see it as a necessary ethical response to archival silence, while critics worry it compromises the boundary between scholarly history and imaginative fiction.
- D) Hartman uses imaginative speculation alongside archival research to reconstruct the experiences of enslaved people, arguing that the gaps and silences in historical records require methods that go beyond what traditional historiography permits.

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (high)
- factual_or_math_error: The notes misattribute the concept: Hartman coined "critical fabulation" in the 2008 essay "Venus in Two Acts," while Lose Your Mother was published in 2007. This real-world inaccuracy in the stimulus does not affect the synthesis task or the answer, but a reviewer would fix the citation.

### R&W · Rhetorical Synthesis · hard · #4 (q01052)

> • A policy memo analyzed three proposed interventions to reduce food insecurity in a mid-sized city.
> • Intervention A: subsidize grocery stores in underserved neighborhoods. Projected cost: $2.1 million annually. Projected reduction in food insecurity: 18%.
> • Intervention B: expand the city's free meal program at community centers. Projected cost: $800,000 annually. Projected reduction in food insecurity: 9%.
> • Intervention C: provide monthly grocery vouchers directly to qualifying households. Projected cost: $1.4 million annually. Projected reduction in food insecurity: 22%.
> • The memo notes that Intervention C reaches households regardless of proximity to community centers or grocery stores.
