**Q:** The student wants to recommend Intervention C and support the recommendation with both its cost-effectiveness and a logistical advantage over the other options. Which choice most effectively uses the notes to accomplish this goal?

- A) The policy memo identifies Intervention C — monthly grocery vouchers — as the most effective option, projecting a 22% reduction in food insecurity at a cost of $1.4 million annually, making it less expensive than Intervention A while achieving greater impact.
- B) Among the three proposed interventions, Intervention C produces the largest projected reduction in food insecurity at 22%, and it has the added logistical advantage of reaching households regardless of their proximity to grocery stores or community centers.
- C) Intervention C should be recommended: at $1.4 million annually it achieves a 22% projected reduction in food insecurity — higher than either alternative at lower cost than Intervention A — while also reaching households regardless of proximity to physical facilities, an advantage neither competing option shares.
- D) Intervention C offers the best projected return on investment, reducing food insecurity by 22% at a cost between those of the other two options, and its direct-to-household delivery model avoids the geographic limitations inherent in facility-based programs.

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (high)
- Also defensible: **D** — D also pairs a cost argument (best return on investment, intermediate cost) with the geographic/logistical advantage, so some readers could accept it; it is weaker, however, because it makes an inference ('best return on investment') not stated in the notes, omits the specific comparative cost data, and never explicitly frames the statement as a recommendation.

### R&W · Rhetorical Synthesis · hard · #19 (q01067)

> - Behavioral economist Richard Thaler and legal scholar Cass Sunstein coined the term "nudge" to describe interventions that alter people's choices without restricting options or changing economic incentives.
> - Classic nudge examples include placing healthier foods at eye level in cafeterias or automatically enrolling employees in retirement savings plans while allowing them to opt out.
> - Thaler and Sunstein argue that because human decision-making is systematically influenced by how choices are presented — the "choice architecture" — there is no such thing as a neutral default; every arrangement of options nudges people in some direction.
> - Critics of nudge theory argue that it is paternalistic because it relies on deliberate manipulation of cognitive biases rather than on transparent persuasion that engages individuals as rational agents.

**Q:** The student wants to present the strongest version of the nudge theorists' response to the paternalism objection. Which choice most effectively uses the notes to accomplish this goal?

- A) Thaler and Sunstein would likely respond to paternalism objections by pointing out that nudges, unlike mandates, always preserve individual freedom of choice — employees can still opt out of retirement plans, and cafeteria visitors can still choose less healthy foods.
- B) Because choice architecture is unavoidable — every arrangement of options already nudges decision-makers in some direction — the paternalism objection misidentifies the alternative to nudging: the real choice is not between manipulation and neutrality but between unconscious, unexamined defaults and deliberate ones designed with human welfare in mind.
- C) Nudge theory has attracted criticism from both ends of the political spectrum: libertarians object to its paternalism, while some progressives argue that nudging treats structural problems as individual behavioral failures amenable to soft interventions.
- D) Thaler and Sunstein's insight that human beings are not the rational agents assumed by classical economics has been widely accepted in both academic and policy circles, lending credibility to the argument that choice environments should be designed with actual human psychology in mind.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- Also defensible: **A** — A also articulates a genuine nudge-theory rebuttal (freedom of choice is preserved), but the stated objection concerns manipulation rather than restriction of options, so A addresses a point critics already concede; B engages the objection's premise directly and is clearly stronger.

### R&W · Boundaries · easy · #4 (q01082)

> Marcus ran three miles every morning _______ he wanted to prepare for the upcoming marathon.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text so that it conforms to the conventions of Standard Written English?

- A) , because
- B) ; because
- C) because,
- D) , and

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (medium)
- Also defensible: **D** — ', and' also produces a conventionally correct sentence (comma + coordinating conjunction joining two independent clauses). It is less logical, since it loses the causal relationship, but the question asks only about conformity to Standard English conventions.
- multiple_correct_answers: Both A and D yield punctuation that conforms to Standard English conventions; the choices vary the conjunction (because vs. and), so the item is decided by meaning rather than convention, contrary to its stated task.
- other: The conventionally preferred version of this sentence has no comma before the restrictive 'because' clause; no such option is offered, so the keyed answer (A) uses punctuation the SAT usually treats as optional/avoided.

### R&W · Boundaries · easy · #6 (q01084)

> The novel was widely praised by critics _______ it sold only a modest number of copies in its first year.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text so that it conforms to the conventions of Standard Written English?

- A) , yet
- B) , so
- C) ; however,
- D) , therefore

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (high)
- Also defensible: **C** — "; however," also correctly joins two independent clauses and conveys the same contrast: 'The novel was widely praised by critics; however, it sold only a modest number of copies in its first year.' This is fully conventional Standard English, making it equally defensible.
- multiple_correct_answers: Both A (', yet') and C ('; however,') are grammatically correct and logically appropriate contrast connectors between two independent clauses. A valid SAT item would alter C (e.g., ', however,' creating a comma splice) so only one option is conventional.

### R&W · Boundaries · easy · #10 (q01088)

> The new bridge was completed ahead of schedule _______ the construction team worked overtime for several consecutive weeks to meet the deadline.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text so that it conforms to the conventions of Standard Written English?

- A) , since
- B) , and
- C) ;
- D) . Because

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (high)
- Also defensible: **B** — A comma plus the coordinating conjunction 'and' correctly joins two independent clauses, so this is grammatically acceptable under Standard English conventions.
- Also defensible: **C** — A semicolon correctly joins the two independent clauses; the causal relationship can be left implied, so this is also grammatically correct.
- multiple_correct_answers: Three of the four options (', since', ', and', and ';') all produce grammatically correct sentences joining two independent clauses. Only D creates a fragment. A Boundaries item should leave exactly one punctuation/conjunction option that conforms to Standard English; here the distinction relies on logical meaning rather than convention, which is not what the question asks.

### R&W · Boundaries · easy · #11 (q01089)

> Maria loves to bake on weekends. Her favorite treats to make are chocolate chip cookies, banana bread, and cinnamon rolls. She usually starts early in the morning so that everything is ready by lunchtime. Her family always gathers in the kitchen when they smell the warm sugar and spice drifting through the house.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text with the most logical and grammatically correct punctuation?

- A) Maria loves to bake on weekends, her favorite treats to make are chocolate chip cookies, banana bread, and cinnamon rolls.
- B) Maria loves to bake on weekends. Her favorite treats to make are chocolate chip cookies, banana bread, and cinnamon rolls.
- C) Maria loves to bake on weekends her favorite treats to make are chocolate chip cookies, banana bread, and cinnamon rolls.
- D) Maria loves to bake on weekends; her favorite treats to make are chocolate chip cookies, banana bread, and cinnamon rolls, she starts early.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: The item has no blank or underlined portion; the choices replace the first two sentences of the passage, and the passage itself already presents the correct version verbatim, giving the answer away.
- unclear_or_ambiguous: The stem asks which choice 'completes the text,' but nothing is incomplete; the task is really to choose the correctly punctuated rewrite, which is not stated.
- other: Choice D adds new content ('she starts early') not present in the passage, which violates SAT Boundaries conventions where options differ only in punctuation.

### R&W · Boundaries · easy · #22 (q01100)

> The school library recently added a new section for graphic novels. Students quickly filled the chairs around the reading tables. James picked up a copy of a popular series, he had been wanting to read it for months. Within minutes, he was completely absorbed in the story.

**Q:** Which choice best corrects the underlined portion of the passage?

- A) James picked up a copy of a popular series. He had been wanting to read it for months.
- B) James picked up a copy of a popular series, and he had been wanting to read it for months.
- C) James picked up a copy of a popular series; and he had been wanting to read it for months.
- D) James picked up a copy of a popular series he had been wanting to read it for months.

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (medium)
- Also defensible: **B** — A comma followed by the coordinating conjunction 'and' is a standard, fully grammatical way to join two independent clauses, so this option also corrects the comma splice without introducing any error.
- multiple_correct_answers: Both A (period separating two independent clauses) and B (comma + coordinating conjunction 'and') are conventionally correct fixes for the comma splice; nothing in the passage makes 'and' illogical, so two choices are defensible.
- formatting: The prompt refers to an 'underlined portion,' but no portion of the passage is actually underlined or otherwise marked, so the student must infer which sentence is being tested.

### R&W · Boundaries · medium-low · #2 (q01110)

> The museum's new exhibit features artifacts from the ancient world, including pottery, jewelry, and tools _______ several of the pieces on display have never before been shown outside their country of origin.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text so that it conforms to the conventions of Standard Written English?

- A) ,
- B) ; and
- C) ;
- D) , and

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (medium)
- Also defensible: **D** — A comma plus the coordinating conjunction 'and' is also a standard way to join two independent clauses, so '..., and several of the pieces...' is grammatically acceptable; it is only disfavored here because 'and' can momentarily seem to extend the series 'pottery, jewelry, and tools.'
- multiple_correct_answers: Both C (semicolon) and D (comma + 'and') correctly join the two independent clauses under Standard English conventions. The intended key is presumably C because ', and' could be misread as continuing the preceding list, but that is a stylistic rather than a grammatical objection, leaving two defensible answers.

### R&W · Boundaries · medium-low · #5 (q01113)

> The hiking trail was well-marked and the terrain was moderate _______ the group completed the ten-mile loop in under four hours.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text so that it conforms to the conventions of Standard Written English?

- A) , so
- B) ;
- C) , but
- D) , and

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (high)
- Also defensible: **B** — A semicolon correctly joins two independent clauses, so this is also conventionally acceptable; only logic (result relationship) argues against it.
- Also defensible: **D** — A comma plus the coordinating conjunction 'and' also correctly joins two independent clauses and is logically acceptable.
- multiple_correct_answers: The prompt asks only about conventions of Standard Written English, but ', so' (A), ';' (B), and ', and' (D) all correctly join the two independent clauses. Distinguishing among them requires a logical/transition judgment the question does not ask for. The item should have only one punctuation-valid option (e.g., replace the distractors with a comma splice, a missing-comma run-on, and an inappropriate colon/dash).

### R&W · Boundaries · medium-low · #9 (q01117)

> The architect's proposal was ambitious _______ it called for replacing the existing structure entirely rather than renovating it, which would cost significantly more and require displacing tenants for up to eighteen months.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text so that it conforms to the conventions of Standard Written English?

- A) ,
- B) :
- C) ; however,
- D) , and

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- Also defensible: **D** — A comma plus the coordinating conjunction 'and' correctly joins two independent clauses ('The architect's proposal was ambitious' and 'it called for replacing...'), so this option is also grammatically acceptable; it is merely less precise than the colon, which signals that the second clause elaborates on the first.
- multiple_correct_answers: Both B (colon introducing an elaboration) and D (comma + 'and' joining two independent clauses) conform to the conventions of Standard Written English. Since the question asks only about conventions, not rhetorical precision, D cannot be eliminated on grammatical grounds. Authentic SAT Boundaries items make every distractor grammatically unacceptable.

### R&W · Boundaries · medium-low · #14 (q01122)

> The concept of informed consent is central to modern medical ethics. Before undergoing any procedure, patients must be told about its risks, benefits, and alternatives. This requirement protects patient autonomy, it ensures that individuals can make decisions about their own bodies based on accurate information.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text with the most logical and grammatically correct use of punctuation?

- A) patient autonomy, it ensures
- B) patient autonomy it ensures
- C) patient autonomy; it ensures
- D) patient autonomy, ensuring

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (high)
- Also defensible: **D** — 'This requirement protects patient autonomy, ensuring that individuals can make decisions...' is also fully grammatical and logical: a comma plus a participial phrase correctly modifies the preceding clause. It conveys the same meaning and violates no convention.
- multiple_correct_answers: Both C (semicolon joining two independent clauses) and D (comma + participial phrase 'ensuring') produce grammatically correct, logical sentences. Authentic SAT Boundaries items make the non-keyed options clearly incorrect (e.g., by creating comma splices, fragments, or misplaced modifiers); here D is a legitimate alternative, so the item has two defensible answers.

### R&W · Boundaries · medium-high · #2 (q01140)

> The legislation passed with bipartisan support _______ a rare occurrence in the current political climate _______ signaling that infrastructure investment had become a priority that transcended party lines.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text so that it conforms to the conventions of Standard Written English?

- A) , . . .
- B) — . . . —
- C) ; . . . ,
- D) , . . . ,

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (medium)
- Also defensible: **D** — Paired commas also correctly set off the nonessential appositive 'a rare occurrence in the current political climate,' and the following participial phrase 'signaling...' attaches acceptably; this version is grammatically sound as well.
- multiple_correct_answers: Both B (paired dashes) and D (paired commas) correctly enclose the nonessential appositive. Since the appositive contains no internal commas, there is no conventions-based reason to prefer dashes over commas, so two choices conform to Standard English.

### R&W · Boundaries · medium-high · #3 (q01141)

> The philosopher's argument rests on a distinction that many readers find initially counterintuitive _______ the distinction between what a person does and what merely happens to a person.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text so that it conforms to the conventions of Standard Written English?

- A) ,
- B) ;
- C) :
- D) , being

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (high)
- Also defensible: **A** — A comma can introduce a nonessential noun appositive ('..., the distinction between what a person does and what merely happens to a person'), so this is grammatically acceptable in Standard English as well, though less precise than the colon for introducing an explanatory restatement.
- multiple_correct_answers: The element after the blank is a noun-phrase appositive, which a comma can legitimately introduce; the item would be cleaner if the comma distractor were replaced (official items usually avoid offering both a comma and a colon before a final appositive).

### R&W · Boundaries · medium-high · #4 (q01142)

> The company's new remote work policy requires employees to be available during core hours _______ nine in the morning to three in the afternoon _______ but allows flexibility outside of that window.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text so that it conforms to the conventions of Standard Written English?

- A) , . . . ,
- B) : . . . ,
- C) — . . . —
- D) ; . . . ;

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (medium)
- Also defensible: **A** — A pair of commas is also standard punctuation for setting off the nonessential appositive 'nine in the morning to three in the afternoon,' which contains no internal commas; nothing in the sentence makes commas incorrect.
- multiple_correct_answers: Both the paired dashes (C) and the paired commas (A) correctly set off the nonessential appositive. On authentic SAT items, paired commas are ruled out only when the inserted element contains internal commas or when the element is essential; neither condition applies here, so A is also grammatically acceptable.

### R&W · Boundaries · medium-high · #5 (q01143)

> The study's authors acknowledge several limitations _______ the sample was drawn from a single urban region, the follow-up period was limited to six months, and self-reported data may not accurately reflect actual behavior.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text so that it conforms to the conventions of Standard Written English?

- A) ,
- B) ;
- C) :
- D) — including that

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (high)
- Also defensible: **B** — A semicolon would also be grammatically permissible, since the material after the blank is itself a compound of three independent clauses joined by commas and 'and' ('the sample was drawn...; the follow-up period was limited...; and self-reported data may not...'). A semicolon can join two independent clauses, so B is not strictly ungrammatical, though the colon is clearly better because the second part specifies/elaborates on 'several limitations.'
- multiple_correct_answers: Because the text following the blank is a series of independent clauses (not a noun list or fragment), the semicolon in choice B produces a technically grammatical sentence as well. Official SAT items normally avoid offering both a colon and a semicolon when the following material is an independent clause. To make the colon uniquely correct, the elaboration should be a non-clausal list or the semicolon option removed.

### R&W · Boundaries · medium-high · #7 (q01145)

> The novel's central tension arises from the gap between what the narrator claims to know and what the reader gradually suspects _______ namely, that the narrator has been deliberately concealing crucial information from the beginning.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text so that it conforms to the conventions of Standard Written English?

- A) ,
- B) ;
- C) —
- D) :

**Key:** D · **Reviewer:** D (medium)
- Also defensible: **C** — An em dash can equally well introduce an explanatory appositive after a complete independent clause; 'suspects — namely, that...' is fully standard punctuation, so C is also grammatically correct.
- multiple_correct_answers: Both the colon (D) and the em dash (C) correctly introduce the explanatory 'namely' appositive following a complete independent clause. SAT items normally avoid pairing colon and dash when the preceding clause is independent and the following element is a non-sentence elaboration, since both are conventionally acceptable.

### R&W · Boundaries · medium-high · #8 (q01146)

> The findings of the two studies appear to contradict each other _______ the first concludes that early intervention produces lasting benefits, while the second finds no significant long-term effect — a discrepancy the researchers attribute to differences in methodology.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text so that it conforms to the conventions of Standard Written English?

- A) ,
- B) :
- C) ; however,
- D) , and

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- Also defensible: **D** — A comma plus the coordinating conjunction 'and' can legitimately join two independent clauses, so ', and' is not a grammatical error; it is only inferior on logical grounds (the second clause elaborates rather than adds).
- multiple_correct_answers: The prompt asks only about conformity to Standard Written English, but both B (colon introducing an elaboration) and D (comma + coordinating conjunction joining two independent clauses) are grammatically acceptable. B is best only because of meaning/logic, which the question stem does not ask about. Replacing D with something clearly ungrammatical (e.g., 'and' with no comma, or a comma before a non-independent element) would fix the item.

### R&W · Boundaries · medium-high · #9 (q01147)

> The senator's speech was notable not for its rhetorical flourishes _______ which were considerable _______ but for the specific policy proposals it contained, several of which broke with her party's established positions.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text so that it conforms to the conventions of Standard Written English?

- A) , . . . ,
- B) — . . . —
- C) ; . . . ;
- D) : . . . ,

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (medium)
- Also defensible: **A** — A matched pair of commas is also a conventional way to set off the nonrestrictive clause 'which were considerable': 'notable not for its rhetorical flourishes, which were considerable, but for the specific policy proposals...' is grammatically correct Standard Written English.
- multiple_correct_answers: Both the paired em dashes (B) and the paired commas (A) correctly set off the nonrestrictive clause 'which were considerable'; neither creates an error, so two choices are defensible. SAT items normally ensure only one punctuation pattern is acceptable.

### R&W · Boundaries · medium-high · #12 (q01150)

> Echolocation, the process by which certain animals emit high-frequency sound waves and interpret the returning echoes, is employed by bats, dolphins, and several species of shrews. Researchers studying this phenomenon have discovered that the neural pathways devoted to auditory processing in these animals are significantly more developed than those found in species that rely primarily on vision. This finding has prompted new investigations into how sensory specialization shapes overall brain architecture.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text with the most logical and grammatically correct use of sentence boundaries?

- A) Echolocation the process by which certain animals emit high-frequency sound waves and interpret the returning echoes, is employed by bats, dolphins, and several species of shrews.
- B) Echolocation, the process by which certain animals emit high-frequency sound waves and interpret the returning echoes is employed by bats, dolphins, and several species of shrews.
- C) Echolocation: the process by which certain animals emit high-frequency sound waves and interpret the returning echoes, is employed by bats, dolphins, and several species of shrews.
- D) Echolocation, the process by which certain animals emit high-frequency sound waves and interpret the returning echoes, is employed by bats, dolphins, and several species of shrews.

**Key:** D · **Reviewer:** D (high)
- formatting: The item is presented as a 'complete the text' question, but the passage contains no blank — the first sentence is already written out in full, and it is identical to choice D. The correct answer is given away in the stimulus.
- difficulty_mismatch: Because the passage reproduces the correct punctuation verbatim, the item requires no reasoning about boundaries and is far easier than the stated medium-high difficulty.
- unclear_or_ambiguous: The prompt asks about 'sentence boundaries,' but all four options test only comma placement around a nonessential appositive within a single sentence; no sentence-boundary (splice/fragment) issue is actually at stake.

### R&W · Boundaries · medium-high · #14 (q01152)

> The philosopher Immanuel Kant argued that moral worth derives not from the consequences of an action but from the intention behind it. An act performed out of duty, regardless of its outcome, carries genuine moral weight, whereas one performed merely for personal gain does not. This distinction between duty-based ethics and outcome-based ethics continues to animate debates in contemporary moral philosophy, with scholars on both sides marshaling increasingly sophisticated arguments.

**Q:** Which choice best maintains the sentence boundaries established in the passage?

- A) An act performed out of duty regardless of its outcome carries genuine moral weight, whereas one performed merely for personal gain does not.
- B) An act performed out of duty, regardless of its outcome carries genuine moral weight; whereas one performed merely for personal gain does not.
- C) An act performed out of duty, regardless of its outcome, carries genuine moral weight, whereas one performed merely for personal gain does not.
- D) An act performed out of duty, regardless of its outcome, carries genuine moral weight; whereas one performed merely for personal gain does not.

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (high)
- formatting: The sentence being tested appears verbatim in the passage rather than as a blank, so the correct answer (C) can be identified by simple matching without applying any punctuation knowledge. A proper Boundaries item would present the sentence with a blank or underlined portion.
- unclear_or_ambiguous: The stem ('best maintains the sentence boundaries established in the passage') is nonstandard; College Board Boundaries items ask which choice 'conforms to the conventions of Standard English.' As written, the task is vague about what is being evaluated.
- difficulty_mismatch: Because the answer is copied directly from the passage, the item is far easier than the stated medium-high difficulty.

### R&W · Boundaries · medium-high · #21 (q01159)

> The philosopher Hannah Arendt argued that the concept of "the banality of evil" was frequently misunderstood by her critics. She did not mean that evil itself was mundane, rather, she meant that the perpetrators of evil acts often lacked the capacity for independent moral reasoning. This absence of reflection, she insisted, was more dangerous than malice, because it allowed ordinary people to participate in atrocities without recognizing their own complicity.

**Q:** Which choice most effectively corrects the boundary error in the sentence above?

- A) She did not mean that evil itself was mundane: rather, she meant that the perpetrators of evil acts often lacked the capacity for independent moral reasoning.
- B) She did not mean that evil itself was mundane rather, she meant that the perpetrators of evil acts often lacked the capacity for independent moral reasoning.
- C) She did not mean that evil itself was mundane, rather she meant that the perpetrators of evil acts often lacked the capacity for independent moral reasoning.
- D) She did not mean that evil itself was mundane. Rather she meant that the perpetrators of evil acts often lacked the capacity for independent moral reasoning.

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (medium)
- Also defensible: **D** — The period also resolves the comma splice; the only remaining issue is the omitted comma after the transition 'Rather,' which some style guides permit, so a lenient reader could accept D as a correct boundary fix.
- multiple_correct_answers: Both A (colon joining two independent clauses) and D (period creating two sentences) eliminate the comma splice. D is presumably meant to be wrong only because it omits the comma after the introductory 'Rather,' which is a comma-usage nicety rather than a boundary error, making the distinction between A and D debatable. A cleaner item would have offered a semicolon option ('mundane; rather, she meant') or included the comma in D to make only one choice fully correct.

### R&W · Boundaries · medium-high · #22 (q01160)

> The ringtail possum, native to the forests of eastern Australia, is a highly social marsupial that builds elaborate nests called dreys. Unlike many of its relatives, it feeds almost exclusively on leaves, it has developed specialized gut bacteria that neutralize the toxins found in eucalyptus foliage. This adaptation allows the ringtail to exploit a food source largely unavailable to other mammals in its habitat.

**Q:** Which choice most effectively corrects the boundary error in the underlined portion of the passage?

- A) Unlike many of its relatives, it feeds almost exclusively on leaves, having developed specialized gut bacteria that neutralize the toxins found in eucalyptus foliage.
- B) Unlike many of its relatives, it feeds almost exclusively on leaves it has developed specialized gut bacteria that neutralize the toxins found in eucalyptus foliage.
- C) Unlike many of its relatives, it feeds almost exclusively on leaves; it has developed specialized gut bacteria that neutralize the toxins found in eucalyptus foliage.
- D) Unlike many of its relatives, it feeds almost exclusively on leaves — it has developed specialized gut bacteria, that neutralize the toxins found in eucalyptus foliage.

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (high)
- Also defensible: **A** — Converting the second clause into a participial phrase ('having developed...') also eliminates the comma splice and produces a fully grammatical, idiomatic sentence, so it is defensible as a correction.
- multiple_correct_answers: Both C (semicolon joining two independent clauses) and A (reducing the second clause to a participial phrase) correct the comma splice and are grammatically acceptable; authentic SAT items include only one error-free option.
- formatting: The passage contains no actual underlining (or other marking) of the portion to be revised, even though the question refers to 'the underlined portion.'

### R&W · Boundaries · medium-high · #23 (q01161)

> Many historians consider the Compromise of 1850 a desperate, if temporary, solution to the sectional tensions tearing the United States apart. Senator Henry Clay, who had brokered major compromises before, presented the package as a final settlement. His colleague Daniel Webster broke with his abolitionist supporters to endorse it, Webster argued that national unity had to take precedence over the slavery question, a position that effectively ended his presidential ambitions.

**Q:** Which choice most effectively corrects the boundary error in the passage?

- A) His colleague Daniel Webster broke with his abolitionist supporters to endorse it. Webster argued that national unity had to take precedence over the slavery question, a position that effectively ended his presidential ambitions.
- B) His colleague Daniel Webster broke with his abolitionist supporters to endorse it — Webster argued that national unity had to take precedence over the slavery question, a position that effectively ended his presidential ambitions.
- C) His colleague Daniel Webster broke with his abolitionist supporters to endorse it; Webster argued that national unity had to take precedence over the slavery question, a position that effectively ended his presidential ambitions.
- D) His colleague Daniel Webster broke with his abolitionist supporters to endorse it, and Webster argued that national unity had to take precedence over the slavery question, a position that effectively ended his presidential ambitions.

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (high)
- Also defensible: **C** — A semicolon correctly joins two independent clauses and is arguably the most idiomatic fix since the second clause elaborates on the first.
- Also defensible: **B** — An em dash is an accepted way to join two independent clauses under SAT conventions, so this also eliminates the comma splice.
- Also defensible: **D** — Adding the coordinating conjunction 'and' after the comma produces a grammatically correct compound sentence, also fixing the splice.
- multiple_correct_answers: The error is a comma splice, and every option repairs it by a conventionally acceptable means: a period (A), an em dash (B), a semicolon (C), and a comma plus coordinating conjunction (D). Since all four are grammatically correct, there is no single best answer; three of the choices would need to be rewritten to retain an error (e.g., keep a bare comma, use a colon incorrectly, or create a fragment).

### R&W · Boundaries · medium-high · #26 (q01164)

> Mycorrhizal networks — the web of fungal filaments that connect plant root systems beneath the soil — play a crucial role in forest ecosystems. Through these networks, trees exchange nutrients, water, and even chemical distress signals. A mature Douglas fir may be linked to hundreds of neighboring trees, some researchers have called these interconnected systems "wood wide webs," a term that, while evocative, has been criticized for overstating the degree of intentional cooperation involved.

**Q:** Which choice most effectively corrects the boundary error in the passage?

- A) A mature Douglas fir may be linked to hundreds of neighboring trees, and some researchers have called these interconnected systems "wood wide webs," a term that, while evocative, has been criticized for overstating the degree of intentional cooperation involved.
- B) A mature Douglas fir may be linked to hundreds of neighboring trees; some researchers have called these interconnected systems "wood wide webs," a term that, while evocative, has been criticized for overstating the degree of intentional cooperation involved.
- C) A mature Douglas fir may be linked to hundreds of neighboring trees — some researchers have called these interconnected systems "wood wide webs," a term that, while evocative, has been criticized for overstating the degree of intentional cooperation involved.
- D) A mature Douglas fir may be linked to hundreds of neighboring trees. Some researchers have called these interconnected systems "wood wide webs," a term that, while evocative, has been criticized for overstating the degree of intentional cooperation involved.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (medium)
- Also defensible: **A** — Adding the coordinating conjunction 'and' after the comma produces a fully grammatical compound sentence, a standard fix for a comma splice.
- Also defensible: **C** — A single em dash can legitimately join an independent clause that elaborates on the preceding one; it is grammatically acceptable here (though the passage already uses a dash pair).
- Also defensible: **D** — Splitting the comma splice into two separate sentences with a period is also fully correct Standard English.
- multiple_correct_answers: The underlying error is a comma splice, and all four choices eliminate it in grammatically acceptable ways (coordinating conjunction, semicolon, em dash, period). SAT Boundaries items normally have exactly one grammatically viable option; here the distinction between choices is purely stylistic, so no single answer is defensibly 'best.'
- unclear_or_ambiguous: The prompt asks which choice 'most effectively corrects' the error without giving any criterion (e.g., concision, emphasis, relationship between ideas) that would distinguish among four correct revisions.

### R&W · Boundaries · medium-high · #27 (q01165)

> The early twentieth-century architect Louis Sullivan is credited with the dictum "form follows function," a principle suggesting that a building's shape should arise naturally from its intended purpose. Sullivan's own structures, however, were famously ornate. His decorative ironwork — dense with geometric and botanical motifs — seemed to contradict the very principle he espoused, critics argued that this tension revealed not hypocrisy but a deeper belief that ornament was itself a functional element of human experience.

**Q:** Which choice most effectively corrects the boundary error in the passage?

- A) His decorative ironwork — dense with geometric and botanical motifs — seemed to contradict the very principle he espoused. Critics argued that this tension revealed not hypocrisy but a deeper belief that ornament was itself a functional element of human experience.
- B) His decorative ironwork — dense with geometric and botanical motifs — seemed to contradict the very principle he espoused; critics argued that this tension revealed not hypocrisy but a deeper belief that ornament was itself a functional element of human experience.
- C) His decorative ironwork — dense with geometric and botanical motifs — seemed to contradict the very principle he espoused, and critics argued that this tension revealed not hypocrisy but a deeper belief that ornament was itself a functional element of human experience.
- D) His decorative ironwork — dense with geometric and botanical motifs — seemed to contradict the very principle he espoused: critics argued that this tension revealed not hypocrisy but a deeper belief that ornament was itself a functional element of human experience.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (medium)
- Also defensible: **A** — Replacing the comma splice with a period produces two fully grammatical, correctly punctuated sentences; this is an equally valid fix.
- Also defensible: **C** — Adding the coordinating conjunction 'and' after the comma creates a grammatically correct compound sentence, another standard way to repair a comma splice.
- Also defensible: **D** — A colon after an independent clause is permissible when what follows elaborates; the critics' explanation can be read as elaborating on the apparent contradiction, so this is also grammatical.
- multiple_correct_answers: The underlying error is a comma splice, and all four choices eliminate it in conventionally acceptable ways (period, semicolon, comma + 'and', colon). No single option is uniquely correct under Standard English conventions, so the item has no defensible single key.
- unclear_or_ambiguous: The prompt asks which choice 'most effectively corrects the boundary error,' but SAT Boundaries items normally have exactly one grammatical option; here the student must guess a stylistic preference among equally valid repairs.

### R&W · Boundaries · medium-high · #30 (q01168)

> During the nineteenth century, the Hudson River School of painting transformed American landscape art by presenting the wilderness not as a hostile void but as a space of spiritual grandeur. Thomas Cole, the movement's founder, believed that the American landscape embodied a kind of divine narrative; in his famous "Course of Empire" series, he extended this vision beyond nature alone to encompass the rise and fall of entire civilizations. His students, particularly Frederic Edwin Church, pushed the style toward even greater technical virtuosity, Church's enormous canvases depicted South American and Arctic scenes with a scientific precision that reflected the era's intertwining of artistic ambition and geographic exploration.

**Q:** Which choice most effectively corrects the boundary error in the passage?

- A) His students, particularly Frederic Edwin Church, pushed the style toward even greater technical virtuosity; Church's enormous canvases depicted South American and Arctic scenes with a scientific precision that reflected the era's intertwining of artistic ambition and geographic exploration.
- B) His students, particularly Frederic Edwin Church, pushed the style toward even greater technical virtuosity, and Church's enormous canvases depicted South American and Arctic scenes with a scientific precision that reflected the era's intertwining of artistic ambition and geographic exploration.
- C) His students, particularly Frederic Edwin Church, pushed the style toward even greater technical virtuosity — Church's enormous canvases depicted South American and Arctic scenes with a scientific precision that reflected the era's intertwining of artistic ambition and geographic exploration.
- D) His students, particularly Frederic Edwin Church, pushed the style toward even greater technical virtuosity: Church's enormous canvases depicted South American and Arctic scenes with a scientific precision that reflected the era's intertwining of artistic ambition and geographic exploration.

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (high)
- Also defensible: **B** — A comma plus the coordinating conjunction 'and' is a standard, grammatically correct way to join two independent clauses, so it also eliminates the comma splice.
- Also defensible: **C** — A dash can legitimately join an independent clause to a following independent clause that expands on it; grammatically correct.
- Also defensible: **D** — A colon is correct after a complete independent clause when what follows explains or illustrates it, which is the case here.
- multiple_correct_answers: The original sentence is a comma splice, and all four choices (semicolon, comma + coordinating conjunction, dash, colon) correct it in Standard English. Authentic SAT Boundaries items include only one grammatically viable option; here there is no basis in grammar for preferring A over B, C, or D.

### R&W · Boundaries · hard · #3 (q01171)

> The researcher's methodology involved collecting data across twelve months rather than the standard six _______ a decision that significantly increased the study's cost and duration but also produced findings of considerably greater reliability.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text so that it conforms to the conventions of Standard Written English?

- A) ,
- B) —
- C) ;
- D) :

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (medium)
- Also defensible: **A** — A comma can introduce a summative appositive noun phrase ('..., a decision that surprised everyone'), so the comma is also standard here.
- Also defensible: **D** — A colon may follow an independent clause to introduce an explanatory noun phrase, so 'six: a decision that...' is also acceptable.
- multiple_correct_answers: The dash (B) is the intended answer, but the preceding words form an independent clause and the following material is a summative appositive noun phrase, which can legitimately be introduced by a comma (A) or a colon (D). Only the semicolon (C) is clearly wrong, so the item has more than one defensible answer.

### R&W · Boundaries · hard · #7 (q01175)

> Critics who dismissed the painting as derivative overlooked the ways in which the artist had subtly subverted the conventions she appeared to be following _______ her use of color, for instance, was a deliberate inversion of the palette her apparent models had employed.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text so that it conforms to the conventions of Standard Written English?

- A) ,
- B) — since
- C) ;
- D) , and so

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (high)
- multiple_correct_answers: Strictly on punctuation grounds, choices B ('— since') and D (', and so') also create conventionally acceptable boundaries (a dash before a subordinate clause; a comma before the coordinating conjunction 'and'). They are only ruled out by logic — 'since' (cause) and 'and so' (result) clash with the illustrative 'for instance' — rather than by Standard English conventions, which is the only criterion the question states. The semicolon (C) remains the clearly intended and best answer, but the distractors are weaker than typical SAT items.

### R&W · Boundaries · hard · #9 (q01177)

> The historian's account of the period, drawing on recently declassified documents, challenges the received narrative in two significant respects _______ first, it revises the timeline of key decisions; second, it attributes primary responsibility to actors who had previously been treated as peripheral figures.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text so that it conforms to the conventions of Standard Written English?

- A) ,
- B) ;
- C) :
- D) — but

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (high)
- Also defensible: **B** — A semicolon can legitimately join independent clauses, and 'The historian's account ... challenges the received narrative in two significant respects; first, it revises ...; second, it attributes ...' is grammatically conventional, though the colon is better because the material that follows elaborates on 'two significant respects.'
- multiple_correct_answers: Both the colon (C) and the semicolon (B) produce punctuation that conforms to Standard English here, since what follows the blank begins with an independent clause. The colon is the stronger choice (it introduces an enumeration/elaboration of 'two significant respects'), but a semicolon between independent clauses is not an error, so the item lacks the single-correct-answer cleanliness College Board items usually maintain.

### R&W · Boundaries · hard · #12 (q01180)

> Marine biologists studying bioluminescence in deep-sea organisms have long been puzzled by the extraordinary diversity of light-producing mechanisms found across unrelated species. Some organisms produce light through symbiotic relationships with luminescent bacteria, others generate it through self-contained chemical reactions involving proteins called luciferases. This apparent redundancy — the independent evolution of bioluminescence in dozens of separate lineages — has led researchers to conclude that light production confers such significant survival advantages in the deep ocean that natural selection has arrived at similar solutions through entirely different biochemical pathways.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text with the most logical and grammatically correct use of sentence boundaries?

- A) Some organisms produce light through symbiotic relationships with luminescent bacteria; others generate it through self-contained chemical reactions involving proteins called luciferases.
- B) Some organisms produce light through symbiotic relationships with luminescent bacteria, others generate it through self-contained chemical reactions involving proteins called luciferases.
- C) Some organisms produce light through symbiotic relationships with luminescent bacteria: others generate it through self-contained chemical reactions involving proteins called luciferases.
- D) Some organisms produce light through symbiotic relationships with luminescent bacteria — others generate it through self-contained chemical reactions involving proteins called luciferases.

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (high)
- Also defensible: **D** — A single em dash can grammatically join two independent clauses, and the second clause here amplifies/contrasts with the first, so a strict grammarian could accept it; however, the balanced contrast makes the semicolon clearly the best choice.
- formatting: The passage already contains the sentence in question (in the comma-splice form matching choice B) rather than presenting a blank or underlined portion, so the prompt 'completes the text' does not match the stimulus.
- multiple_correct_answers: Choices A and D are both defensible punctuation for joining two independent clauses (semicolon and em dash), which weakens the single-best-answer design; real SAT items avoid pairing these two options.

### R&W · Boundaries · hard · #14 (q01182)

> The novelist Toni Morrison once observed that the purpose of racist ideology is not simply to oppress but to distract — to keep both the oppressor and the oppressed so consumed with the mechanics of race that neither can attend fully to questions of class, justice, or shared humanity. This insight, which Morrison developed across both her fiction and her critical essays, positioned race not as a natural category but as a socially constructed instrument, one maintained through cultural narratives, legal structures, and economic arrangements that serve particular interests.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text with the most logical and grammatically correct use of sentence boundaries?

- A) This insight, which Morrison developed across both her fiction and her critical essays, positioned race not as a natural category but as a socially constructed instrument: one maintained through cultural narratives, legal structures, and economic arrangements that serve particular interests.
- B) This insight which Morrison developed across both her fiction and her critical essays positioned race not as a natural category but as a socially constructed instrument, one maintained through cultural narratives, legal structures, and economic arrangements that serve particular interests.
- C) This insight, which Morrison developed across both her fiction and her critical essays, positioned race not as a natural category but as a socially constructed instrument, one maintained through cultural narratives, legal structures, and economic arrangements that serve particular interests.
- D) This insight — which Morrison developed across both her fiction and her critical essays — positioned race not as a natural category, but as a socially constructed instrument, one maintained through cultural narratives, legal structures, and economic arrangements that serve particular interests.

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (medium)
- Also defensible: **A** — A colon may legitimately follow an independent clause to introduce an appositive/elaboration ('...instrument: one maintained through...'), so A is also grammatically correct; only convention/preference for the comma-appositive makes C 'best.'
- formatting: The passage itself reproduces the full sentence (identical to choice C) rather than presenting a blank or an underlined portion, so the stimulus gives away the intended answer and the item has no actual blank to complete.
- multiple_correct_answers: Both A (colon introducing an appositive after an independent clause) and C (comma-appositive) are grammatically acceptable and logical; nothing in the sentence distinguishes them, so two answers are defensible.

### R&W · Boundaries · hard · #15 (q01183)

> Cognitive scientists have increasingly challenged the classical model of memory, which depicted the brain as a kind of recording device that stored experiences as stable, retrievable files. In reality, memory is reconstructive rather than reproductive — each act of recall involves actively rebuilding a past experience from fragmentary traces, influenced by subsequent knowledge, current emotional state, and the expectations of the remembering self. This means that two people who witness the same event may later produce radically different, and equally sincere, accounts of what occurred.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text with the most logical and grammatically correct use of sentence boundaries?

- A) In reality, memory is reconstructive rather than reproductive, each act of recall involves actively rebuilding a past experience from fragmentary traces, influenced by subsequent knowledge, current emotional state, and the expectations of the remembering self.
- B) In reality, memory is reconstructive rather than reproductive: each act of recall involves actively rebuilding a past experience from fragmentary traces, influenced by subsequent knowledge, current emotional state, and the expectations of the remembering self.
- C) In reality, memory is reconstructive rather than reproductive — each act of recall involves actively rebuilding a past experience from fragmentary traces, influenced by subsequent knowledge, current emotional state, and the expectations of the remembering self.
- D) In reality, memory is reconstructive rather than reproductive; each act of recall involves actively rebuilding a past experience from fragmentary traces influenced by subsequent knowledge, current emotional state, and the expectations of the remembering self.

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (medium)
- Also defensible: **B** — A colon after an independent clause correctly introduces an elaboration; grammatically and logically flawless.
- Also defensible: **D** — A semicolon correctly joins two independent clauses; the only difference is the omitted comma before 'influenced,' which makes the modifier restrictive but remains grammatical.
- multiple_correct_answers: Only A (a comma splice) is clearly wrong. Choices B (colon), C (em dash), and D (semicolon) all correctly separate two independent clauses and all convey the same elaborative relationship, so there is no single best answer.
- formatting: The passage is presented complete, with the em-dash version (choice C) already embedded, rather than containing a blank or underlined portion to be replaced. This both makes the task unclear and telegraphs the intended key.

### R&W · Boundaries · hard · #16 (q01184)

> Economists distinguish between two types of unemployment: frictional, which occurs when workers are temporarily between jobs as they search for better opportunities, and structural, which results from a fundamental mismatch between the skills workers possess and the skills employers require. Frictional unemployment is generally considered benign, even healthy, it reflects a dynamic labor market in which individuals are actively seeking more suitable positions. Structural unemployment, by contrast, poses a far more serious policy challenge, as it cannot be resolved simply by waiting for the economy to recover.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text with the most logical and grammatically correct use of sentence boundaries?

- A) Frictional unemployment is generally considered benign, even healthy; it reflects a dynamic labor market in which individuals are actively seeking more suitable positions.
- B) Frictional unemployment is generally considered benign, even healthy: it reflects a dynamic labor market in which individuals are actively seeking more suitable positions.
- C) Frictional unemployment is generally considered benign — even healthy, it reflects a dynamic labor market in which individuals are actively seeking more suitable positions.
- D) Frictional unemployment is generally considered benign, even healthy, it reflects a dynamic labor market in which individuals are actively seeking more suitable positions.

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (high)
- Also defensible: **B** — A colon after the independent clause 'Frictional unemployment is generally considered benign, even healthy' is also grammatically correct, and the second clause explains/elaborates on the first, which is precisely the function a colon serves. Nothing distinguishes it as wrong.
- multiple_correct_answers: Both A (semicolon joining two independent clauses) and B (colon introducing an explanatory independent clause after a complete clause) are grammatically correct and logical. Authentic SAT Boundaries items include only one punctuation option that works; here the colon choice is equally defensible, so the item has two correct answers.

### R&W · Boundaries · hard · #18 (q01186)

> Scholars of Renaissance art have long debated the extent to which patrons constrained or liberated the artists they commissioned. On one view, the elaborate contracts specifying subject matter, pigment quality, and compositional requirements reduced painters to skilled craftsmen executing a client's vision. On another view, however, these constraints paradoxically freed artists, by clearly delimiting what was required, the contracts left implicit permission for innovation in everything they did not specify, including gesture, light, and psychological depth.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text with the most logical and grammatically correct use of sentence boundaries?

- A) On another view, however, these constraints paradoxically freed artists; by clearly delimiting what was required, the contracts left implicit permission for innovation in everything they did not specify, including gesture, light, and psychological depth.
- B) On another view, however, these constraints paradoxically freed artists, by clearly delimiting what was required, the contracts left implicit permission for innovation in everything they did not specify, including gesture, light, and psychological depth.
- C) On another view, however, these constraints paradoxically freed artists: by clearly delimiting what was required, the contracts left implicit permission for innovation in everything they did not specify, including gesture, light, and psychological depth.
- D) On another view, however, these constraints paradoxically freed artists — by clearly delimiting what was required, the contracts left implicit permission for innovation in everything they did not specify, including gesture, light, and psychological depth.

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (high)
- Also defensible: **A** — A semicolon correctly joins two independent clauses, so this version is fully grammatical and logical.
- Also defensible: **D** — An em dash can also legitimately link an independent clause to a following elaborating independent clause, so this version is grammatically acceptable as well.
- multiple_correct_answers: Three of the four options (A semicolon, C colon, D em dash) all correctly separate two independent clauses and all convey the elaborative relationship; only B (comma splice) is clearly wrong. A real SAT Boundaries item would include only one punctuation mark that works.

### R&W · Boundaries · hard · #19 (q01187)

> The discovery of the Higgs boson in 2012 confirmed a central prediction of the Standard Model of particle physics, which had remained theoretically complete but observationally incomplete for nearly half a century. Physicists celebrated the finding as a triumph, some cautioned, however, that the boson's measured mass created significant theoretical difficulties — it appeared far lighter than the equations of supersymmetry predicted it should be, a discrepancy that pointed to gaps in even the most successful framework in the history of physics.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text with the most logical and grammatically correct use of sentence boundaries?

- A) Physicists celebrated the finding as a triumph; some cautioned, however, that the boson's measured mass created significant theoretical difficulties — it appeared far lighter than the equations of supersymmetry predicted it should be, a discrepancy that pointed to gaps in even the most successful framework in the history of physics.
- B) Physicists celebrated the finding as a triumph, some cautioned, however, that the boson's measured mass created significant theoretical difficulties — it appeared far lighter than the equations of supersymmetry predicted it should be, a discrepancy that pointed to gaps in even the most successful framework in the history of physics.
- C) Physicists celebrated the finding as a triumph: some cautioned, however, that the boson's measured mass created significant theoretical difficulties — it appeared far lighter than the equations of supersymmetry predicted it should be, a discrepancy that pointed to gaps in even the most successful framework in the history of physics.
- D) Physicists celebrated the finding as a triumph — some cautioned, however, that the boson's measured mass created significant theoretical difficulties; it appeared far lighter than the equations of supersymmetry predicted it should be, a discrepancy that pointed to gaps in even the most successful framework in the history of physics.

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (medium)
- Also defensible: **D** — Punctuation in D is also technically correct: an em dash can legitimately join the two independent clauses ('triumph — some cautioned...'), and a semicolon correctly links 'difficulties' to the independent clause 'it appeared far lighter...'. A is preferable only on stylistic grounds (the dash better signals the explanatory elaboration), not on strict grammatical grounds.
- multiple_correct_answers: Choice D contains no boundary error: a dash may join independent clauses and a semicolon may link the explanatory clause. The item therefore has two grammatically defensible options, with A superior only stylistically.

### R&W · Boundaries · hard · #23 (q01191)

> The novelist spent years researching the historical milieu of her subject, immersing herself in period correspondence, municipal records, and the private diaries of figures who had known her protagonist's real-life counterpart. The resulting work bore the unmistakable weight of that labor: every incidental detail, from the texture of a merchant's ledger to the cadence of a street vendor's cry, felt authenticated by genuine archival intimacy.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text so that it conforms to the conventions of Standard English?

- A) labor, every incidental detail — from the texture of a merchant's ledger to the cadence of a street vendor's cry — felt authenticated by genuine archival intimacy.
- B) labor: every incidental detail, from the texture of a merchant's ledger to the cadence of a street vendor's cry, felt authenticated by genuine archival intimacy.
- C) labor; every incidental detail, from the texture of a merchant's ledger to the cadence of a street vendor's cry felt authenticated by genuine archival intimacy.
- D) labor — every incidental detail, from the texture of a merchant's ledger to the cadence of a street vendor's cry, felt authenticated by genuine archival intimacy.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- Also defensible: **D** — An em dash, like a colon, can legitimately join an independent clause to a following independent clause that elaborates on it; the parenthetical 'from the texture... cry' is correctly set off by a matched pair of commas. D is therefore also fully conventional, making it equally correct.
- formatting: The passage is printed in completed form (the second sentence already uses the colon punctuation of choice B) instead of containing a blank or an underlined portion, so the item gives away the intended answer and presents no actual task.
- multiple_correct_answers: Both B (colon + paired commas) and D (em dash + paired commas) are punctuated correctly by SAT conventions; only A (comma splice) and C (missing the second comma of the parenthetical) are defective.

### R&W · Boundaries · hard · #25 (q01193)

> Critics of the behavioral sink experiments conducted by ethologist John Calhoun in the 1960s have raised methodological objections that are difficult to dismiss: the artificial conditions of his rodent colonies, including extreme crowding and unlimited food, created stressors so atypical that extrapolating from them to human urban density requires extraordinary caution. Nevertheless, the experiments captured the popular imagination and continue to shape public discourse about the psychological effects of overpopulation.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text so that it conforms to the conventions of Standard English?

- A) dismiss, the artificial conditions of his rodent colonies — including extreme crowding and unlimited food — created stressors so atypical that extrapolating from them to human urban density requires extraordinary caution.
- B) dismiss: the artificial conditions of his rodent colonies, including extreme crowding and unlimited food, created stressors so atypical that extrapolating from them to human urban density requires extraordinary caution.
- C) dismiss; the artificial conditions of his rodent colonies, including extreme crowding and unlimited food, created stressors so atypical, that extrapolating from them to human urban density requires extraordinary caution.
- D) dismiss — the artificial conditions of his rodent colonies, including extreme crowding and unlimited food, created stressors so atypical that extrapolating from them to human urban density requires extraordinary caution.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- Also defensible: **D** — An em dash can join an independent clause to an elaborating independent clause just as a colon can; D is punctuated correctly throughout, so it is also grammatically defensible.
- formatting: The passage is printed with the sentence already complete (it contains the colon version verbatim) and no blank or underlined portion, so there is nothing for the student to complete; the stimulus also gives away the intended answer.
- multiple_correct_answers: Both B (colon) and D (em dash) correctly link the independent clause to the elaborating independent clause and are otherwise identically punctuated, so two choices satisfy Standard English conventions.

### R&W · Boundaries · hard · #26 (q01194)

> The paleoclimatological record preserved in Antarctic ice cores reveals that atmospheric carbon dioxide concentrations have fluctuated dramatically over the past 800,000 years, cycling between roughly 180 parts per million during glacial maxima and approximately 280 parts per million during interglacial periods. The current concentration, which now exceeds 420 parts per million, thus represents a departure from natural variability so extreme that it has no precedent in the entire ice-core record.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text so that it conforms to the conventions of Standard English?

- A) periods, the current concentration, which now exceeds 420 parts per million, thus represents
- B) periods; the current concentration — which now exceeds 420 parts per million — thus represents
- C) periods. The current concentration, which now exceeds 420 parts per million, thus represents
- D) periods: the current concentration, which now exceeds 420 parts per million, thus represents

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (medium)
- Also defensible: **B** — A semicolon correctly joins two independent clauses, and the paired em dashes correctly set off the nonessential relative clause, so this version is fully conventional as well.
- Also defensible: **D** — A colon after an independent clause may introduce a second clause that explains or amplifies it, which the 'thus' clause arguably does, making this punctuation defensible.
- formatting: The passage is presented already completed (with the period version), with no blank, underline, or bracketed gap indicating where the choice goes. This both breaks the standard item format and telegraphs the intended answer (C).
- multiple_correct_answers: Choices B, C, and D are all punctuated acceptably under Standard English conventions: semicolon + em dashes, period, and (defensibly) colon introducing an amplifying independent clause. Only A, the comma splice, is clearly wrong, so the item lacks a single best answer.

### R&W · Boundaries · hard · #28 (q01196)

> Scholars of Renaissance patronage have observed that the relationship between artists and their patrons was far more collaborative — and more contentious — than the conventional narrative of dependent genius allows. Patrons did not merely fund the work; they negotiated its subject matter, its dimensions, its iconographic program, and sometimes even its stylistic register, exercising a degree of curatorial authority that modern audiences are ill-equipped to appreciate.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text so that it conforms to the conventions of Standard English?

- A) Patrons did not merely fund the work, they negotiated its subject matter, its dimensions, its iconographic program, and sometimes even its stylistic register
- B) Patrons did not merely fund the work; they negotiated its subject matter, its dimensions, its iconographic program, and sometimes even its stylistic register
- C) Patrons did not merely fund the work — they negotiated its subject matter, its dimensions, its iconographic program, and sometimes even its stylistic register
- D) Patrons did not merely fund the work: they negotiated its subject matter, its dimensions, its iconographic program, and sometimes even its stylistic register

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- Also defensible: **C** — A dash can correctly join two independent clauses, so this version is also conventionally acceptable.
- Also defensible: **D** — A colon is acceptable before an independent clause that elaborates on the first, so this version is also grammatically correct.
- multiple_correct_answers: Choices B, C, and D all conform to Standard English conventions (semicolon, dash, and colon can each link these two independent clauses). Only A (a comma splice) is clearly wrong, so the item has three defensible answers.
- formatting: The passage is printed in full with the correct punctuation already in place and contains no blank or underlined portion, so the answer is given away by the stimulus.

### R&W · Form, Structure, and Sense · easy · #4 (q01202)

> The company announced that it _______ expanding its operations to three new countries by the end of the fiscal year.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text so that it conforms to the conventions of Standard Written English?

- A) is
- B) were
- C) will have been
- D) had been

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (medium)
- Also defensible: **C** — "will have been expanding ... by the end of the fiscal year" is grammatically well-formed future perfect progressive and collocates naturally with the deadline phrase "by the end of the fiscal year," so a reviewer could accept it, though it is semantically awkward and the simpler present progressive is the intended key.
- multiple_correct_answers: Choice C is not clearly ungrammatical; the future perfect progressive is standard with "by the end of..." time phrases, which weakens the uniqueness of A. Also, strict sequence of tenses after past-tense "announced" would favor "was expanding" or "would be expanding," neither of which is offered, making A slightly imperfect as well.

### R&W · Form, Structure, and Sense · easy · #14 (q01212)

> The new library downtown has an impressive collection of audiobooks, graphic novels, and magazines. Visitors _____ a library card for free by presenting a valid form of identification and proof of their current address. The staff encourages everyone in the community to take advantage of the many free resources the library offers throughout the year.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text so that it conforms to the conventions of Standard English?

- A) can obtain
- B) obtaining
- C) have obtained
- D) obtains

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (high)
- multiple_correct_answers: Distractor C ('have obtained') is grammatically well-formed and agrees with the plural subject 'Visitors'; it is rejected only on contextual/tense grounds (the passage otherwise uses the simple present for general facts), not on a clear violation of Standard English conventions. A tighter item would use a distractor that is unambiguously ungrammatical (e.g., 'has obtained'). A remains the clearly best answer.

### R&W · Form, Structure, and Sense · medium-low · #27 (q01255)

> The ancient Roman engineer Vitruvius outlined three fundamental principles for architecture in his treatise De Architectura: firmitas (strength), utilitas (usefulness), and venustas (beauty). According to Vitruvius, a building that _____ all three qualities would stand the test of time, serving both practical needs and aesthetic ideals. His framework has continued to shape architectural theory for more than two thousand years, influencing designers from the Renaissance through the modern era.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text so that it conforms to the conventions of Standard English?

- A) embody
- B) embodies
- C) embodied
- D) has embodied

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (medium)
- Also defensible: **C** — "a building that embodied all three qualities would stand the test of time" is fully grammatical as backshifted/hypothetical reported speech, and it actually pairs more idiomatically with the conditional "would stand" than the present tense does.
- multiple_correct_answers: The item appears to test subject-verb agreement (singular "a building" \u2192 "embodies"), but choice C ("embodied") is also grammatically correct and idiomatic with the following conditional "would stand," since the sentence reports Vitruvius's claim in the past. A well-constructed SAT item would eliminate the past-tense option (e.g., by using "will stand" or distractors that are unambiguously nonstandard).

### R&W · Form, Structure, and Sense · medium-low · #30 (q01258)

> Economists distinguish between "positive" economics, which describes how economies actually function, and "normative" economics, which prescribes how they _____ function. A positive statement, such as "raising the minimum wage increases unemployment among low-skilled workers," can in principle be tested with data, even if the evidence remains contested. A normative statement, such as "the government should guarantee a living wage," reflects value judgments that data alone cannot resolve.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text so that it conforms to the conventions of Standard English?

- A) should
- B) will
- C) can
- D) would have

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (high)
- unclear_or_ambiguous: The stem asks which choice 'conforms to the conventions of Standard English,' but the selection is semantic rather than grammatical: 'should,' 'will,' and 'can' are all grammatically well formed in the blank (only 'would have' is ungrammatical). The intended answer 'should' is determined by logic/meaning (the definition of normative economics as prescriptive), so the item should be framed as a word-choice/logical-completion question (e.g., 'most logical and precise word or phrase').

### R&W · Form, Structure, and Sense · medium-high · #3 (q01261)

> The administration's decision to reclassify the wetlands as developable land has been criticized by environmental groups, several conservation scientists, and _______ who argues that the move violates federal protections established in 1972.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text so that it conforms to the conventions of Standard Written English?

- A) a legal scholar
- B) a legal scholar, one
- C) legal scholars
- D) one legal scholar

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (medium)
- Also defensible: **D** — "one legal scholar who argues..." is equally grammatical: a singular noun phrase agreeing with the singular verb "argues" in the relative clause.
- Also defensible: **B** — "a legal scholar, one who argues..." is also well-formed, using an appositive pronoun "one" as the head of the restrictive relative clause; wordier but not a conventions error.
- multiple_correct_answers: The item appears to test subject-verb agreement in the relative clause ("who argues"), which eliminates only C ("legal scholars who argues"). Choices A, D, and B are all grammatically acceptable completions, so there is no single defensible answer under Standard Written English conventions.
- unclear_or_ambiguous: Because three options are grammatical, the distinction among them comes down to style/concision rather than conventions, which is not a tested SAT skill in this question type.

### R&W · Form, Structure, and Sense · medium-high · #9 (q01267)

> The delegation arrived at the summit having _______ for months — reviewing position papers, consulting stakeholders, and running simulations of the most likely negotiating scenarios.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text so that it conforms to the conventions of Standard Written English?

- A) prepared
- B) been preparing
- C) prepare
- D) to prepare

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (low)
- Also defensible: **A** — "having prepared for months" is also fully grammatical Standard English; the perfect participle 'having + past participle' is the standard construction, so A is equally acceptable.
- multiple_correct_answers: Both A ("having prepared for months") and B ("having been preparing for months") conform to the conventions of Standard Written English. The duration phrase 'for months' and the ongoing activities slightly favor the perfect progressive, but the simple perfect participle is not an error, so the item lacks a single defensible key.

### R&W · Form, Structure, and Sense · medium-high · #15 (q01273)

> The city council announced that the new transit corridor, which will connect the downtown hub to three underserved neighborhoods, is expected to reduce average commute times by nearly thirty percent. Advocates for the project insist that the corridor, together with planned bike lane expansions, make the city's transportation network significantly more equitable and sustainable for residents across all income levels.

**Q:** Which choice best corrects the underlined verb in the sentence?

- A) making the city's transportation network
- B) makes the city's transportation network
- C) make the city's transportation network
- D) have made the city's transportation network

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: No underlining or other marking appears in the passage, so the student must infer that the targeted verb is 'make'. The phrase is recoverable from the choices, but the prompt's reference to an 'underlined verb' is unsupported by the text as presented.
- unclear_or_ambiguous: Minor: 'insist that' can introduce the mandative subjunctive ('insist that the corridor make...'), which would make C grammatical under a directive reading. The context (a factual claim about benefits, with 'is expected to' earlier) clearly calls for the indicative, and the prompt stipulates the verb needs correcting, so B remains the single best answer.

### R&W · Form, Structure, and Sense · medium-high · #18 (q01276)

> The success of the reintroduction program, measured by the number of breeding pairs established in the restored wetland, hinges on whether officials will continue to restrict agricultural runoff upstream. Without these protections, the carefully balanced chemical environment that supports amphibian reproduction — including the precise ratios of dissolved oxygen and pH levels — deteriorates rapidly, rendering decades of conservation effort meaningless.

**Q:** The student wants to replace the underlined portion with the most grammatically precise alternative. Which choice best accomplishes this?

- A) deteriorate rapidly, rendering
- B) deteriorating rapidly, rendering
- C) deteriorates rapidly, rendering
- D) had deteriorated rapidly, rendering

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (high)
- formatting: No portion of the passage is actually underlined or otherwise marked, so students must infer which phrase is being tested. The intended target ('deteriorates rapidly, rendering') can only be deduced from the answer choices.
- other: The correct answer (C) is verbatim identical to the text already in the passage, which conflicts with the instruction to 'replace' the underlined portion; standard SAT items present the choice as 'NO CHANGE' or omit the phrasing from the passage.
