- unclear_or_ambiguous: 'Most grammatically precise' is nonstandard SAT phrasing; the actual test is simple subject-verb agreement (singular subject 'environment' with an intervening appositive), which is closer to medium than medium-high difficulty.

### R&W · Form, Structure, and Sense · medium-high · #23 (q01281)

> Researchers studying the migration patterns of Arctic terns have documented one of the most extraordinary journeys in the animal kingdom. Each year, these small seabirds travel from their Arctic breeding grounds to the Antarctic and back, a round trip that can exceed seventy thousand kilometers. Because they follow the sun, Arctic terns experience more daylight hours annually than any other creature on Earth. The data gathered by scientists, who attached lightweight tracking devices to the birds, _______ confirmed that individual terns make this journey for up to thirty consecutive years.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text so that it conforms to the conventions of Standard English?

- A) have
- B) has
- C) having
- D) to have

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** A (low)
- Also defensible: **B** — "Data" is widely accepted in contemporary Standard English as a singular mass noun, so "The data ... has confirmed" is also grammatically defensible; the only other agreement cue in the sentence ("scientists") is itself plural, so nothing in the text settles the number of "data."
- multiple_correct_answers: The item hinges solely on whether "data" takes a plural or singular verb. Both the traditional plural ("the data have confirmed," standard in scientific writing) and the now widely accepted singular mass-noun use ("the data has confirmed") conform to Standard English, so A and B are both defensible. SAT conventions items normally avoid subjects whose grammatical number is disputed.
- unclear_or_ambiguous: Choices C ("having") and D ("to have") are easily eliminated as non-finite, leaving a 50/50 decision that depends on a contested usage point rather than a testable convention.

### R&W · Form, Structure, and Sense · medium-high · #27 (q01285)

> The concept of terroir, borrowed from French viticulture, holds that the taste of wine is shaped not only by the grape variety and winemaking technique but also by the specific environment in which the grapes _______ — the soil composition, topography, climate, and even the microorganisms present in the vineyard. Proponents argue that wines produced in a given region carry an irreducible geographical identity that cannot be replicated elsewhere, no matter how closely a winemaker mimics the methods of the original producer.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text so that it conforms to the conventions of Standard English?

- A) are grown
- B) were grown
- C) grow
- D) had grown

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (medium)
- Also defensible: **C** — "grow" is also present tense and grammatically correct here; "the specific environment in which the grapes grow" is idiomatic and consistent with the passage's present-tense framing, so it is equally acceptable.
- multiple_correct_answers: Both A ("are grown") and C ("grow") are present-tense, grammatically sound completions that fit the passage's present-tense discussion of terroir. The only distinction is active vs. passive voice, which is a stylistic, not a conventions, matter. A valid SAT verb-form item would leave only one option that is both tense-appropriate and grammatically correct.
- unclear_or_ambiguous: The question asks only about conformity to Standard English conventions, but no option is ungrammatical in isolation; B and D are merely contextually odd in tense, making the intended discrimination unclear.

### R&W · Form, Structure, and Sense · hard · #3 (q01291)

> The novelist's prose style has evolved considerably since her debut: whereas her early work _______ fragmented sentences and a deliberately sparse vocabulary, her recent novels favor long, sinuous constructions that embed subordinate clauses within subordinate clauses.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text so that it conforms to the conventions of Standard Written English?

- A) relied on
- B) relies on
- C) had relied on
- D) was relying on

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (medium)
- Also defensible: **B** — Using the literary present ("her early work relies on fragmented sentences") is also idiomatic and parallels the present-tense "her recent novels favor," since the early work itself still exhibits those features.
- multiple_correct_answers: Both the simple past (A) and the literary present (B) are grammatically acceptable here; the second clause's present-tense verb "favor" actually supports the present-tense reading, so the item does not isolate a single conventionally correct option as cleanly as an SAT item should. The past perfect (C) and past progressive (D) are clearly wrong, so the intended answer is A, but B is not unambiguously unacceptable.

### R&W · Form, Structure, and Sense · hard · #7 (q01295)

> The manuscript, which scholars had long assumed _______ lost during the fire that destroyed the monastery's library in 1742, was discovered last year in a private collection in Vienna.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text so that it conforms to the conventions of Standard Written English?

- A) to have been
- B) had been
- C) was
- D) to be

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (medium)
- Also defensible: **B** — "which scholars had long assumed had been lost" is fully grammatical: the relative pronoun serves as subject of the embedded clause after 'assumed' (with 'that' omitted), and the past perfect correctly situates the loss before the assumption.
- Also defensible: **C** — "which scholars had long assumed was lost" is likewise standard, with the simple past in the embedded clause referring to the 1742 event.
- multiple_correct_answers: Three options yield grammatical Standard English sentences. Besides the apparently intended A (object-raising: 'assumed the manuscript to have been lost'), both B ('had assumed had been lost') and C ('had assumed was lost') are acceptable reduced that-clauses in which 'which' is the subject of the embedded verb. Even D is arguably defensible, differing only in nuance of time reference. No single choice is uniquely correct.
- unclear_or_ambiguous: The item gives no basis (e.g., parallelism, subject-verb agreement, or a clear tense-sequencing violation) for preferring one acceptable form over the others, so the task of selecting a 'best' answer is not well defined.

### R&W · Form, Structure, and Sense · hard · #13 (q01301)

> The mycorrhizal network — a vast subterranean web of fungal filaments connecting the root systems of neighboring trees — facilitates the transfer of carbon, nitrogen, and water between plants in ways that challenge conventional definitions of individuality in biology. A single mature Douglas fir may be linked to hundreds of other trees of varying ages and species, effectively functioning as both a recipient and a distributor of chemical resources. Whether this mutualistic exchange constitutes a form of cooperation in any meaningful sense, or whether _______ it merely reflects the mechanistic outcome of evolutionary pressures favoring resource-sharing traits, remains a subject of active debate among ecologists and philosophers of biology.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text so that it conforms to the conventions of Standard English?

- A) it merely reflects the mechanistic outcome
- B) merely reflecting the mechanistic outcome
- C) the mechanistic outcome is merely reflected
- D) merely the mechanistic outcome reflecting

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (high)
- formatting: The passage text immediately after the blank already reads "it merely reflects the mechanistic outcome of evolutionary pressures..." — i.e., the intended answer is printed in the passage. With the blank present, inserting any choice produces a garbled, redundant sentence. The passage should end at "or whether _______ of evolutionary pressures favoring resource-sharing traits, remains a subject of active debate."
- unclear_or_ambiguous: Because the completed clause is duplicated in the passage, a student cannot tell what portion of the sentence the blank is meant to supply, undermining the parallel-structure/clause test being assessed.

### R&W · Form, Structure, and Sense · hard · #17 (q01305)

> Among the most consequential — yet least examined — transformations in post-war urban planning was the deliberate construction of what sociologists now call "infrastructural segregation": the routing of highways, industrial corridors, and waste facilities through predominantly Black and low-income neighborhoods in ways that simultaneously displaced residents and concentrated environmental harm. Robert Moses, whose influence over New York's physical landscape was almost without parallel, is perhaps the most studied architect of these patterns, though similar dynamics _______ in virtually every major American city during the 1950s and 1960s, suggesting that Moses's methods reflected a systemic logic rather than individual malfeasance.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text so that it conforms to the conventions of Standard English?

- A) were unfolding
- B) had unfolded
- C) unfolded
- D) would unfold

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (medium)
- Also defensible: **A** — The past progressive 'were unfolding' is also grammatically correct and idiomatic with the durational phrase 'during the 1950s and 1960s'; nothing in the passage rules it out.
- multiple_correct_answers: The item asks only about 'conventions of Standard English,' but both C ('unfolded') and A ('were unfolding') are conventionally correct with the plural subject and the time marker 'during the 1950s and 1960s.' The distinction between them is stylistic/aspectual rather than a matter of grammatical convention, so the item lacks a single defensible answer under its own stated criterion.
- unclear_or_ambiguous: No context clue (e.g., an earlier past-perfect anchor or a future reference point) is supplied to force one tense/aspect over another, which is what genuine SAT verb-tense items provide.

### R&W · Form, Structure, and Sense · hard · #18 (q01306)

> In the study of collective memory, scholars have long distinguished between "communicative memory" — the living recollections shared among people who experienced events firsthand, typically spanning two to three generations — and "cultural memory," which is institutionalized through monuments, archives, rituals, and canonical texts. The transition between these two modes is rarely smooth; as eyewitnesses die and lived experience gives way to mediated representation, communities must negotiate which versions of the past _______ preserved, and in whose interests those versions are constructed and transmitted to future generations.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text so that it conforms to the conventions of Standard English?

- A) are being
- B) get
- C) will be
- D) are

**Key:** D · **Reviewer:** D (low)
- Also defensible: **C** — "which versions of the past will be preserved" is fully grammatical and arguably fits the forward-looking sense ('transmitted to future generations').
- Also defensible: **A** — "are being preserved" is also a grammatical finite verb phrase in this clause.
- Also defensible: **B** — "get preserved" is grammatical (get-passive), though informal in register; conventions questions do not normally penalize register.
- multiple_correct_answers: All four options produce grammatically correct sentences; none violates subject-verb agreement, tense sequence requirements, or any other rule of Standard English. The item lacks a single defensible correct answer, so it tests stylistic preference (parallelism with 'are constructed and transmitted') rather than a convention.
- unclear_or_ambiguous: The intended convention under test is not identifiable; students must guess whether tense parallelism, aspect, or register is being assessed.

### R&W · Form, Structure, and Sense · hard · #25 (q01313)

> The emergence of the English sonnet in the sixteenth century owed much to the work of translators and imitators who adapted the Italian Petrarchan form to the different rhythmic demands of the English language. Where Italian's abundance of vowel-final words made the hendecasyllable feel natural, English's prevalence of stressed monosyllables pushed poets toward the iambic pentameter line. The result was not a simple copy but a genuine formal innovation: a fourteen-line structure whose rhetorical architecture—the three quatrains building toward a concluding couplet—gave English poets a compact instrument for staging argument, paradox, and resolution.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text so that it conforms to the conventions of Standard English? The result was not a simple copy but a genuine formal innovation: a fourteen-line structure whose rhetorical architecture—the three quatrains building toward a concluding couplet—_______ English poets a compact instrument for staging argument, paradox, and resolution.

- A) giving
- B) to give
- C) gave
- D) gives

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (medium)
- Also defensible: **D** — "gives" is also a finite verb that supplies the needed predicate for the relative clause; since the sonnet form still exists, a generic present tense is not ungrammatical, so only tense consistency with the surrounding past-tense narration rules it out.
- multiple_correct_answers: The item appears to test finite vs. non-finite verb form (eliminating A and B), but it leaves two finite options, C ("gave") and D ("gives"). Nothing in the sentence itself makes the present tense ungrammatical; only the broader past-tense narrative favors "gave." SAT conventions items normally include just one finite verb option to avoid this ambiguity.

### R&W · Form, Structure, and Sense · hard · #28 (q01316)

> The historian's account of the French Revolution has been praised for its narrative drive and its sensitivity to contingency—the sense that events could have unfolded otherwise at nearly every turn. Less appreciated, though equally significant, is the methodological discipline underlying this narrative: the historian's refusal to project later outcomes back onto earlier moments, treating each actor as someone who _______ yet ignorant of what would follow, navigating choices whose consequences remained radically uncertain.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text so that it conforms to the conventions of Standard English?

- A) is
- B) was
- C) were
- D) being

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- Also defensible: **A** — "is" produces a grammatically well-formed clause and could be defended as a historical present; however, the surrounding past-referring language ("would follow," "remained") makes "was" clearly preferable.
- unclear_or_ambiguous: The blank is followed immediately by "yet ignorant" with no preceding predicate adjective (e.g., "purposeful yet ignorant"), so the intended sentence reads as the archaic "someone who was yet ignorant" (= still ignorant). The missing parallel element makes the test point (tense rather than agreement) harder to see than necessary.
- difficulty_mismatch: Despite the dense passage, the actual decision is a routine singular-subject/past-tense verb choice, which is easier than the stated 'hard' tier; the distractors 'were' and 'being' are trivially eliminable.

### Math · Algebra · hard · #1 (q01439)

**Q:** If x + y = 10 and xy = 21, what is the larger of the two values?

- A) 3
- B) 5
- C) 6
- D) 7

**Key:** D · **Reviewer:** D (high)
- difficulty_mismatch: Finding two numbers with sum 10 and product 21 (3 and 7) is a straightforward factoring/guess-and-check task; this is an easy-level item, not hard.
- unclear_or_ambiguous: 'the larger of the two values' is loose phrasing; it should say 'the larger of x and y' or 'the greater of the two numbers,' since 'values' has no clear antecedent.

### Math · Algebra · hard · #12 (q01450)

**Q:** A line has a slope of 2/3 and passes through the point (3, 4). What is the y-intercept of the line?

**Key:** 2 · **Reviewer:** 2 (high)
- difficulty_mismatch: This is a routine one-step slope-intercept computation (y = 4 - (2/3)(3) = 2), typical of an easy-tier item, not a hard one.
- unclear_or_ambiguous: Minor: 'y-intercept' could be read as a point (0, 2), but a student-produced response requires a number; the intended answer 2 is clear enough.

- difficulty_mismatch: Stated difficulty is 'hard,' but the task is a single substitution (4+8=12), which is a very easy item and tests no real algebraic reasoning.

- Also defensible: **A** — Without the table, any of the four percentages could be correct; A is equally defensible.
- Also defensible: **B** — Without the table, any of the four percentages could be correct; B is equally defensible.
- Also defensible: **D** — Without the table, any of the four percentages could be correct; D is equally defensible.
- formatting: The question refers to a 'Pet Preference by Gender' table, but no table (or any data) is included in the item. The item cannot be rendered or solved as presented.
- no_correct_answer: Because the underlying data are missing, none of the answer choices can be verified as correct; the answer is indeterminate.

- unclear_or_ambiguous: Strictly speaking, the sample gives an estimate of the population percentage; the phrasing 'what percent of voters support the measure' should be 'what is the best estimate of the percent of all voters.' This is a minor wording issue that does not change the intended answer (56).

- difficulty_mismatch: A single subtraction (50 \u2212 35) is well below even 'medium-low' SAT Data Analysis difficulty; real items at this tier typically require reading a two-way table or computing a proportion/percentage.

- unclear_or_ambiguous: The stem opens with "Using the same jar," referencing a prior item that is not shown; the parenthetical restates the needed information, so the item is still solvable, but the framing is not self-contained as SAT items should be.
- difficulty_mismatch: Setting red = 5k, green = 3k and solving 3k + 8 = 5k is a routine one-equation ratio problem, closer to easy/medium than the stated medium-high tier.

**Q:** Using the same scenario (revenue goes from $100,000 to $99,000 over two years), what is the overall percent decrease from the original amount?

- unclear_or_ambiguous: The stem refers to "the same scenario" from a previous (absent) question, so the item is not self-contained. Although the key numbers are parenthetically supplied, it is unclear whether the intended path involves intermediate yearly percent changes (e.g., +10% then -10%) that are not shown.
- difficulty_mismatch: Computing (100,000 - 99,000)/100,000 = 1% is a single-step percent-change calculation, far easier than the stated medium-high difficulty.

### Math · Data Analysis · medium-high · #29 (q01587)

**Q:** If the actual defect rate in the full batch of 1,000 parts is later found to be 5%, how many defective parts would be expected?

- difficulty_mismatch: Computing 5% of 1,000 is a single-step percent calculation, far easier than the stated medium-high difficulty; it would rank as an easy item.
- unclear_or_ambiguous: The question appears to be an orphaned follow-up ('the actual defect rate... is later found') that references context (a sample, survey, or earlier estimate) not provided. As written it is answerable only because the percent and total are both given, but the framing implies missing stimulus material.

### Math · Data Analysis · medium-high · #30 (q01588)

**Q:** A histogram of exam scores is skewed left (has a long tail toward lower values). Which statement is generally true?

- A) The mean is greater than the median
- B) The mean is less than the median
- C) The mean equals the median
- D) The mode is greater than both the mean and the median

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- Also defensible: **D** — For a left-skewed (negatively skewed) unimodal distribution the standard ordering is mean < median < mode, so the mode is indeed generally greater than both the mean and the median, making D also a true statement.
- multiple_correct_answers: Both B (mean < median) and D (mode > mean and median) are generally true for a left-skewed unimodal distribution, since the classic ordering is mean < median < mode. The item needs to eliminate D (e.g., by not referencing the mode or by noting the distribution may be multimodal).
- difficulty_mismatch: Recognizing that left skew pulls the mean below the median is a basic recall fact, closer to easy/medium than medium-high.

### Math · Data Analysis · hard · #15 (q01613)

**Q:** Using the same class of 40 students (12 averaged 85), how many students averaged 75?

**Key:** 28 · **Reviewer:** 28 (low)
- unclear_or_ambiguous: The item refers to "the same class" and "the same" data from a previous question or passage that is not provided. Without the original context (e.g., the overall class average or the full distribution of averages), the problem cannot be solved as written.
- no_correct_answer: No deterministic answer exists from the information given. Only if one assumes the class consists of exactly two groups (those averaging 85 and those averaging 75) does 40 − 12 = 28 follow, but that assumption is never stated.
- formatting: The question appears to be an orphaned follow-up; missing stimulus/table and missing statement of the class's overall average.
- difficulty_mismatch: If the intended solution is simply 40 − 12, the item is far easier than the stated 'hard' tier; as written it is unsolvable rather than hard.

### Math · Geometry · medium-low · #15 (q01693)

- formatting: Digital SAT student-produced response fields accept only numbers (integers, decimals, fractions); they cannot accept 'π'. Asking for an answer 'in terms of π' in a grid-in is inconsistent with SAT conventions — the item should be multiple choice or ask for a decimal approximation.

- unclear_or_ambiguous: The stem says 'Using the same right triangle,' referring to a prior question that is not provided. Although the needed measurements are restated parenthetically, the self-referential framing is confusing and nonstandard for a standalone item.
- difficulty_mismatch: Applying the 30-60-90 ratio with the hypotenuse and short leg already given is a straightforward one-step computation (or single Pythagorean step), which is easy rather than medium-high.

- difficulty_mismatch: A single Pythagorean-triple recall (5-12-13) is an easy-tier task, not a hard one.

### Math · Geometry · hard · #24 (q01782)

**Q:** Using the same triangle (sides 13, 14, 15, area 84), what is the length of the altitude drawn to the side of length 14?

**Key:** 12 · **Reviewer:** 12 (high)
- difficulty_mismatch: The task reduces to one step (84 = ½·14·h, so h = 12) since both the side lengths and the area are given. This is an easy-tier computation, not a hard one.
- unclear_or_ambiguous: The phrase 'Using the same triangle' refers to a previous item that is not shown; although the needed data (sides 13, 14, 15 and area 84) are restated, the stand-alone wording is awkward and assumes missing context.

### Math · Geometry · hard · #27 (q01785)

**Q:** Using the same circle of radius 6 (in which the hexagon is inscribed), what is the area of the circle, in terms of π?

**Key:** 36π · **Reviewer:** 36π (high)
- Also defensible: **36** — Since the Digital SAT student-produced response field cannot accept the symbol π, a student might be forced to enter only the coefficient 36, making this the only gradable entry.
- formatting: The question asks for an answer 'in terms of π' but is formatted as a student-produced response. Digital SAT fill-in answers must be numeric (integers, decimals, or fractions) and cannot include π, so 36π is not an enterable answer.
- unclear_or_ambiguous: The stem refers to 'the same circle of radius 6 (in which the hexagon is inscribed)' and 'the hexagon,' but no figure, prior part, or hexagon description is provided in this standalone item.
- difficulty_mismatch: Computing A = πr² = 36π from a directly given radius is a one-step recall task, far below the stated 'hard' tier; the hexagon reference is irrelevant to the computation.

### Math · Geometry · hard · #30 (q01788)

**Q:** Using the same cone (radius 6, height equal to radius), what is the height of the cone?

**Key:** 6 · **Reviewer:** 6 (high)
- difficulty_mismatch: The stated difficulty is 'hard,' but the item is trivial: the stem itself states the height equals the radius and the radius is 6, so the answer is read directly off the prompt with no reasoning, computation, or geometry knowledge required.
- unclear_or_ambiguous: The phrase 'Using the same cone' refers to a previous question/figure that is not provided, so the item is not self-contained; students cannot tell what context (if any) they are meant to carry over.
- other: The question is self-answering/circular — it supplies the exact information it asks for, which is not a valid SAT item format.

### Math · Geometry · hard · #33 (q01791)

**Q:** Using the same rectangular prism (dimensions 10 by 6 by 6), what is its total surface area?

**Key:** 312 · **Reviewer:** 312 (high)
- unclear_or_ambiguous: The stem says "Using the same rectangular prism," referring to a previous item that is not shown; a standalone SAT item must be self-contained (the dimensions are luckily restated in parentheses, but the phrasing is still orphaned).
- difficulty_mismatch: Direct application of SA = 2(lw + lh + wh) with given dimensions is an easy-tier computation, not a hard one; no multi-step reasoning or modeling is required.

### Math · Geometry · hard · #37 (q01795)

**Q:** Using the same sector (arc length 8, radius 6), what is the measure of the central angle, rounded to the nearest degree?

- A) 65 degrees
- B) 70 degrees
- C) 76 degrees
- D) 82 degrees

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (high)
- difficulty_mismatch: This is a one-step application of θ = s/r (8/6 rad ≈ 76.4°), which is closer to an easy/medium item than a 'hard' one.
- unclear_or_ambiguous: The phrase 'Using the same sector' refers to a prior question not included here; although the needed values are restated parenthetically, the item is not self-contained as written.

### Math · Geometry · hard · #39 (q01797)

**Q:** Using the same relationship (radius = s√3/2), if s=4, what is the radius of the circumscribed sphere? (Express your answer in simplest radical form.)

**Key:** 2√3 · **Reviewer:** 2√3 (high)
- formatting: SAT student-produced response answers must be entered as integers, decimals, or fractions; a radical form such as 2√3 cannot be entered. The instruction 'express your answer in simplest radical form' is incompatible with the grid-in format (a decimal like 3.46 would be required).
- unclear_or_ambiguous: The question refers to 'the same relationship' and 'the circumscribed sphere' from an unstated previous item; no figure or solid (e.g., cube with edge s) is described, so the geometric context is missing and the item cannot stand alone.
- difficulty_mismatch: Labeled 'hard,' but the task is a one-step substitution into a formula that is handed to the student, requiring no geometric reasoning.

## minor (356)

### R&W · Central Ideas and Details · easy · #13 (q00013)

> Malala Yousafzai grew up in Pakistan's Swat Valley, where she began speaking out for girls' right to education at a young age. In 2012, she was shot by a member of the Taliban on her way home from school. She survived and continued her advocacy from the United Kingdom, where she recovered. In 2014, at age seventeen, she became the youngest person ever to receive the Nobel Peace Prize. Today she leads the Malala Fund, an organization dedicated to ensuring twelve years of free, quality education for every girl in the world.

**Q:** According to the passage, what did Malala Yousafzai do after surviving the attack?

- A) She returned to her school in the Swat Valley and resumed her classes.
- B) She continued advocating for girls' education and founded an organization to support it.
- C) She wrote a book about her experiences and used the proceeds to build schools.
- D) She became a member of the Pakistani government to change education laws.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- other: Choice B says she "founded an organization," while the passage only states she "leads the Malala Fund." The inference is reasonable and B is clearly the best answer, but a stricter wording ("leads an organization") would better match the text.

### R&W · Central Ideas and Details · medium-high · #7 (q00067)

> For decades, the prevailing scientific consensus maintained that the adult human brain was structurally static, incapable of generating new neurons. However, the discovery of adult neurogenesis—specifically within the dentate gyrus of the hippocampus—shattered this dogma. Research reveals that environmental enrichment, physical exercise, and cognitive challenges stimulate the production and integration of these mature new cells. Conversely, chronic stress and sleep deprivation significantly suppress neurogenesis, potentially accelerating cognitive decline. This dynamic plasticity indicates that the structural integrity of the adult brain is continuously modulated by lifestyle factors and environmental interactions.

**Q:** Which choice best states the main idea of the text?

- A) The discovery of adult neurogenesis proved that the brain's structure is fixed from birth and unaffected by chronic stress.
- B) Physical exercise is the only lifestyle factor capable of preventing the degeneration of the dentate gyrus.
- C) Modern neuroscience has confirmed that the adult human brain undergoes continuous structural modification influenced by external and lifestyle inputs.
- D) Chronic stress completely eliminates the human brain's capacity to process cognitive challenges by destroying the hippocampus.

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (high)
- difficulty_mismatch: The three distractors contain blatant contradictions of the passage ('fixed from birth,' 'the only lifestyle factor,' 'completely eliminates... destroying the hippocampus'), making the item far easier than a medium-high Central Ideas question; effective SAT distractors are usually plausible but too narrow or subtly distorted.
- other: Minor scientific imprecision in the passage: newly generated neurons are initially immature, so describing stimulated 'production and integration of these mature new cells' is internally awkward, though it does not affect the answer.

### R&W · Central Ideas and Details · medium-high · #8 (q00068)

> In his 1689 treatise An Essay Concerning Human Understanding, philosopher John Locke attacked the doctrine of innate ideas—the notion that human beings are born with pre-existing knowledge and concepts. Locke famously posited that the human mind at birth is a *tabula rasa*, or blank slate. According to his empiricist framework, all knowledge is subsequently derived from experience, entering the mind via sensory perception and internal reflection on those sensations. Locke contended that complex ideas do not emerge from an internal genetic blueprint but are constructed through the mind’s active combining of simple sensory inputs received from the external world.

**Q:** Which choice best summarizes John Locke's argument as presented in the text?

- A) Human minds are born with pre-existing complex ideas that are gradually unlocked by physical experiences.
- B) The human mind begins as a blank slate, and all knowledge is constructed out of subsequent sensory and reflective experiences.
- C) Sensory perception is unreliable because the external world is constantly shifting its structural inputs.
- D) Genetic blueprints determine how the mind combines pre-existing innate concepts into complex ideas.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- difficulty_mismatch: The correct answer closely paraphrases the passage while distractors contradict it outright; this is easier than the stated medium-high difficulty.

### R&W · Central Ideas and Details · medium-high · #9 (q00069)

> As global temperatures rise, Arctic permafrost—permanently frozen soil that blankets polar regions—is thawing at an unprecedented rate. This thaw creates a perilous positive feedback loop: permafrost contains vast reservoirs of organic matter, consisting of ancient dead plants and animals. When the soil warms, microbes decompose this organic material, releasing enormous volumes of methane and carbon dioxide into the atmosphere. These greenhouse gases trap additional solar radiation, further accelerating global warming and inducing even more permafrost to melt. This self-reinforcing cycle amplifies the localized impacts of Arctic warming into a global climatic threat.

**Q:** Which choice best explains the "positive feedback loop" described in the text?

- A) Rising temperatures melt permafrost, which releases gases that trap more heat and cause further melting.
- B) Decomposing microbes consume greenhouse gases, which cools the Arctic and slows permafrost decay.
- C) The growth of polar plants absorbs methane, neutralizing the negative effects of permafrost thaw.
- D) Solar radiation directly destroys organic matter in the permafrost, preventing gases from escaping into the atmosphere.

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (high)
- difficulty_mismatch: The three distractors contradict the passage outright (microbes consuming gases, plants absorbing methane, radiation preventing gas release), making this a straightforward paraphrase item better suited to an easy tier than medium-high.

### R&W · Central Ideas and Details · medium-high · #10 (q00070)

> Classical scholarship often characterized the ancient Roman economy as a localized, agrarian system focused primarily on Mediterranean trade. However, recent archaeological excavations at ports along the Red Sea coast have revealed a sophisticated, long-distance maritime network connecting Rome with the Indian subcontinent. Artifacts such as Roman pottery, coins, and glass beads found in India—matched by Indian pepper, silk, and teakwood discovered in Egyptian Roman ports—demonstrate a robust commercial exchange. This evidence forces historians to revise previous assumptions, showing that the Roman economic apparatus was deeply integrated into a globalizing network of Eurasian commerce.

**Q:** Which choice best states the main idea of the text?

- A) New archaeological discoveries prove that the ancient Roman economy was entirely dependent on Indian silk and teakwood.
- B) Traditional historical scholarship was correct in characterizing the Roman economy as purely agrarian and localized.
- C) Trade along the Red Sea coast was limited exclusively to the exchange of pottery and glass beads.
- D) Recent evidence reveals that the Roman economy engaged in extensive long-distance maritime commerce with India, altering historical consensus.

**Key:** D · **Reviewer:** D (high)
- difficulty_mismatch: Distractors are obviously wrong (extreme words like 'entirely,' 'exclusively,' and a direct contradiction of the text), making this easier than a medium-high item.

### R&W · Central Ideas and Details · medium-high · #12 (q00072)

> Marine biologist Rachel Przeslawski has documented a troubling consequence of rising ocean temperatures: the disruption of behavioral synchrony in coral reef ecosystems. Many reef species—including fish, invertebrates, and corals themselves—time their spawning events to coincide with one another, creating brief but intense reproductive windows. This synchrony evolved over millennia to maximize fertilization rates and overwhelm predators through sheer reproductive volume. However, Przeslawski's research shows that as water temperatures rise unpredictably, different species respond to thermal cues at different rates, causing their reproductive cycles to fall out of alignment. The result is a cascade of reproductive failures that threatens the genetic diversity and long-term resilience of entire reef communities.

**Q:** Which choice best states a key detail that supports the passage's central claim?

- A) Rising ocean temperatures cause different reef species to respond to thermal cues at varying rates, disrupting their shared reproductive timing.
- B) Rachel Przeslawski has spent decades studying the effects of climate change on marine ecosystems throughout the Pacific Ocean.
- C) Coral reef ecosystems depend entirely on fish populations to maintain the spawning cycles that sustain reef biodiversity.
- D) The genetic diversity of coral reef communities was already declining before rising temperatures began disrupting spawning synchrony.

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (high)
- difficulty_mismatch: The correct choice is nearly verbatim from the passage while the three distractors contain claims clearly unsupported (decades of Pacific study, 'entirely' dependent on fish, prior decline), making this easier than a medium-high item.

### R&W · Command of Evidence (Textual) · medium-high · #8 (q00188)

> A zoologist hypothesized that male túngara frogs in urban environments produce louder, more complex mating calls than their rural counterparts to overcome ambient city traffic noise, despite this adaptation making them significantly more vulnerable to predatory fringe-lipped bats, which hunt frogs by tracking their vocalizations. The zoologist conducted speaker playback experiments in controlled environments to test bat attraction rates to urban and rural frog call recordings.

**Q:** Which choice, if true, would most directly support the zoologist's hypothesis?

- A) Urban male frogs produced calls that were 15 decibels louder than rural frogs, and predatory bats attacked speakers playing the urban call recordings three times more frequently than speakers playing rural recordings.
- B) Predatory bats completely ignored all frog call recordings when researchers played loud city traffic noise over the experimental chamber's sound system.
- C) Rural male frogs instantly silenced themselves when a mechanical bat model passed overhead, whereas urban frogs continued calling without interruption.
- D) Urban male frogs possessed smaller body sizes on average than rural frogs, allowing them to hide more effectively in shallow puddles.

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (high)
- difficulty_mismatch: Choice A supports both halves of the hypothesis explicitly while B, C, and D are clearly irrelevant or contradictory, making the item easier than the stated medium-high tier.

### R&W · Command of Evidence (Textual) · medium-high · #9 (q00189)

> An public policy researcher evaluated the efficacy of an excise tax imposed on sugar-sweetened beverages in an urban city. The researcher hypothesized that the tax successfully reduces consumption of unhealthy drinks among low-income households by increasing prices, while leaving the purchasing habits of high-income households completely unaffected due to their lower sensitivity to minor price changes.

**Q:** Which choice, if true, would most directly support the researcher's hypothesis?

- A) Following the implementation of the tax, sales of sugar-sweetened beverages dropped by 30% in grocery stores located within low-income neighborhoods, but remained completely unchanged in high-income neighborhoods.
- B) Total tax revenue collected from sugar-sweetened beverages increased steadily across all neighborhoods during the first twelve months of the program.
- C) Low-income residents reported that they noticed the price increase but chose to buy untaxed fruit juices that contained identical amounts of sugar.
- D) High-income residents reported that they reduced their overall grocery spending by purchasing generic brands instead of name-brand sugary beverages.

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (high)
- other: Typo in the passage: "An public policy researcher" should be "A public policy researcher." On a test of Standard English conventions, such an error in the stimulus is a notable flaw.
- difficulty_mismatch: Choice A restates the hypothesis almost verbatim (drop among low-income, unchanged among high-income), while B, C, and D are plainly non-supporting. The item plays closer to easy than medium-high.

### R&W · Command of Evidence (Textual) · medium-high · #16 (q00196)

> The reintroduction of wolves to Yellowstone National Park in 1995 has become a landmark case in ecology, frequently cited as evidence for "trophic cascades" — the idea that a top predator's presence reorganizes an entire ecosystem through chain reactions. Elk, fearful of wolf predation, began avoiding riverbanks and valleys where they were vulnerable, allowing vegetation in those areas to recover. Recovered vegetation stabilized riverbanks, reduced erosion, and altered the physical course of streams — a phenomenon researchers called a "landscape of fear." Critics of the trophic cascade narrative, however, note that other variables, including drought cycles and changes in human hunting pressure, also coincided with the ecological changes observed, complicating any simple causal story.

**Q:** The critics mentioned in the passage would most likely use which of the following to challenge the claim that wolf reintroduction caused the observed ecological changes?

- A) Evidence that elk populations in Yellowstone did not decline significantly after wolves were reintroduced
- B) The observation that wolves in other national parks have not produced comparable vegetation recovery
- C) The fact that ecological changes coincided with other environmental and human factors that could have independently produced similar effects
- D) Research showing that riverbank erosion is primarily controlled by annual precipitation levels rather than vegetation cover

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (high)
- difficulty_mismatch: The correct answer is a near-verbatim restatement of the passage's final sentence ('other variables... also coincided'), making the item considerably easier than the stated medium-high tier; the distractors introduce outside information not hinted at in the text.

### R&W · Command of Evidence (Textual) · medium-high · #17 (q00197)

> The historian E.P. Thompson famously argued that the English working class was not a passive product of industrialization but an active agent in its own formation. Drawing on popular culture, Methodist religion, radical politics, and craft traditions, workers developed a shared sense of class identity before the industrial revolution had fully transformed the economy. Thompson's approach implicitly criticized the tendency — common in structuralist sociology — to treat class as a position in an economic system that automatically generates corresponding consciousness. For Thompson, class was not a structure but a relationship: something that happened as people lived and worked together and came to recognize common interests against those of other groups.

**Q:** According to the passage, Thompson's concept of class differs from the structuralist sociological view in what fundamental way?

- A) Thompson believes class identity forms only after economic transformation is complete, while structuralists argue it can precede industrialization.
- B) Thompson treats class as something dynamically produced through lived human experience, while structuralists treat it as a position that automatically generates consciousness.
- C) Thompson limits his analysis to the English context, while structuralists claimed their economic models applied universally.
- D) Thompson focuses on religious and cultural factors, while structuralists argue that radical politics is the primary driver of class formation.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- difficulty_mismatch: The correct answer is a near-verbatim paraphrase of the passage's final sentence ('class was not a structure but a relationship... as people lived and worked together') and the distractors each contain clear contradictions of the text, making this easier than the stated medium-high tier.
- other: Skill label mismatch: the item asks for a comparison stated in the passage, which is a Central Ideas/Details (or Inferences) task rather than Command of Evidence (Textual), which typically asks which quotation or finding supports/weakens a claim.

### R&W · Command of Evidence (Textual) · medium-high · #20 (q00200)

> In analyzing political propaganda, scholars have identified a technique called "strategic ambiguity," in which a message is deliberately constructed to mean different things to different audiences. Unlike mere vagueness, which arises from imprecision, strategic ambiguity is intentional: the communicator crafts language that allows incompatible groups to each hear what they wish. This technique is particularly effective in coalition-building, where a leader must appeal simultaneously to constituencies with contradictory interests. The risk, however, is that once a leader must govern — and therefore make actual policy choices — the contradictions suppressed by ambiguous language resurface as political liabilities, creating the perception of betrayal among supporters who had projected their preferred meanings onto the original message.

**Q:** According to the passage, what distinguishes strategic ambiguity from ordinary vagueness?

- A) Strategic ambiguity involves technical language that audiences without specialized knowledge cannot interpret correctly.
- B) Vagueness is unintentional, while strategic ambiguity is deliberately engineered to allow multiple incompatible interpretations simultaneously.
- C) Strategic ambiguity is used primarily in written communication, whereas vagueness more commonly appears in spoken political discourse.
- D) Vagueness tends to alienate audiences, while strategic ambiguity creates a sense of clarity for each individual listener.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- difficulty_mismatch: The passage states the distinction almost verbatim ('Unlike mere vagueness, which arises from imprecision, strategic ambiguity is intentional'), and the three distractors introduce content absent from the text. This is a straightforward retrieval item, easier than the stated medium-high tier.

### R&W · Command of Evidence (Textual) · hard · #24 (q00234)

> Maria Montessori's educational philosophy rested on the conviction that children possess an intrinsic drive toward competence — what she called the "absorbent mind" — that is stifled rather than cultivated by traditional classroom instruction centered on rote memorization and external reward. Montessori classrooms were designed to allow children to select their own activities from a prepared environment and to work without interruption for extended periods, under the premise that intrinsic motivation, when allowed to operate, produces deeper learning and more durable understanding than external incentives can. Critics have argued, however, that this model assumes a degree of self-regulation that many children, particularly those from chaotic home environments, may not yet have developed, and that without structured guidance, such children risk reinforcing existing gaps rather than closing them.

**Q:** The critics' argument, as presented in the passage, assumes which of the following about the Montessori model?

- A) The model's dependence on children's intrinsic motivation makes it effective for most children but systematically disadvantages children who require external incentives to engage with academic material
- B) The model's effectiveness is contingent on a level of self-regulation that is not uniformly distributed across children, particularly those whose home environments have not supported its development
- C) The model was designed with middle-class children in mind and therefore fails to incorporate the cultural knowledge and learning styles of children from lower socioeconomic backgrounds
- D) The model produces deeper learning for individual tasks but does not adequately prepare children for the externally structured environments they will encounter in higher education and professional life

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- difficulty_mismatch: Labeled 'hard,' but B is a nearly verbatim paraphrase of the passage's final sentence while the distractors introduce ideas (external incentives being needed, socioeconomic/cultural design, preparation for later structured environments) that are plainly absent, making the item easy to medium.

### R&W · Command of Evidence (Quantitative) · medium-low · #2 (q00272)

> A climatologist studied the carbon sequestration efficiency (how much CO2 is absorbed per square kilometer) of four distinct forest ecosystems: tropical rainforest, temperate deciduous forest, boreal coniferous forest, and coastal mangrove forest. The researcher hypothesized that while tropical rainforests absorb the largest total volume of carbon globally due to their vast geographic size, coastal mangrove forests are significantly more efficient on a per-square-kilometer basis.
> Table: Forest Carbon Sequestration Data
> | Forest Ecosystem    | Global Area (million km²) | CO2 Absorbed per km² (tons/year) |
> |---------------------|---------------------------|----------------------------------|
> | Tropical Rainforest | 12.4                      | 2,100                            |
> | Temperate Deciduous | 8.1                       | 1,350                            |
> | Boreal Coniferous   | 15.2                      | 980                              |
> | Coastal Mangrove    | 0.15                      | 4,500                            |

**Q:** Which choice best uses data from the table to support the climatologist's hypothesis?

- A) Boreal coniferous forests blanket the largest global area at 15.2 million km² but absorb only 980 tons of CO2 per km² each year. |B) Tropical rainforests cover 12.4 million km² and sequester 2,100 tons per km², which represents the highest total global volume.
- B) 
- C) Coastal mangrove forests occupy just 0.15 million km² but sequester 4,500 tons of CO2 per km² per year, more than double the per-km² rate of tropical rainforests.
- D) Temperate deciduous forests exhibit an efficiency of 1,350 tons per km², making them more efficient than boreal coniferous forests.

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (high)
- formatting: The answer choices are broken: option A contains two separate choices concatenated with a stray '|' delimiter (the A text and the intended B text), and option B is left blank. As presented, a student cannot select the intended choice B at all.

### R&W · Command of Evidence (Quantitative) · hard · #2 (q00332)

> In solid-state physics, the efficiency of an thermoelectric material—which converts heat gradients directly into electrical energy—is quantified by its dimensionless figure of merit, ZT. Traditional theoretical models suggest that a material's ZT value is maximized when its lattice structures are doped with heavy crystalline compounds like bismuth telluride, which suppress thermal conductivity while maintaining high electrical transport. Physicist Akira Sato hypothesized that while bismuth-doped alloys deliver high performance under low-temperature configurations, a newly synthesized silicon-germanium nanostructure matrix achieves significantly superior ZT values once temperatures exceed 600 Kelvin (K).
> Table: Dimensionless Figure of Merit (ZT) Across Thermal Profiles
> | Tested Material Composition | Low Temp (300 K) | Mid Temp (500 K) | High Temp (700 K) | Ultra High Temp (900 K) |
> |-----------------------------|------------------|------------------|-------------------|-------------------------|
> | Bismuth Telluride Alloy     | 1.20             | 1.35             | 0.85              | 0.40                    |
> | Silicon-Germanium Matrix    | 0.25             | 0.60             | 1.65              | 1.90                    |
> | Lead Selenide Compound      | 0.50             | 0.95             | 1.10              | 1.05                    |

**Q:** Which choice best uses data from the table to support Sato's hypothesis?

- A) Bismuth Telluride achieved its peak performance at 500 K with a ZT of 1.35, outperforming the Silicon-Germanium matrix across both low and mid-temperature intervals.
- B) At 700 K and 900 K, the Silicon-Germanium matrix reached ZT values of 1.65 and 1.90 respectively, significantly outperforming Bismuth Telluride (0.85 and 0.40) and Lead Selenide (1.10 and 1.05).
- C) Lead Selenide demonstrated a highly stable performance envelope, holding a ZT value of around 1.05 across all high-temperature testing environments.
- D) The Silicon-Germanium matrix exhibited a continuous upward trajectory, expanding its ZT value by exactly 0.35 points between 300 K and 500 K.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- other: Minor typo in the passage: 'an thermoelectric material' should be 'a thermoelectric material.' Does not affect the answer.

### R&W · Command of Evidence (Quantitative) · hard · #4 (q00334)

> In evolutionary microbiology, the "cost of resistance" model states that bacteria acquiring mutations to survive antibiotic treatments exhibit compromised metabolic efficiency when grown in drug-free environments, making them lose out to wild-type strains. Geneticist Marcus Thorne challenged this dogma, hypothesizing that while a single resistance mutation (*mut-1*) incurs a severe metabolic penalty, the subsequent acquisition of a compensatory secondary mutation (*mut-2*) fully restores reproductive fitness, allowing resistant strains to match or exceed wild-type growth rates even in environments completely free of antibiotics.
> Table: Average Bacterial Growth Rate (Generations per Hour)
> | Strain Type            | Baseline Glucose Medium | Enriched L-Lactate Medium | Minimal Salt Medium |
> |------------------------|-------------------------|---------------------------|---------------------|
> | Wild-Type (Control)    | 2.4                     | 1.8                       | 1.1                 |
> | Resistant (*mut-1*)    | 1.1                     | 0.8                       | 0.4                 |
> | Compensated (*mut-2*)  | 2.5                     | 1.9                       | 1.2                 |

**Q:** Which choice best uses data from the table to support Thorne's hypothesis?

- A) The single-mutation resistant strain (*mut-1*) grew at a sluggish rate of 1.1 in glucose and 0.8 in L-lactate, confirming the classic cost of resistance model.
- B) Across all three drug-free media types, the compensated strain (*mut-2*) achieved growth rates of 2.5, 1.9, and 1.2, which matched or slightly exceeded the wild-type control benchmarks (2.4, 1.8, and 1.1) and far surpassed the *mut-1* strain.
- C) Wild-type control bacteria demonstrated their highest reproductive efficiency when grown in the baseline glucose medium, reaching 2.4 generations per hour.
- D) The resistant strain (*mut-1*) experienced its absolute worst metabolic performance in the minimal salt medium, clearing only 0.4 generations per hour.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- difficulty_mismatch: The correct choice is the only one that mentions both the compensated strain and the wild-type comparison, making it easy to spot; this is closer to easy/medium than 'hard.'

### R&W · Command of Evidence (Quantitative) · hard · #9 (q00339)

> The following text is adapted from a historical archaeology study on maritime trade.
> Economic historians frequently utilize amphora shard distributions to model the integration of maritime supply chains in the ancient Roman Mediterranean. A common historical paradigm assumes that large urban hubs (such as Port Alpha) imported olive oil via an indiscriminate market system, drawing supplies uniformly from across the empire's three production provinces (Baetica, Proconsularis, and Italia). Archaeologist Marcus Vance challenged this paradigm, arguing that supply networks were strictly compartmentalized by geographical distance and political alliances. He hypothesized that Port Alpha relied almost exclusively on Baetica for oil imports, with Proconsularis and Italia remaining entirely restricted to minor secondary fractions across all excavation strata.
> Table: Olive Oil Amphora Frequencies (% of Total Recovered Oil Shards)
> | Excavation Stratum Period | Baetica Province Shards | Proconsularis Province Shards | Italia Province Shards |
> |---------------------------|-------------------------|------------------------------|-------------------------|
> | Stratum I (Early Empire)  | 84.5%                   | 8.2%                         | 7.3%                    |
> | Stratum II (Mid Empire)   | 88.2%                   | 6.4%                         | 5.4%                    |
> | Stratum III (Late Empire) | 81.0%                   | 10.5%                        | 8.5%                    |

**Q:** Which choice best uses data from the table to support Vance's compartmentalization hypothesis over the indiscriminate market paradigm?

- A) Across all three historical stratum periods, Baetica province shards overwhelmingly dominated Port Alpha's assemblage at over 80%, while Proconsularis and Italia combined never accounted for more than 19% of the oil shards.
- B) Shard frequencies for the Proconsularis province increased slightly from 6.4% during the Mid Empire stratum up to 10.5% during the Late Empire excavation block.
- C) In Stratum II, Baetica oil shards reached an absolute peak frequency of 88.2%, demonstrating that mid-empire supply lines were exceptionally profitable.
- D) Olive oil shards originating from the Italia province dropped to their absolute lowest historical frequency during the Mid Empire period, matching a baseline of 5.4%.

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (high)
- other: In choice A, the Stratum III combined total of Proconsularis and Italia is exactly 19.0%, so 'never accounted for more than 19%' is technically true but borderline; a cleaner threshold (e.g., 'never exceeded 20%') would avoid the edge case.

### R&W · Command of Evidence (Quantitative) · hard · #10 (q00340)

> A glacial morphologist monitored the rate of ice sheet velocity shifts across three distinct polar ice shelves (Shelves A, B, and C) to evaluate the impact of sub-glacial meltwater channels. The traditional glaciological model assumes that ice sheet acceleration is a uniform, direct function of atmospheric temperature increases, predicting that all ice shelves accelerate at identical rates during peak summer melting months. The morphologist argues that this model oversimplifies glaciological dynamics. He hypothesizes that ice acceleration is fundamentally controlled by underlying bedtime topography: shelves resting on slick, downward-sloping bedrock (Shelf B) experience explosive velocity increases during summer melts, whereas shelves locked behind protective sub-glacial rock ridges (Shelves A and C) exhibit rigid structural stability regardless of atmospheric warming inputs.
> Table: Average Ice Sheet Flow Velocity (Meters per Year)
> | Monitored Ice Shelf | Winter Baseline Flow | Early Summer Melt Flow | Peak Summer Melt Flow |
> |---------------------|----------------------|------------------------|-----------------------|
> | Shelf A (Ridge Lock) | 120 m/year           | 122 m/year             | 125 m/year            |
> | Shelf B (Slick Slope)| 145 m/year           | 480 m/year             | 1,650 m/year          |
> | Shelf C (Ridge Lock) | 95 m/year            | 98 m/year              | 101 m/year            |

**Q:** Which choice best uses data from the table to support the morphologist's hypothesis over the traditional glaciological model?

- A) Shelf B's velocity surged dramatically from a winter baseline of 145 m/year to a peak of 1,650 m/year during the summer melt, while Ridge-locked Shelves A and C remained highly stable, moving only from 120 to 125 and 95 to 101 respectively.
- B) Winter baseline flow records indicated that Shelf B was already moving faster than the other two ice shelves prior to the onset of any atmospheric thermal inputs. |C) Ridge-locked Shelf A moved at a flow velocity of 122 m/year during the early summer melt phase, outperforming Shelf C's early summer tracking metric by 24 meters.
- C) 
- D) Combined average flow velocities across all three polar ice shelves escalated continuously as the tracking environment transitioned from winter to peak summer conditions.

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (high)
- formatting: The answer choices are broken: choice B's text runs into choice C's text ("...thermal inputs. |C) Ridge-locked Shelf A moved at..."), and the actual choice C is listed as blank. The option set must be repaired so each of B, C, and D appears separately.
- other: Typo in the passage: "underlying bedtime topography" should read "bedrock topography."
- difficulty_mismatch: Labeled hard, but the correct choice is unmistakably the only one citing the contrast central to the hypothesis; the distractors are obviously irrelevant, making this closer to easy/medium.

### R&W · Command of Evidence (Quantitative) · hard · #22 (q00352)

> A public health researcher argued that the relationship between urban green space coverage and self-reported stress levels is not simply inverse — that is, more green space does not uniformly predict lower stress. Instead, she proposed that neighborhood income level moderates the relationship: in lower-income neighborhoods, green space has a stronger stress-reducing effect than in higher-income neighborhoods, where residents may have access to additional stress-relief resources. She presented survey data from six neighborhoods varying in green space coverage and median household income.
> Table: Green Space, Income Level, and Mean Stress Score by Neighborhood
> | Neighborhood | Green Space (% of area) | Median Household Income ($) | Mean Stress Score (1–10) |
> |---|---|---|---|
> | Alderton | 8 | 38,000 | 7.4 |
> | Brynn Park | 22 | 37,500 | 5.1 |
> | Claridge | 9 | 82,000 | 6.8 |
> | Dunmore | 21 | 79,000 | 6.5 |
> | Eastfield | 7 | 41,000 | 7.6 |
> | Fenwick | 20 | 77,000 | 6.6 |

**Q:** Which choice best uses data from the table to support the researcher's claim?

- A) Brynn Park, with 22% green space and a median income of $37,500, had a mean stress score of 5.1, the lowest in the dataset, while Alderton, with only 8% green space and a similar income, had a stress score of 7.4 — a difference of 2.3 points among low-income neighborhoods.
- B) Dunmore and Fenwick, both higher-income neighborhoods with roughly 20–21% green space, had stress scores of 6.5 and 6.6 respectively, nearly identical to Claridge's score of 6.8 despite Claridge having only 9% green space, suggesting green space has little effect in higher-income areas.
- C) Across all six neighborhoods, mean stress scores declined as green space coverage increased, confirming that more green space uniformly predicts lower resident stress regardless of income level.
- D) Both A and B taken together — the large stress reduction associated with green space in low-income neighborhoods and the muted effect in high-income neighborhoods — support the researcher's moderation hypothesis.

**Key:** D · **Reviewer:** D (high)
- formatting: Choice D is an "all/both of the above"-style option referring to other answer choices rather than presenting data itself. The Digital SAT never uses this construction; authentic Command of Evidence (Quantitative) items present four independent data statements. This makes the item unrepresentative of the test, and it also signals the key almost immediately (the moderation claim obviously requires both halves of the comparison).
- difficulty_mismatch: Labeled 'hard,' but the test-taking shortcut of selecting the combined option makes it easier than a genuine hard quantitative-evidence item; choice C is also an obvious throwaway that contradicts the table.

### R&W · Inferences · easy · #1 (q00361)

> To evaluate how urban light pollution impacts insect populations, a research team compared the behavior of nocturnal moths in a city park with those in an isolated rural forest. They observed that moths in the city park spent significantly less time foraging for food and more time hovering around artificial streetlights, while rural moths focused exclusively on survival behaviors like feeding and avoiding predators.

**Q:** Which choice most logically completes the text?

- A) Artificial urban lighting disrupts the natural nocturnal behaviors of moths relative to their rural counterparts.
- B) City parks contain fewer natural food sources for nocturnal insects than rural forests do.
- C) Rural moths would quickly adapt to artificial lighting if relocated to an urban park environment.
- D) Nocturnal moths are the only insect species negatively affected by city light pollution.

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (high)
- formatting: The question asks which choice "most logically completes the text," but the passage ends with a complete sentence and contains no blank or lead-in clause (e.g., "Therefore, the researchers likely concluded that ___"). The item functions as an inference/conclusion question rather than a completion question as presented.

### R&W · Inferences · easy · #3 (q00363)

> An art historian analyzed the brushstrokes of two Renaissance painters, Artist X and Artist Y. The historian found that Artist X used thin, precisely layered strokes that required several days to dry, allowing for intricate facial details. In contrast, Artist Y applied thick, rapid strokes that dried quickly, prioritizing the depiction of motion and broad atmospheric lighting over fine details.

**Q:** Which choice most logically completes the text?

- A) Artist Y was able to finish individual paintings in less time than Artist X typically required.
- B) Paintings created by Artist X were highly valued by Renaissance critics for their emotional depth.
- C) Artist X and Artist Y frequently collaborated to combine their distinct painting techniques.
- D) The artistic style of Artist X was objectively superior to the fast-paced technique of Artist Y.

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (high)
- formatting: The item asks which choice 'most logically completes the text,' but the passage ends as a complete sentence with no blank or trailing incomplete clause (e.g., 'Therefore, it can be concluded that ___'). College Board convention requires the stimulus to end with an unfinished statement for this question type.

### R&W · Inferences · easy · #4 (q00364)

> Many deep-sea fish species possess organs called photophores that emit bioluminescent light. Marine biologists have discovered that certain species use these lights to illuminate dark waters to hunt, while other species use rhythmic flashing patterns to communicate with potential mates in the pitch-black environment.

**Q:** Which choice most logically completes the text?

- A) Bioluminescence in deep-sea fish serves a variety of ecological functions depending on the species.
- B) Deep-sea fish would be unable to find mates if they did not possess photophores.
- C) Photophores developed primarily as a defense mechanism against apex deep-sea predators.
- D) Every deep-sea organism relies on bioluminescence to navigate through dark benthic zones.

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (high)
- formatting: The prompt asks which choice "most logically completes the text," but the passage ends with a complete sentence and contains no blank or transitional lead-in (e.g., "Therefore, ______"). SAT completion items present an incomplete final sentence; as written the task reads more like an inference/main-idea question.

### R&W · Inferences · easy · #5 (q00365)

> A team of architects evaluated the internal climate of traditional adobe homes compared to modern concrete brick structures during hot summer months. They recorded that while concrete buildings heated up rapidly during peak daylight hours, the thick mud-and-straw walls of adobe homes absorbed the sun's heat slowly, keeping the interior cool during the day and releasing that trapped heat inside only during the cold nighttime hours.

**Q:** Which choice most logically completes the text?

- A) Modern concrete brick homes require less energy to cool at night than traditional adobe homes do.
- B) Traditional adobe construction methods provide natural thermal regulation well-suited for hot climates.
- C) Adobe walls are structurally more durable during extreme summer storms than concrete walls.
- D) Architects should completely stop using concrete brick materials in all modern residential housing.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: Inference items of this type normally end with an incomplete sentence or a blank (e.g., 'Therefore, ____'). Here the passage is a complete paragraph, so the prompt 'most logically completes the text' does not match the text as presented. This doesn't change which answer is best, but it deviates from College Board format.

### R&W · Inferences · easy · #6 (q00366)

> Linguists tracking the evolution of a dialect in an isolated mountain region noticed that younger speakers consistently adopted vocabulary introduced by external digital media, whereas older community members retained archaic phrasing. After a new highway connected the region to a major metropolitan center, the adoption of modern vocabulary accelerated rapidly among all age groups.

**Q:** Which choice most logically completes the text?

- A) Older speakers were completely unable to understand the digital media vocabulary prior to the highway construction.
- B) Increased physical connection to outside communities can speed up linguistic shifts within an isolated group.
- C) The regional dialect will completely disappear within the next generation of speakers.
- D) Young speakers prefer driving to metropolitan centers more than older community members do.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: The passage has no blank or incomplete final clause (e.g., 'Therefore, ___'), yet the question asks which choice 'most logically completes the text.' Standard SAT inference items end with an unfinished sentence; the choices here function as conclusions rather than completions.

### R&W · Inferences · easy · #8 (q00368)

> To study plant communication, researchers wounded the leaves of tomato plants to simulate an attack by pests. Within hours, the damaged plants released airborne chemical compounds called volatile organic compounds (VOCs). Nearby undamaged tomato plants that detected these VOCs immediately began producing defensive toxins in their own leaves, making them less palatable to insects.

**Q:** Which choice most logically completes the text?

- A) Tomato plants can use airborne chemicals to trigger preventative defense responses in neighboring plants.
- B) Wounded tomato plants are unable to survive insect attacks without the assistance of nearby crops.
- C) Defensive toxins produced by tomato plants are highly dangerous to human consumers.
- D) Volatile organic compounds are only released when a plant is entirely destroyed by a pest.

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (high)
- formatting: The prompt asks which choice "most logically completes the text," but the passage is a set of complete sentences with no concluding blank or lead-in (e.g., "Therefore, researchers concluded that ______"). The item functions as a conclusion/inference question, so the stem and passage don't match College Board conventions.

### R&W · Inferences · easy · #9 (q00369)

> A wildlife biologist observed that gray wolves in a national park adjusted their hunting routes based on human hiking patterns. During the peak summer tourist season, when hikers frequented the primary valley trails during the day, wolves shifted their hunting activities to the higher ridges and hunted exclusively at night. In the winter, when tourist numbers dropped to near zero, wolves returned to hunting in the valleys during daylight hours.

**Q:** Which choice most logically completes the text?

- A) Gray wolves are fundamentally incapable of hunting successfully along high ridges during the winter.
- B) Human presence can influence the spatial and temporal hunting behaviors of gray wolves.
- C) The wolf population in the national park increases significantly during the winter months.
- D) Valley trails contain a higher density of prey animals during the summer than during the winter.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: The prompt asks which choice "most logically completes the text," but the passage is a complete paragraph with no trailing blank or lead-in phrase (e.g., "Therefore, it can be concluded that ______"). The choices function as conclusions, so the stem should either include the blank or ask what the observations most strongly suggest.

### R&W · Inferences · easy · #10 (q00370)

> An educational psychologist tracked the reading comprehension scores of elementary school students who participated in two different summer programs. Group A was assigned a fixed list of classic literature books to read, while Group B was allowed to choose any reading materials they preferred from the local library. At the start of the school year, Group B students demonstrated significantly higher engagement and score improvements compared to Group A.

**Q:** Which choice most logically completes the text?

- A) Allowing students autonomy in selecting their reading materials can positively impact reading outcomes.
- B) Classic literature is inherently too difficult for elementary school students to comprehend.
- C) Group A students would have performed better if they had read fewer books from their assigned list.
- D) Summer reading programs should completely eliminate all guidance from teachers and parents.

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (high)
- formatting: The question asks which choice 'most logically completes the text,' but the passage ends with a complete sentence and no blank. Standard SAT inference items end with an incomplete sentence (e.g., 'This suggests that ____').

### R&W · Inferences · easy · #12 (q00372)

> The school library opens thirty minutes before classes begin and stays open until five o'clock in the afternoon. Students can borrow up to five books at a time and must return them within two weeks. If a book is returned late, the student cannot borrow any new books until the overdue item is returned. The librarian, Mr. Chen, organizes weekly reading groups for students who want to discuss books they have read on their own.

**Q:** Based on the passage, what can be reasonably inferred about the library's policies?

- A) Students who return books late must pay a fine to the library.
- B) The library is designed to encourage responsible borrowing habits.
- C) Mr. Chen created the library's rules to increase book discussion.
- D) Students are only allowed to borrow books during reading group meetings.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- other: Minor internal illogic in the passage: "If a book is returned late, the student cannot borrow any new books until the overdue item is returned" is circular (the item has already been returned). It should read "If a book is not returned on time..." This doesn't change the correct answer but is sloppy.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: The prompt asks which choice 'most logically completes the text,' but the passage contains no blank or incomplete concluding sentence (e.g., 'This evidence suggests that ______'). As written, the item functions more like an inference/main-idea question than a completion question.

- formatting: The prompt asks which choice "most logically completes the text," but the passage ends with a complete sentence and contains no blank or lead-in clause (e.g., "Therefore, the researcher concluded that ___"). ### R&W · Inferences · medium-low · #10 (q00400)
