**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: The prompt asks which choice 'most logically completes the text,' but the passage is a complete set of sentences with no trailing blank or lead-in phrase (e.g., '...this suggests that ______'). The stem and passage are mismatched.
- difficulty_mismatch: Stated as medium-high, but the three distractors are transparently absurd (inherently malicious managers, 'guarantee' of no failures, executives 'enjoy' altered reports), making the item very easy.

### R&W · Inferences · medium-high · #9 (q00429)

> A literary analysis of 19th-century gothic novels examined the structural function of the "uncanny"—instances where a familiar domestic setting suddenly feels strange and threatening. The researcher noted that authors used detailed, comforting descriptions of family estates early in their narratives to establish a deep sense of psychological security in the reader. When supernatural or psychological disruptions occurred later in the same settings, the contrast magnified the reader's sense of dread far more than if the story had taken place in an inherently terrifying location, such as a ruined castle.

**Q:** Which choice most logically completes the text?

- A) Ruined castles are completely ineffective settings for generating dread in gothic literature.
- B) Gothic authors manipulated familiar domestic backdrops to amplify the emotional impact of narrative disruptions.
- C) 19th-century readers preferred stories about comforting family estates over tales of supernatural horror.
- D) The use of the uncanny was a technique invented exclusively by authors who disliked traditional horror tropes.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: The prompt asks which choice "most logically completes the text," but the passage ends with a complete sentence and contains no blank (e.g., a final clause ending in '____'). College Board inference items always include an explicit blank for the completion.
- difficulty_mismatch: For a stated medium-high difficulty, the distractors are implausibly extreme (words like 'completely ineffective,' 'exclusively,' and an unsupported claim about reader preference), making B obvious; the correct answer is also close to a direct paraphrase rather than an inference.

### R&W · Inferences · medium-high · #10 (q00430)

> A marine biology team tracked the foraging dives of leatherback sea turtles using satellite tags. They observed that the turtles regularly dove to extreme depths exceeding one thousand meters during the middle of the day, where they fed on bioluminescent jellyfish. At night, however, the turtles restricted their diving to the upper fifty meters of the water column, because the jellyfish populations naturally migrated upward to the ocean surface after sunset to feed on plankton.

**Q:** Which choice most logically completes the text?

- A) Leatherback sea turtles alter their vertical diving profiles to match the chronological movements of their primary prey.
- B) Bioluminescent jellyfish are unable to survive in shallow waters during peak daylight hours due to solar radiation.
- C) Leatherback sea turtles are physically incapable of diving to depths below fifty meters during nighttime hours.
- D) Plankton populations remain locked at the ocean surface throughout both day and night cycles.

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (high)
- formatting: The prompt asks which choice "most logically completes the text," but the passage ends with a complete sentence and contains no blank or trailing clause (e.g., "This suggests that ___"). The question should either include a blank/incomplete final sentence or be reworded as "Which choice best states the main idea / is most strongly supported by the text?"
- difficulty_mismatch: For a stated medium-high item, the correct answer is unusually transparent: A simply restates the passage's day/night pattern, while B, C, and D each assert claims flatly contradicted by or absent from the text (D is even contradicted since plankton are what the jellyfish surface to eat). No distractor is genuinely competitive.

### R&W · Inferences · medium-high · #19 (q00439)

> The concept of "island gigantism" describes the tendency of certain animal species to evolve larger body sizes on islands than their mainland relatives. Biologists have proposed two main explanations: reduced predation pressure, which relaxes the survival advantage of small body size, and increased resource competition among species that share an island ecosystem. Notably, island gigantism most commonly appears in species that occupy the largest predator niche on their island—that is, species that are themselves the top predators—suggesting that predation pressure may be the more influential factor.

**Q:** Which inference about island gigantism is best supported by the passage?

- A) Resource competition is unlikely to play any role in driving larger body sizes among island species.
- B) The prevalence of gigantism among top island predators suggests that freedom from predation is a stronger driver of the phenomenon than resource competition.
- C) Species that are not top predators never develop larger body sizes on islands than their mainland counterparts.
- D) Island gigantism is most pronounced in species that colonized islands more recently, when predation pressure was at its lowest.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- difficulty_mismatch: Choice B essentially restates the passage's final clause verbatim, while A, C, and D are eliminated by obvious absolute/unsupported language ('never,' 'any role,' a time claim never mentioned). This makes the item considerably easier than a medium-high inference question and tests paraphrase recognition more than inference.

### R&W · Inferences · hard · #1 (q00451)

> In resource economics, Jevons' Paradox occurs when technological progress increases the efficiency with which a resource is used, but the falling cost of using that resource causes a massive surge in market demand, ultimately increasing total consumption rather than reducing it. A transport analyst studied a metropolitan transit initiative that introduced highly fuel-efficient hybrid engines across a city's public bus network. The initiative succeeded in cutting fuel use per mile by 40%, which lowered operational costs and allowed the city to drastically reduce ticket prices. Consequently, daily ridership tripled, and the city expanded its bus routes by 200%.

**Q:** Which choice most logically completes the text?

- A) The metropolitan transit initiative will likely result in an aggregate increase in the city's total bus fuel consumption, illustrating Jevons' Paradox.
- B) Fuel-efficient hybrid engines are structurally incapable of reducing carbon emissions in large urban environments.
- C) The city would have saved more money if it had kept ticket prices high and refused to expand bus routes.
- D) Public transit ridership is entirely independent of ticket pricing structures and operational route expansions.

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (high)
- difficulty_mismatch: Labeled 'hard,' but the three distractors are absurd absolutes ('structurally incapable,' 'entirely independent') or unsupported counterfactuals, making the correct answer immediately obvious. This plays as an easy item.

### R&W · Inferences · hard · #2 (q00452)

> The "red queen hypothesis" in evolutionary biology posits that organisms must constantly adapt and evolve not merely to gain a reproductive advantage, but simply to survive against ever-evolving co-dependent species in an adversarial ecosystem. A botanist evaluated a specialized orchid species that relies on a single pollinator moth with an exceptionally long proboscis (tongue) to reach its deep nectar wells. Over millennia, fossil and genetic records indicate that whenever mutations caused the moth's proboscis to grow longer, the orchid's nectar wells sequentially evolved to be deeper, preventing the moth from stealing nectar without collecting pollen.

**Q:** Which choice most logically completes the text?

- A) The orchid species developed deep nectar wells to attract a wider variety of pollinator insects to its ecosystem.
- B) The structural modifications of the orchid and the moth represent a co-evolutionary arms race that supports the red queen hypothesis.
- C) The moth species will eventually evolve a short proboscis once the orchid nectar wells hit a maximum structural limit.
- D) Natural selection favors the pollinator moth over the orchid species in all historical maritime ecosystems.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: The item asks which choice "most logically completes the text," but the passage ends with a complete sentence and contains no blank or incomplete lead-in (e.g., "Therefore, the botanist concluded that ___"). SAT inference items require an explicit completion point.
- difficulty_mismatch: Labeled 'hard,' but the correct answer is transparently signaled: the passage defines the red queen hypothesis and then describes reciprocal escalation, while the three distractors contain obvious disqualifiers (unsupported 'wider variety,' speculative future 'will eventually,' and the absurd 'in all historical maritime ecosystems'). This is an easy-level item.

### R&W · Inferences · hard · #3 (q00453)

> A behavioral economist investigated the "endowment effect"—the psychological tendency for individuals to value an object more highly merely because they own it. In an experiment, participants were randomly divided into two groups: Sellers, who were given a luxury coffee mug and asked to set a minimum selling price, and Buyers, who were given cash and asked to set a maximum purchase price. Traditional rational-choice theory predicts that random distribution should result in a near-identical average valuation between buyers and sellers. However, the data revealed that the average minimum price set by Sellers was $8.50, while the average maximum price offered by Buyers was only $3.20.

**Q:** Which choice most logically completes the text?

- A) Luxury coffee mugs possess an inherent, objective market value of exactly $5.85 across all demographics.
- B) The large valuation gap between participants supports the existence of the endowment effect over traditional rational-choice predictions.
- C) Buyers were deliberately trying to manipulate the experiment by offering unrealistically low prices.
- D) Randomly assigning participants to groups completely eliminates all forms of cognitive bias in economic transactions.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: The prompt asks which choice "most logically completes the text," but the passage ends with a complete sentence and no blank or trailing transition (e.g., 'Therefore, ___'). SAT inference items require an incomplete final sentence to be filled in.
- difficulty_mismatch: Labeled 'hard,' but the distractors are obviously flawed (absolute claims like 'exactly,' 'completely eliminates all,' and an unsupported motive claim), making this an easy item.

### R&W · Inferences · hard · #4 (q00454)

> The concept of "functional fixedness" refers to a cognitive bias that limits a person to using an object only in the way it is traditionally used, severely impairing creative problem-solving. In a classic experiment, participants were given a candle, a box of tacks, and a book of matches, and asked to attach the candle to a wooden wall so it would not drip wax onto the table below. Group A received the tacks inside the box, and most failed because they viewed the box only as a container. Group B received the tacks piled on the table next to the empty box; nearly all solved the task quickly by realizing the empty box could be tacked to the wall to serve as a platform for the candle.

**Q:** Which choice most logically completes the text?

- A) Participants in Group B possessed a higher baseline intelligence score than those randomly assigned to Group A.
- B) Presenting an object as a separate, distinct element can mitigate functional fixedness by highlighting its potential utility as a standalone tool.
- C) Group A participants would have solved the task faster if they had been given a larger volume of matches.
- D) Wooden walls are structurally unsuited for experiments involving candle wax and metal tacks.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: The stem asks which choice "most logically completes the text," but the passage ends with a complete sentence and contains no blank (e.g., a concluding 'Thus, the experiment suggests that ____'). SAT completion items require the blank to be present.
- difficulty_mismatch: Labeled 'hard,' but the item is easy: only B restates the passage's logic, while A, B's competitors introduce irrelevant content (baseline intelligence, number of matches, wall structure) that is obviously unsupported. Distractors are not plausible enough for a hard-tier item.

### R&W · Inferences · hard · #5 (q00455)

> A political philosopher examining the "paradox of tolerance" argued that if a society extends unlimited tolerance even to those who are actively intolerant and advocate for the destruction of tolerant institutions, then the tolerant society will eventually be seized and destroyed. The philosopher concluded that to maintain a stable, tolerant society, the system must reserve the structural right to refuse tolerance to philosophies that reject the foundational rules of peaceful democratic coexistence.

**Q:** Which choice most logically completes the text?

- A) Unlimited tolerance is a prerequisite for sustaining long-term peace in any democratic nation.
- B) Preserving a tolerant society may paradoxically require imposing restrictions on groups that refuse to practice tolerance themselves.
- C) Intolerant philosophies will naturally become tolerant if they are given complete freedom to operate within a government.
- D) The paradox of tolerance applies exclusively to ancient historical empires and has no relevance to modern political philosophy.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: The question asks which choice "most logically completes the text," but the passage is a complete, self-contained paragraph with no blank or trailing incomplete sentence. Standard SAT inference items end with a blank (often introduced by a transition like "Therefore,").
- difficulty_mismatch: Labeled 'hard,' but the credited answer is essentially a direct paraphrase of the passage's final sentence, and the three distractors are transparently wrong (one contradicts the passage, one is absurd, one is irrelevant). This is an easy item.

### R&W · Inferences · hard · #6 (q00456)

> In conservation ecology, "trophic cascades" occur when the removal or addition of a top predator triggers massive, alternating shifts in population densities throughout lower levels of a food web. When grey wolves were reintroduced to Yellowstone National Park, they began hunting elk, which had severely overgrazed the local valleys during the wolves' long absence. As elk numbers stabilized and changed their grazing routes to avoid wolf encounters, streamside willow and aspen groves recovered rapidly, providing materials for beavers to build dams, which in turn created deep ponds that supported diverse fish and amphibian populations.

**Q:** Which choice most logically completes the text?

- A) Apex predators can influence physical and biological landscape structures by altering the behaviors and populations of primary herbivores.
- B) Elk populations are fundamentally detrimental to the survival of fish and amphibians across all North American ecosystems.
- C) Beavers are highly aggressive competitors that actively prevent grey wolves from establishing territories near streams.
- D) Reintroducing wolves to an ecosystem invariably reduces the total biodiversity of aquatic habitats over time.

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (high)
- formatting: The item asks which choice 'most logically completes the text,' but the passage ends with a complete sentence and contains no blank or trailing clause (e.g., 'Therefore, ___'). SAT inference items require an incomplete final sentence; as written the task is really 'which choice is best supported' / main idea.
- difficulty_mismatch: Labeled hard, but the three distractors are flagrantly wrong (absolute language: 'all North American ecosystems,' 'invariably reduces,' plus an unsupported claim about beavers blocking wolves). The correct answer is identifiable without close reading, making this an easy item.

### R&W · Inferences · hard · #7 (q00457)

> A materials engineer investigated the "hall-petch relationship," a mechanical model stating that the yield strength of a metal increases as its crystalline grain size decreases, because smaller grains introduce more grain boundaries that block the movement of internal structural defects called dislocations. The engineer synthesized samples of pure copper with grain sizes ranging from 100 micrometers down to 5 nanometers. The data confirmed that yield strength increased continuously until grain size hit 15 nanometers; below this threshold, however, the yield strength crashed abruptly because the grains became so small that they began sliding past one another along their boundaries, a phenomenon known as grain-boundary sliding.

**Q:** Which choice most logically completes the text?

- A) The Hall-Petch relationship remains perfectly linear and accurate across all structural scales down to the single-atom level.
- B) Reducing crystalline grain size increases metal strength indefinitely without any physical or mechanical limitations.
- C) The mechanical mechanism that reinforces metals at micro-scales can be overridden by alternative structural behaviors at extreme nano-scales.
- D) Pure copper is inherently unsuited for industrial applications because its grain boundaries are highly unstable.

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (high)
- formatting: The question asks which choice "most logically completes the text," but the passage ends with a complete sentence and contains no blank or trailing transitional clause (e.g., "Therefore, the engineer concluded that ___"). The item functions as an inference question but is missing the required completion cue.
- other: Minor inconsistency: the relationship is written as lowercase "hall-petch" in the passage but capitalized "Hall-Petch" in choice A.

### R&W · Inferences · hard · #8 (q00458)

> The "linguistic relativity hypothesis" suggests that the structural parameters of a language can shape or constrain the cognitive processing and perceptual maps of its speakers. Linguists evaluated speakers of Language A, which possesses only two primary terms for color mapping ("light" and "dark"), and speakers of Language B, which uses eleven distinct color terms. In a sorting task involving subtle gradients of blue and green chips, speakers of Language B categorized the variations with rapid precision, while Language A speakers grouped them into broad categories based on brightness, taking significantly longer to isolate matching color hues.

**Q:** Which choice most logically completes the text?

- A) Language A speakers are physically color-blind and unable to perceive any variations between blue and green wavelengths.
- B) The experiment provides empirical support for the idea that vocabulary structures can influence the speed and categorization of sensory perceptions.
- C) Language B is objectively superior to Language A for all forms of human communication and artistic expression.
- D) Color categorization performance is entirely determined by genetic differences in eye structure rather than linguistic habits.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: The question asks which choice 'most logically completes the text,' but the passage contains no blank or trailing clause (e.g., 'This suggests that ___'). SAT inference items always end with an incomplete sentence or blank.
- difficulty_mismatch: Labeled 'hard,' but the three distractors are absurd extremes (physical color blindness, language superiority, purely genetic determination), making the correct answer obvious; this is an easy item.

### R&W · Inferences · hard · #20 (q00470)

> The concept of the "overjustification effect" in motivational psychology holds that introducing external rewards for an activity a person already finds intrinsically enjoyable can undermine that intrinsic motivation. Early experiments offered children unexpected rewards for drawing, an activity they had freely chosen; subsequent observation found that those children drew less spontaneously after the reward was removed than children who had received no reward. Theorists explained this as a shift in the individual's attribution of their own behavior: where previously they drew because they wanted to, they now inferred they had drawn because they were paid, and without payment, the activity lost its pull. Critics have pointed out, however, that the effect is highly context-dependent: unexpected rewards, informational feedback framed as rewards, and performance-contingent rewards have markedly different effects, complicating any simple generalization.

**Q:** Based on the passage, which inference about intrinsic motivation is most strongly supported?

- A) Any introduction of an external reward for a freely chosen activity will reliably destroy the intrinsic motivation to engage in that activity.
- B) The overjustification effect demonstrates that humans are fundamentally incapable of maintaining intrinsic interest in activities they associate with external compensation.
- C) The overjustification effect likely depends on how individuals interpret the reward and its relationship to their behavior, rather than on the simple presence or absence of reward.
- D) Because the effect is context-dependent, the concept of intrinsic motivation has been shown to be psychologically meaningless and should be discarded.

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (high)
- difficulty_mismatch: Labeled 'hard,' but the three incorrect choices are all extreme overstatements ('any,' 'fundamentally incapable,' 'meaningless and should be discarded'), making the correct inference identifiable without close reading. This plays more like an easy/medium item.

### R&W · Words in Context · medium-low · #20 (q00530)

> Sleep researchers have challenged the widespread assumption that eight hours of sleep is a universal requirement. Their studies suggest that sleep needs vary considerably across individuals due to genetic factors, age, and lifestyle. A person who consistently feels alert and functions well on six hours may have a legitimate biological basis for that pattern, while another individual may genuinely require nine hours to achieve the same level of cognitive performance.

**Q:** As used in the passage, "considerable" most nearly means

- A) thoughtful
- B) admirable
- C) significant
- D) slight

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (high)
- formatting: The question quotes "considerable," but the passage actually contains the adverb "considerably." The quoted form should match the text exactly (SAT convention).

### R&W · Words in Context · medium-high · #1 (q00541)

> The senator's speech was widely described as incendiary, drawing sharp rebukes from colleagues on both sides of the aisle who felt she had deliberately stoked tensions rather than sought common ground. Her supporters, however, maintained that her remarks were a necessary provocation, designed to force a conversation that the chamber had too long deferred.

**Q:** As used in the text, what does the word "incendiary" most nearly mean?

- A) Carefully reasoned and evidence-based
- B) Likely to cause strong anger or conflict
- C) Legally actionable
- D) Emotionally moving but ultimately ineffective

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- difficulty_mismatch: The item is considerably easier than a medium-high Words in Context question: the context ('deliberately stoked tensions,' 'provocation') points directly to the answer, and the distractors are weak (A is the opposite, C is unrelated to the context, D is contradicted by the supporters' claim of necessity).

### R&W · Words in Context · medium-high · #4 (q00544)

> Literary scholars have long debated whether the ambiguity in the novel's ending is deliberate or simply the result of hasty composition. The author left no letters or journals that might settle the question. Some argue the unresolved conclusion is the novel's crowning achievement; others find it an unsatisfying abdication of the author's responsibility to her readers.

**Q:** As used in the text, what does the word "abdication" most nearly mean?

- A) A formal announcement made to a public audience
- B) An abandonment of a duty or responsibility
- C) A creative technique used to build suspense
- D) A legal transfer of rights to another party

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- difficulty_mismatch: The sentence already supplies 'of the author's responsibility to her readers,' so the intended meaning is handed to the student; combined with the fact that only B mentions duty/responsibility, the item plays closer to easy than medium-high.

### R&W · Words in Context · medium-high · #6 (q00546)

> Although the theory had dominated the field for over three decades, a series of anomalous findings began to accumulate that could not be explained within its framework. At first, researchers attributed these discrepancies to measurement error. As the evidence mounted, however, a growing number of scientists began to question whether the theory itself needed to be fundamentally revised.

**Q:** As used in the text, what does the word "anomalous" most nearly mean?

- A) Statistically significant and widely replicated
- B) Deviating from what is expected or standard
- C) Produced by flawed experimental methods
- D) Consistent with earlier theoretical predictions

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- difficulty_mismatch: The item is labeled medium-high, but 'anomalous' is a common word whose definition ('deviating from the standard') is directly signaled by the context ('could not be explained within its framework'), and the distractors are not closely competitive. This plays as an easy item.

### R&W · Words in Context · medium-high · #13 (q00553)

> Virginia Woolf's essay "Modern Fiction" challenges the conventions of Edwardian novels, which she accuses of fixating on the external trappings of life — the furniture, the appointments, the social calendar — while neglecting the interior flux of consciousness. For Woolf, the truly significant moments of human experience are not the grand, legible events but the "luminous halo" of impressions surrounding them. She argues that the novelist's task is not to construct a well-carpenterd plot but to capture the irregular, flickering quality of the mind as it moves through ordinary time.

**Q:** As used in the passage, "legible" most nearly means

- A) written down or recorded
- B) easy to read aloud
- C) clearly defined and interpretable
- D) morally instructive

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (high)
- formatting: Typo in the passage: "well-carpenterd" should be "well-carpentered." It does not affect the answer but should be corrected.

### R&W · Words in Context · hard · #24 (q00594)

> Literary critics who study the gothic tradition have often described its landscapes as
> "projective" — the moors, dungeons, and ruined abbeys do not merely provide atmosphere
> but externalize the psychological states of the characters who move through them.
> What is less often noted is that this projection runs in both directions: the landscape
> also shapes the characters who inhabit it, so that the boundary between psyche
> and setting becomes genuinely unstable. To call the gothic landscape merely a
> backdrop is to misread the form's most essential claim about interiority.

**Q:** As used in the passage, "projective" most nearly means

- A) expressive
- B) symbolic
- C) reflective
- D) externalizing

**Key:** D · **Reviewer:** D (high)
- difficulty_mismatch: The passage literally supplies the definition in the appositive clause ("externalize the psychological states"), and choice D is a near-verbatim restatement ("externalizing"). This makes the item considerably easier than a 'hard' Words in Context question, which typically requires inference rather than lexical matching.

### R&W · Text Structure and Purpose · easy · #30 (q00630)

> Crows are among the most intelligent birds in the world. Scientists have observed them using sticks as tools to reach food in hard-to-access places, solving multi-step puzzles, and even dropping nuts onto roads so that cars will crack them open. Crows also have excellent memories and can recognize individual human faces, remembering people who have treated them well or poorly for years. These behaviors suggest a level of problem-solving ability that rivals many mammals and surprises even experienced animal researchers.

**Q:** The primary purpose of this passage is to

- A) to warn readers that crows can be aggressive toward humans who disturb them
- B) to explain why crows are considered intelligent by describing specific behaviors they display
- C) to compare the intelligence of crows to that of parrots and other smart birds
- D) to argue that crows should be protected by law because of their unusual abilities

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: The stem ends with "is to" while every choice also begins with "to," producing a redundant "to to" when read together. Choices should omit the leading "to" (or the stem should end with "is").

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · easy · #1 (q00721)

> Regular aerobic exercise has well-documented benefits for cardiovascular health. Studies consistently show that individuals who exercise at least three times per week have lower rates of heart disease, reduced blood pressure, and improved cholesterol levels compared to sedentary individuals. Doctors widely recommend moderate aerobic activity as one of the most effective preventive health measures available.
> Passage 2:
> While exercise is often promoted as a cure-all, its benefits depend heavily on an individual's baseline health. For people already managing chronic conditions such as joint disease or heart failure, unsupervised vigorous exercise can cause harm rather than benefit. Medical supervision is essential before beginning any new exercise program, particularly for older adults.

**Q:** Based on the texts, how would the author of Passage 2 most likely respond to the recommendation made in Passage 1?

- A) By arguing that aerobic exercise has no proven cardiovascular benefits
- B) By agreeing with the recommendation but adding that it should be applied with care depending on individual health circumstances
- C) By suggesting that exercise should be replaced entirely by medication for heart disease prevention
- D) By claiming that only younger adults benefit from aerobic exercise

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: The first passage is not labeled (no "Passage 1"/"Text 1" header) while the second is labeled "Passage 2," which is slightly inconsistent, though the structure remains clear.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · easy · #2 (q00722)

> Zoos play a vital role in wildlife conservation. By maintaining breeding populations of endangered species, they serve as insurance against extinction. Many animals that would have vanished from the wild entirely — including the Arabian oryx and the California condor — have been saved through zoo-based breeding programs and reintroduction efforts.
> Passage 2:
> Zoos, however well-intentioned, cannot substitute for the preservation of wild habitats. Animals kept in captivity often suffer from stress, limited space, and inability to perform natural behaviors. Conservation funding directed toward zoo operations might achieve greater impact if redirected to habitat protection and anti-poaching efforts in the regions where endangered species actually live.

**Q:** Which of the following best describes the relationship between the two passages?

- A) Passage 2 fully agrees with Passage 1 and adds supporting evidence.
- B) Passage 2 challenges Passage 1's claim by questioning zoos' effectiveness and proposing an alternative approach.
- C) Passage 2 focuses on a different subject than Passage 1.
- D) Passage 2 provides historical context for the argument made in Passage 1.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: The first passage is not labeled 'Passage 1' (it appears only under the generic 'Passage / text' heading), while the second is labeled; the missing label is a minor presentation flaw but does not impede comprehension.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · easy · #3 (q00723)

> Reading fiction develops empathy. When readers inhabit the perspectives of characters whose lives differ from their own, they practice imagining how others think and feel. Researchers have found that people who read literary fiction score higher on tests measuring the ability to understand others' mental states — a skill known as theory of mind.
> Passage 2:
> Books of all kinds expand the mind. Whether through biography, history, science writing, or fiction, reading exposes people to new ideas, perspectives, and ways of understanding the world. The habit of regular reading is one of the strongest predictors of intellectual curiosity and openness to new experiences.

**Q:** How do the two passages relate to each other?

- A) Passage 1 makes a specific claim about one benefit of fiction that is consistent with the broader argument of Passage 2.
- B) Passage 1 contradicts the main point of Passage 2 by focusing only on fiction.
- C) Passage 2 directly refutes the research findings cited in Passage 1.
- D) The two passages argue for completely opposite conclusions about the value of reading.

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (high)
- formatting: The first passage is not labeled 'Passage 1:' while the second is labeled 'Passage 2:', which is inconsistent though unlikely to confuse students.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · easy · #4 (q00724)

> Social media platforms have made it easier than ever for people to stay connected with friends and family. Users can share life updates, coordinate events, and maintain relationships across geographic distances that would have made regular contact nearly impossible in earlier generations. For many people, platforms like these are a genuine source of community and belonging.
> Passage 2:
> Despite their surface sociability, social media platforms frequently leave users feeling more isolated than before. Studies have linked heavy social media use to increased feelings of loneliness and lower life satisfaction, particularly among young people. Curated highlight reels and the pressure of public performance create a form of connection that is wide but shallow.

**Q:** Based on the texts, the authors of Passage 1 and Passage 2 would most likely disagree about which of the following?

- A) Whether social media platforms are used by large numbers of people
- B) Whether the connections formed through social media contribute positively to users' wellbeing
- C) Whether geographic distance makes friendship more difficult to maintain
- D) Whether young people use social media more than older adults

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: Passage 1 is not labeled (only 'Passage 2' is marked), though the division is still inferable from the text.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · easy · #5 (q00725)

> The invention of the printing press in the fifteenth century revolutionized the spread of knowledge. By making books affordable and widely available, Gutenberg's press broke the monopoly that clergy and scholars had held over literacy and learning. Ideas could now travel faster and farther than ever before, laying the groundwork for the Renaissance and the Scientific Revolution.
> Passage 2:
> The internet has done for the twenty-first century what the printing press did for the fifteenth. Both technologies dramatically lowered the cost of sharing information and disrupted existing gatekeepers of knowledge. Just as the press challenged the authority of the church and universities, the internet has challenged the authority of established media, governments, and academic institutions.

**Q:** Which of the following best describes how the author of Passage 2 uses the information in Passage 1?

- A) As a counterexample that weakens the argument about the internet
- B) As a historical parallel to strengthen the claim about the internet's significance
- C) As evidence that the internet is less transformative than the printing press
- D) As a source of data about fifteenth-century literacy rates

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: The first passage is not labeled 'Passage 1' (it appears under a generic 'Passage / text' header), which is a minor inconsistency with the labeled 'Passage 2'.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · easy · #6 (q00726)

> Homework is essential for academic success. Practicing skills outside the classroom reinforces what students learn during the school day and helps them develop the discipline and time-management abilities they will need throughout their education and careers.
> Passage 2:
> Research on the effectiveness of homework tells a more complicated story than many educators assume. Studies show that homework has a positive relationship with achievement at the high school level, but little to no measurable benefit for elementary school students. Assigning homework to young children may create stress without academic payoff.

**Q:** Based on the texts, the author of Passage 2 would most likely respond to the claim in Passage 1 by pointing out that it:

- A) Overlooks the role of parental involvement in completing homework
- B) Applies a generalization that the evidence does not support equally across all age groups
- C) Underestimates the amount of homework currently assigned in schools
- D) Fails to consider the importance of classroom instruction

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: The first text is not labeled 'Passage 1' (only 'Passage 2' is labeled), which is a minor formatting inconsistency, though context makes it clear.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · easy · #7 (q00727)

> Space exploration is one of humanity's greatest achievements and should continue to receive robust public funding. The technologies developed for space missions — from satellites to medical imaging — have transformed everyday life on Earth. Exploring the universe also inspires future generations of scientists and engineers.
> Passage 2:
> Public funds invested in space exploration could address urgent problems closer to home. Millions of people lack access to clean water, adequate healthcare, and stable food supplies. Before we spend billions sending probes to distant planets, we should ensure that basic human needs are met on this one.

**Q:** Which of the following best describes the relationship between the two passages?

- A) Passage 2 agrees with Passage 1 that space exploration has produced valuable technologies.
- B) Passage 2 disputes the scientific value of space exploration entirely.
- C) Passage 2 accepts that space exploration has value but questions whether it should be a funding priority given unmet human needs.
- D) The two passages discuss entirely different uses of government funding and do not address the same issue.

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (high)
- formatting: The first text is not labeled "Passage 1" (only "Passage 2" is labeled), though the question refers to two passages. Minor, but inconsistent.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · easy · #9 (q00729)

> Artists throughout history have drawn on their personal suffering as creative fuel. Many of the most enduring works of literature, music, and visual art were created by individuals who experienced significant hardship, loss, or psychological struggle. There appears to be a deep connection between emotional pain and artistic depth.
> Passage 2:
> The idea that suffering is necessary for artistic greatness is a myth that has caused real harm. It romanticizes mental illness, discourages artists from seeking help, and ignores the vast body of great work created from positions of stability, joy, or contentment. Many celebrated artists created their best work during periods of relative peace in their lives.

**Q:** Based on the texts, how would the author of Passage 2 most likely characterize the claim made in Passage 1?

- A) As a well-supported finding that should inform how we train artists
- B) As a misleading overgeneralization with potentially harmful consequences
- C) As an interesting hypothesis that requires further empirical research
- D) As a claim that applies to visual artists but not to writers or musicians

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: The first text lacks a "Text 1"/"Passage 1" label, though the second is labeled "Passage 2"; standard SAT cross-text items label both texts.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · easy · #10 (q00730)

> Urban green spaces — parks, community gardens, and tree-lined streets — provide measurable benefits to city residents. Studies show that access to green space reduces stress, lowers rates of depression and anxiety, and encourages physical activity. Cities that invest in parks are investing in the mental and physical health of their populations.
> Passage 2:
> Green space alone is not sufficient to improve urban health outcomes. Research shows that the health benefits of parks are distributed unequally: residents of wealthier neighborhoods enjoy more and higher-quality green space, while lower-income communities often have less access to safe, well-maintained parks. Without intentional investment in underserved areas, green space can reinforce rather than reduce health inequities.

**Q:** Based on the texts, both authors would most likely agree that:

- A) All cities currently provide equal access to green space for all residents
- B) Green space can contribute positively to the health of urban residents
- C) The health benefits of parks are limited to physical fitness
- D) Urban planners should reduce spending on parks and prioritize other infrastructure

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: The first passage is labeled only 'Passage / text' rather than 'Passage 1,' which is slightly inconsistent with the 'Passage 2' label, though meaning remains clear.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · easy · #12 (q00732)

> Regular physical exercise provides well-documented benefits for cardiovascular health. Studies consistently show that people who engage in aerobic activities such as jogging, swimming, or cycling experience lower rates of heart disease. Exercise strengthens the heart muscle, improves circulation, and helps maintain healthy blood pressure levels. Health organizations around the world recommend at least 150 minutes of moderate aerobic activity per week for adults.
> Passage 2:
> Physicians increasingly emphasize that mental health benefits are among the most compelling reasons to exercise regularly. Physical activity stimulates the release of endorphins, which reduce feelings of stress and anxiety. Research shows that individuals who exercise consistently report lower rates of depression than those who lead sedentary lifestyles. For many patients, a structured exercise routine can be as effective as medication in managing mild to moderate depression.

**Q:** Based on the passages, how do the two authors' views on the benefits of exercise compare?

- A) Both authors agree that exercise is beneficial, but they focus on different types of benefits.
- B) The authors disagree about whether exercise is beneficial for adults.
- C) Passage 1 claims exercise is harmful, while Passage 2 claims it is helpful.
- D) Both passages focus exclusively on the cardiovascular benefits of aerobic exercise.

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (high)
- formatting: The first passage is labeled only 'Passage / text:' rather than 'Passage 1,' though the question and second label make the structure clear.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · easy · #15 (q00735)

> Wolves were reintroduced to Yellowstone National Park in 1995 after being absent for decades. Their return had dramatic effects on the ecosystem. Elk herds, which had grown very large and overgrazed riverbanks, began to change their behavior to avoid wolves. This allowed vegetation along rivers to recover, which in turn stabilized riverbanks and improved habitats for fish and birds. Scientists call this type of chain reaction a trophic cascade.
> Passage 2:
> Not all ecologists fully agree that wolf reintroduction alone caused the dramatic ecological changes observed at Yellowstone. Some researchers point out that other factors, including drought cycles and changes in human activity in the park, may have contributed to vegetation recovery. While wolves clearly have an impact on prey behavior, the extent to which they drove the trophic cascade remains a subject of ongoing scientific debate.

**Q:** How does Passage 2 respond to the claims made in Passage 1?

- A) Passage 2 agrees completely with Passage 1 and adds more evidence in support of wolf reintroduction.
- B) Passage 2 argues that wolves should be removed from Yellowstone again.
- C) Passage 2 questions whether wolf reintroduction alone fully explains the ecological changes described in Passage 1.
- D) Passage 2 denies that wolves have any impact on prey behavior.

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (high)
- formatting: The first passage is labeled 'Passage / text' rather than 'Passage 1,' which is slightly inconsistent with the question's reference to 'Passage 1' (though the meaning remains clear).

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · easy · #16 (q00736)

> Solar energy has become one of the fastest-growing sources of electricity in the world. Advances in photovoltaic technology have made solar panels significantly cheaper over the past two decades. Many homeowners and businesses now install solar panels to reduce their electricity bills and lower their carbon footprints. Governments in many countries offer tax incentives to encourage the adoption of solar energy.
> Passage 2:
> Despite its growth, solar energy faces real limitations that must be addressed. Solar panels only generate electricity when the sun is shining, which means energy storage systems such as batteries are necessary to provide power at night or on cloudy days. Battery storage technology is improving but remains expensive. Critics argue that until storage costs fall substantially, solar energy alone cannot reliably serve as a primary energy source for most regions.

**Q:** Based on the two passages, which statement best describes the relationship between their views on solar energy?

- A) Passage 1 presents solar energy positively, while Passage 2 identifies challenges that still need to be overcome.
- B) Both passages conclude that solar energy is currently too expensive to be practical.
- C) Passage 2 argues that solar energy has no future as an energy source.
- D) Passage 1 focuses on the limitations of solar energy, while Passage 2 focuses on its benefits.

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (high)
- formatting: The first text is not labeled 'Passage 1:' while the second is labeled 'Passage 2:', a minor inconsistency in presentation (the question refers to 'Passage 1').

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · easy · #17 (q00737)

> Ancient Greek philosophers believed that the Earth was the center of the universe, with the sun, moon, and stars revolving around it. This geocentric model seemed to match everyday observation — after all, the sun appears to rise and set as if it moves around the Earth. The model was so widely accepted that it remained the dominant view of the cosmos for nearly two thousand years.
> Passage 2:
> In the sixteenth century, Nicolaus Copernicus proposed that the sun, not the Earth, was at the center of the solar system. His heliocentric model was controversial because it contradicted both religious teaching and centuries of scientific tradition. Eventually, the work of Galileo, Kepler, and Newton provided strong evidence supporting the heliocentric model, and the geocentric view was abandoned by the scientific community.

**Q:** How do the two passages relate to each other in terms of their subject matter?

- A) Passage 1 describes the geocentric model, and Passage 2 describes how that model was eventually replaced by the heliocentric model.
- B) Both passages argue that the geocentric model remains scientifically valid today.
- C) Passage 2 argues that Copernicus was wrong about the position of the sun.
- D) Passage 1 and Passage 2 focus on entirely unrelated topics.

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (high)
- formatting: The first passage is not labeled 'Passage 1' (it appears directly under 'Passage / text'), while the second is labeled 'Passage 2'. The label should be present for parallel structure, though the question remains answerable.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · easy · #18 (q00738)

> Libraries have long served as important community spaces where people can access books, research materials, and educational resources at no cost. They provide essential services to students, job seekers, and lifelong learners who may not be able to afford books or internet access. Librarians help patrons find information, navigate databases, and develop research skills. For many communities, the local library remains a vital public institution.
> Passage 2:
> In recent years, libraries have expanded their roles far beyond lending books. Many modern libraries offer makerspaces equipped with 3D printers, recording studios, and woodworking tools. Others host job training workshops, tax preparation assistance, and mental health resource centers. Libraries are reinventing themselves as dynamic community hubs that respond to the changing needs of the populations they serve.

**Q:** How does Passage 2 build upon the ideas presented in Passage 1?

- A) Passage 2 argues that traditional libraries are outdated and should be closed.
- B) Passage 2 extends the idea in Passage 1 that libraries serve communities by describing new and expanded services libraries now offer.
- C) Passage 2 contradicts Passage 1 by arguing that libraries no longer provide access to books.
- D) Both passages focus solely on the role of librarians in helping patrons find information.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: The first passage is not labeled "Passage 1" (the heading reads only "Passage / text:"), which is inconsistent with the paired-passage format and with the label given to Passage 2.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · easy · #19 (q00739)

> Migration of monarch butterflies is one of nature's most remarkable phenomena. Each autumn, millions of monarchs travel thousands of miles from Canada and the United States to overwintering sites in central Mexico. The butterflies navigate using the sun as a compass and rely on an internal time-compensated mechanism to adjust for the sun's movement throughout the day. Scientists marvel at how such a small insect can complete such a long and precise journey.
> Passage 2:
> Monarch butterfly populations have declined dramatically over the past several decades. Habitat loss due to agricultural expansion has reduced milkweed, the only plant on which monarchs lay their eggs and which their caterpillars eat. Pesticide use has further reduced milkweed availability and directly harmed butterflies. Conservation organizations are working to restore milkweed habitats along migration routes to help the species recover.

**Q:** How do the two passages relate to each other in their treatment of monarch butterflies?

- A) Passage 1 focuses on the impressive biology and behavior of monarchs, while Passage 2 focuses on threats to their survival.
- B) Both passages argue that monarch butterfly populations are currently stable and thriving.
- C) Passage 2 argues that the migration described in Passage 1 no longer occurs.
- D) Passage 1 focuses on threats to monarchs, while Passage 2 focuses on their remarkable migration.

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (high)
- formatting: The first passage is not labeled 'Passage 1' (the header is missing), though the question and choices refer to 'Passage 1'. Minor but worth fixing.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · easy · #20 (q00740)

> In the nineteenth century, many cities began building public parks to give urban residents access to green space. Designers like Frederick Law Olmsted believed that exposure to nature within cities was essential for the health and well-being of residents. Parks such as New York's Central Park were designed to feel like natural landscapes, with winding paths, lakes, and meadows that provided an escape from dense urban environments.
> Passage 2:
> Today, urban green spaces serve purposes that go beyond recreation and aesthetic pleasure. Research shows that trees and plants in cities reduce air pollution, lower ambient temperatures, and decrease storm water runoff. City planners now treat green infrastructure — including parks, green roofs, and planted corridors — as essential tools for managing the environmental challenges of urban living.

**Q:** Both passages discuss urban green spaces, but they differ primarily in what way?

- A) Passage 1 opposes the creation of urban parks, while Passage 2 supports them.
- B) Passage 1 focuses on the historical and human-centered reasons for urban parks, while Passage 2 focuses on their modern environmental functions.
- C) Both passages focus exclusively on the design of Central Park in New York City.
- D) Passage 2 argues that parks are no longer useful because cities have other green infrastructure.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: The first passage is not labeled 'Passage 1' (only 'Passage 2' is labeled), which could briefly confuse students, though context makes it clear.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · easy · #21 (q00741)

> Many educators argue that learning a musical instrument during childhood provides cognitive benefits that extend beyond music. Studies have found that children who receive music instruction perform better in reading and mathematics than peers who do not. Researchers believe that music training strengthens memory, attention, and the ability to process patterns — skills that transfer to academic subjects. Some schools have responded by expanding music programs in early grades.
> Passage 2:
> While music education has its advocates, some researchers urge caution in interpreting the studies that link music training to academic performance. Many of these studies do not account for the fact that families who enroll children in music lessons may also provide other enriching educational experiences at home. It is possible that the apparent academic benefits of music training reflect these broader advantages rather than music instruction itself.

**Q:** How does the author of Passage 2 respond to the argument presented in Passage 1?

- A) The author of Passage 2 agrees that music training directly causes improvements in reading and mathematics.
- B) The author of Passage 2 argues that music has no value for children whatsoever.
- C) The author of Passage 2 questions whether the academic benefits attributed to music training are caused by music itself or by other factors.
- D) The author of Passage 2 argues that schools should eliminate music programs entirely.

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (high)
- formatting: The first passage is not labeled "Text 1"/"Passage 1" while the second is labeled "Passage 2"; the missing header is a minor presentation inconsistency (though the question's reference to Passage 1 still makes the intent clear).

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · easy · #22 (q00742)

> The Amazon rainforest is often called the "lungs of the Earth" because it absorbs enormous amounts of carbon dioxide and produces oxygen. The forest is home to an estimated ten percent of all species on Earth and plays a critical role in regulating the global climate. Deforestation in the Amazon threatens not only local biodiversity but also has consequences for weather patterns worldwide.
> Passage 2:
> Indigenous communities have lived in the Amazon for thousands of years and have developed deep knowledge of the forest's plants, animals, and ecosystems. Research shows that areas of the Amazon managed by indigenous peoples have lower rates of deforestation than surrounding regions. Many conservationists now argue that supporting indigenous land rights is one of the most effective strategies for protecting the Amazon rainforest.

**Q:** How do the two passages relate to each other in their discussion of the Amazon rainforest?

- A) Passage 1 describes why the Amazon is ecologically important, and Passage 2 identifies a human-centered approach to protecting it.
- B) Both passages argue that deforestation in the Amazon has already been stopped.
- C) Passage 2 contradicts Passage 1 by arguing that the Amazon is not ecologically important.
- D) Passage 1 focuses on indigenous communities, while Passage 2 focuses on the Amazon's role in the global climate.

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (high)
- formatting: The first passage is not labeled "Passage 1" (only "Passage 2" is labeled), though the question and answer choices refer to "Passage 1." Minor but should be fixed.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · easy · #24 (q00744)

> Space exploration has driven the development of technologies that have improved everyday life on Earth. Innovations originally developed for NASA missions, including memory foam, water filtration systems, and scratch-resistant lenses, have been adapted for commercial use. Proponents of space exploration argue that the investment in space programs yields significant returns in the form of technological progress that benefits the broader public.
> Passage 2:
> Critics of large-scale government space programs argue that the billions of dollars spent on space exploration could be directed toward urgent problems on Earth, such as poverty, disease, and climate change. While acknowledging that space exploration produces some useful technologies, these critics maintain that the benefits do not justify the enormous costs when so many immediate human needs remain unmet.

**Q:** How do the two passages differ in their views on government investment in space exploration?

- A) Passage 1 supports government investment in space exploration by pointing to its technological benefits, while Passage 2 questions that investment by arguing the money could address more pressing earthly problems.
- B) Both passages argue that space exploration is not worth the financial cost.
- C) Passage 1 argues that space exploration is too expensive, while Passage 2 argues it is affordable.
- D) Passage 2 argues that no useful technologies have come from space exploration.

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (high)
- formatting: The first passage is not labeled ("Passage 1:" header is missing), while the second is labeled "Passage 2:". Minor, but it could momentarily confuse students.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · easy · #26 (q00746)

> William Shakespeare's plays have been performed continuously for more than four centuries, making him one of the most enduring figures in literary history. His works explore universal themes such as ambition, jealousy, love, and betrayal that resonate with audiences across cultures and time periods. Shakespeare's influence on the English language itself has been profound — he is credited with coining hundreds of words and phrases still used today.
> Passage 2:
> Some literary scholars argue that the persistent focus on Shakespeare in English curricula comes at a cost. When educators spend significant time on one playwright from sixteenth-century England, students may have fewer opportunities to encounter works by writers from other cultures, time periods, and backgrounds. These scholars advocate for a broader literary canon that exposes students to a more diverse range of voices and perspectives.

**Q:** Based on the two passages, what is the key difference in how the authors view Shakespeare's place in education?

- A) Passage 1 implies Shakespeare's lasting importance, while Passage 2 questions whether that importance justifies the amount of focus he receives in school curricula.
- B) Both passages agree that Shakespeare should be removed from school curricula immediately.
- C) Passage 2 argues that Shakespeare's plays are of poor literary quality.
- D) Passage 1 argues that Shakespeare should be replaced by writers from other cultures.

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (high)
- formatting: The first passage is not labeled (only "Passage 2:" appears), which is inconsistent with standard paired-text formatting.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · easy · #27 (q00747)

> Fast fashion — the rapid production of inexpensive clothing designed to follow the latest trends — has made fashionable clothing accessible to consumers who could not previously afford it. Lower prices allow people across income levels to participate in fashion culture and express their personal style. Retailers argue that fast fashion has democratized the clothing industry.
> Passage 2:
> Environmentalists have raised serious concerns about the impact of fast fashion on the planet. The fast fashion industry is one of the largest sources of water pollution and textile waste globally. Garments produced cheaply are often discarded after just a few wearings, contributing to overflowing landfills. Critics argue that the low prices of fast fashion do not reflect the true environmental and social costs of production.

**Q:** How do the two passages present different perspectives on fast fashion?

- A) Passage 1 focuses on the consumer accessibility benefits of fast fashion, while Passage 2 focuses on its environmental costs.
- B) Both passages conclude that fast fashion is harmful to the environment and should be banned.
- C) Passage 1 argues that fast fashion is environmentally friendly, while Passage 2 argues it is not.
- D) Passage 2 argues that fast fashion has made clothing less affordable for most consumers.

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (high)
- formatting: The first passage lacks a 'Passage 1:' label while the second is labeled 'Passage 2:', which is inconsistent with standard paired-passage formatting (though context makes it clear).

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · easy · #28 (q00748)

> Vaccines work by training the immune system to recognize and fight specific pathogens without causing the disease itself. By introducing a weakened or inactive form of a pathogen — or instructions for making a harmless protein associated with it — vaccines prepare the body to mount a rapid defense if it later encounters the real infection. Widespread vaccination in a population can create herd immunity, protecting even those who cannot be vaccinated.
> Passage 2:
> Despite the proven effectiveness of vaccines, vaccine hesitancy remains a significant public health challenge. Misinformation spread through social media has led some individuals to distrust vaccine safety, even when scientific consensus strongly supports their use. Public health officials have responded by increasing outreach efforts, partnering with community leaders, and working to provide accurate information in ways that are accessible and culturally appropriate.

**Q:** How do the two passages relate to each other in their discussion of vaccines?

- A) Passage 1 explains how vaccines work scientifically, while Passage 2 discusses the social challenges of encouraging vaccine uptake.
- B) Both passages argue that vaccines are ineffective against most diseases.
- C) Passage 2 argues that vaccines are dangerous and should not be used.
- D) Passage 1 focuses on public resistance to vaccines, while Passage 2 focuses on how vaccines function in the body.

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (high)
- formatting: The first passage is not labeled 'Text 1'/'Passage 1' even though the second is labeled 'Passage 2' and the question/choices refer to 'Passage 1'; the missing header is a minor formatting inconsistency.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · easy · #29 (q00749)

> Zoos have long justified their existence on educational grounds. By allowing people to observe animals up close, zoos give the public an opportunity to develop appreciation for wildlife and learn about species from around the world. Many zoos also conduct conservation programs, including captive breeding of endangered species. Supporters argue that these institutions play an important role in wildlife education and species preservation.
> Passage 2:
> Animal rights advocates argue that even well-maintained zoos cannot meet the complex physical and psychological needs of wild animals. Large mammals such as elephants and orcas require vast ranges and complex social environments that captivity cannot replicate. Studies have documented stress-related behaviors in zoo animals, including repetitive pacing and self-harm. These advocates argue that conservation goals are better achieved through habitat preservation and wildlife sanctuaries than through zoos.

**Q:** How do the two passages differ in their views on zoos?

- A) Passage 1 argues zoos serve educational and conservation purposes, while Passage 2 argues that captivity causes harm to animals and that conservation could be better achieved by other means.
- B) Both passages conclude that zoos are necessary for the survival of all endangered species.
- C) Passage 2 argues that zoos are only harmful to small animals, not large mammals.
- D) Passage 1 argues that zoos should be replaced by wildlife sanctuaries immediately.

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (high)
- formatting: The first passage is not labeled 'Passage 1' (it appears only under the generic 'Passage / text' heading), while the second is labeled 'Passage 2'. Minor, but inconsistent with College Board paired-passage formatting.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · easy · #30 (q00750)

> Meditation, once associated mainly with religious and spiritual traditions, has entered mainstream healthcare as a tool for managing stress and improving mental health. Clinical studies have found that mindfulness meditation can reduce symptoms of anxiety, depression, and chronic pain. Many hospitals and clinics now offer mindfulness programs to patients as a complement to conventional medical treatment. The practice has attracted growing interest from both patients and healthcare professionals.
> Passage 2:
> Some researchers caution that the enthusiasm for meditation in clinical settings has outpaced the evidence. While early studies showed promising results, more rigorous research with larger sample sizes and control groups has produced more modest findings. Critics point out that the benefits of meditation, while real for some individuals, may be smaller and less universal than media reports suggest. They urge careful evaluation of meditation alongside other evidence-based treatments rather than wholesale adoption.

**Q:** Based on the two passages, how do the authors differ in their perspectives on meditation in healthcare?

- A) Passage 1 presents meditation's entry into healthcare positively, while Passage 2 urges caution about overestimating the strength of the evidence supporting it.
- B) Both passages argue that meditation has no proven benefits and should not be used in healthcare.
- C) Passage 2 argues that meditation is harmful to patients and should be banned from clinical settings.
- D) Passage 1 argues that meditation is only effective for religious practitioners, not for medical patients.

**Key:** A · **Reviewer:** A (high)
- formatting: The first passage is introduced only as 'Passage / text:' with no 'Passage 1' label, while the second is labeled 'Passage 2'. Minor, since the question refers to Passage 1.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · medium-low · #1 (q00751)

> Standardized testing provides an objective measure of student achievement that is immune to the subjective biases inherent in teacher grading. A student's grade from a particular teacher reflects not just their knowledge but also factors like class participation, effort, and the teacher's personal impressions. Standardized tests, by contrast, apply the same criteria uniformly across all students, providing a more reliable basis for comparison.
> Passage 2:
> The claim that standardized tests are objective is itself misleading. Tests are designed by humans who make choices about what knowledge to assess, how to phrase questions, and which skills to prioritize — choices that inevitably reflect certain cultural assumptions and educational values. Students from backgrounds that match those assumptions tend to perform better, not because they know more, but because the test was implicitly designed around their experience.

**Q:** Based on the texts, how would the author of Passage 2 most likely respond to the author of Passage 1's claim that standardized tests apply "the same criteria uniformly"?

- A) By agreeing that uniform criteria make tests fairer than teacher grades
- B) By arguing that uniform criteria are valuable but need to be updated more frequently
- C) By contending that applying criteria uniformly does not make those criteria free from cultural bias
- D) By suggesting that standardized tests should be replaced by student portfolios

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (high)
- formatting: The first passage lacks a 'Passage 1' (or 'Text 1') heading even though the second is labeled 'Passage 2' and the question refers to 'Passage 1'; this is a minor labeling inconsistency.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · medium-low · #3 (q00753)

> The decline of print journalism has had serious consequences for local accountability. Investigative reporters at local newspapers once monitored city councils, school boards, and regional courts — an unglamorous but essential function that few digital outlets have replicated at the local level. When local newsrooms close, corruption and mismanagement often go unreported and unchecked.
> Passage 2:
> While it is true that print journalism has declined, new forms of local accountability journalism have emerged to fill some of the gap. Nonprofit investigative newsrooms, community newsletters, and local podcasts now cover many of the stories that traditional papers once did. The landscape has changed, but the assumption that accountability journalism is disappearing overstates the loss.

**Q:** Based on the texts, the author of Passage 2 would most likely respond to Passage 1 by arguing that Passage 1:

- A) Correctly identifies a serious problem with no viable solution
- B) Overstates the decline by failing to acknowledge new forms of local journalism that have emerged
- C) Underestimates the quality of reporting at traditional print newspapers
- D) Should focus on national rather than local journalism trends

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: The first text is not labeled (no "Passage 1" heading), while the second is labeled "Passage 2" — minor inconsistency, though context makes it clear.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · medium-low · #4 (q00754)

> Caffeine is one of the most widely consumed psychoactive substances in the world, and for most healthy adults its effects are benign or mildly beneficial. Research has linked moderate coffee consumption to reduced risk of several diseases, including Parkinson's and type 2 diabetes. For the vast majority of people, a few cups of coffee a day poses no meaningful health risk.
> Passage 2:
> The safety of caffeine consumption depends heavily on individual variation. Genetic differences in how people metabolize caffeine mean that a dose that is harmless for one person can significantly elevate cardiovascular risk for another. Individuals who are slow metabolizers of caffeine face higher risks of heart attack from regular consumption, and blanket assurances that caffeine is safe for "most adults" can obscure these meaningful individual differences.

**Q:** Based on the texts, both authors would most likely agree that:

- A) Caffeine is harmful to all adults and should be avoided
- B) Moderate caffeine consumption poses no risk whatsoever to any individual
- C) Caffeine's effects on health are not identical for every person
- D) Research has failed to identify any health benefits of caffeine consumption

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (high)
- formatting: The first text lacks a 'Text 1' (or 'Passage 1') label while the second is labeled 'Passage 2', which is slightly inconsistent but does not impede comprehension.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · medium-low · #5 (q00755)

> The most effective leaders adapt their communication style to their audience. A good manager does not speak to a room of engineers the same way they speak to a board of investors. Reading the room and adjusting accordingly — varying tone, vocabulary, and level of detail — is a core leadership skill that separates excellent communicators from merely competent ones.
> Passage 2:
> Authentic leadership requires consistency. Leaders who dramatically alter their communication style depending on their audience risk appearing calculating rather than genuine. Employees who witness a manager speaking one way to them and a completely different way to executives often lose trust in that manager's authenticity and wonder which version represents the person's true values.

**Q:** Based on the texts, the authors of Passage 1 and Passage 2 would most likely disagree about whether:

- A) Leaders should communicate clearly with all audiences
- B) Adapting communication style to different audiences is compatible with authentic leadership
- C) Trust is important in relationships between managers and employees
- D) Leadership can be developed through practice and training

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: The first text is not labeled 'Passage 1' (only the second is labeled), which is inconsistent with the question stem's reference to Passage 1 and Passage 2.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · medium-low · #6 (q00756)

> Ancient Rome's system of roads was one of its most consequential engineering achievements. Spanning over 250,000 miles at its peak, the network allowed armies to move rapidly, merchants to trade across vast distances, and administrators to govern an empire that stretched from Britain to Mesopotamia. The roads were so well engineered that many remained in use for centuries after Rome's fall.
> Passage 2:
> The Roman road network, impressive as it was, served primarily military and administrative purposes — the needs of the state rather than the general population. The roads' longevity reflects their military-grade construction, not their accessibility to common people.

**Q:** Based on the texts, how does the author of Passage 2 complicate the account given in Passage 1?

- A) By arguing that the Roman roads were not as well-engineered as Passage 1 claims
- B) By questioning whether the Roman Empire was actually as large as Passage 1 suggests
- C) By challenging the claim that the roads benefited Roman society broadly, arguing they mainly served state and military functions
- D) By suggesting that the roads were built by enslaved people rather than Roman engineers

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (high)
- formatting: The first passage is labeled only 'Passage / text' rather than 'Text 1'/'Passage 1', which is slightly inconsistent with the 'Passage 2' label and the question's reference to 'Passage 1'. Minor and non-blocking.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · medium-low · #7 (q00757)

> Competitive sports teach children valuable life lessons: how to handle losing with grace, how to work as part of a team, and how to push through difficulty to achieve a goal. > Passage 2:

Youth sports environments frequently reward aggressive competition, early specialization, and win-at-all-costs mentalities that do not translate into healthy adult behavior. For many children — especially those who are not high performers — competitive sports create anxiety and exclusion rather than resilience and belonging.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · medium-low · #9 (q00759)

> Contemporary art museums have a responsibility to challenge their audiences. Great art has always provoked discomfort, forced new ways of seeing, and disrupted settled assumptions. > Passage 2:
> Museums must balance challenge with accessibility. Work that alienates or confuses visitors without offering any pathway into its meaning can undermine the institution's role as a public resource. Provocation without context leaves audiences feeling excluded rather than expanded, and risks reinforcing the perception that contemporary art is an insider game for the already initiated.

**Q:** Based on the texts, both authors would most likely agree that:

- B) Contemporary art museums serve an important public function that shapes how their programming decisions are judged
- C) The most valuable art is always immediately accessible to all viewers
- D) Museums should display only work by artists from underrepresented communities

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · medium-low · #10 (q00760)

> Travel is one of the most transformative experiences available to a person. Encountering unfamiliar cultures, foods, languages, and landscapes forces travelers to question their assumptions and broaden their understanding of what it means to live a human life. People who travel widely tend to be more empathetic, more flexible in their thinking, and more comfortable with ambiguity.
> Passage 2:
> The transformative benefits of travel are real but unevenly accessible and often romanticized. For millions of people, international travel is prohibitively expensive, practically impossible due to visa restrictions, or dangerous due to political instability. When travel is held up as a uniquely broadening experience, it implicitly devalues the intellectual and empathetic growth available through reading, conversation, art, and community engagement — pursuits accessible to those for whom travel is not.

**Q:** Based on the texts, the author of Passage 2 would most likely argue that the author of Passage 1 has failed to consider:

- A) The environmental cost of international air travel
- B) Whether travel is equally available to all people, and whether it is the only path to the growth it is said to produce
- C) The possibility that travel can reinforce cultural stereotypes rather than challenge them
- D) Whether travelers actually retain the lessons they learn abroad once they return home

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: The first text is not labeled 'Passage 1' (only 'Passage 2' is labeled), though the question refers to Passage 1. Minor but should be fixed.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · medium-low · #12 (q00762)

> Mindfulness meditation, once rooted in Buddhist contemplative traditions, has been widely adopted in Western clinical settings as a tool for reducing stress and anxiety. Proponents argue that regular practice helps individuals observe their thoughts without judgment, creating a psychological distance from distressing emotions. This detachment, they claim, allows people to respond to challenges more calmly rather than reacting impulsively.
> Passage 2:
> Critics of clinical mindfulness programs caution that the practice's translation from spiritual to therapeutic contexts strips away essential elements. When mindfulness is reduced to a stress-reduction technique, its deeper ethical dimensions — including compassion, community, and a commitment to reducing suffering in others — are often discarded.
