The result, some scholars argue, is a practice focused narrowly on individual well-being at the expense of its original communal and moral orientation.

**Q:** Which choice best describes how the authors of Passage 1 and Passage 2 differ in their views of clinical mindfulness programs?

- A) The author of Passage 1 sees clinical mindfulness as incomplete, while the author of Passage 2 views it as fully effective.
- B) The author of Passage 1 emphasizes the psychological benefits of adapted mindfulness, while the author of Passage 2 argues that adaptation has come at a cost to the practice's broader values.
- C) The author of Passage 1 argues that mindfulness should remain tied to Buddhist traditions, while the author of Passage 2 supports its clinical use.
- D) Both authors agree that clinical mindfulness is beneficial but disagree about which populations benefit most.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: The first passage lacks a "Passage 1" label (only "Passage 2:" is marked), though context makes the division clear.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · medium-low · #15 (q00765)

> Urban green spaces — parks, street trees, and community gardens — provide measurable benefits to city residents. Research consistently shows that access to greenery reduces psychological stress, encourages physical activity, and fosters social interaction among neighbors. City planners increasingly incorporate green infrastructure into urban design, viewing it not as a luxury but as a public health necessity.
> Passage 2:
> The distribution of urban green spaces is rarely equitable. Studies in cities across the United States and Europe have found that lower-income and minority neighborhoods consistently have less park access, fewer street trees, and smaller green areas than wealthier districts. Without deliberate policy intervention, the health benefits of urban greenery flow disproportionately to those who already enjoy advantages in housing, income, and opportunity.

**Q:** Both passages discuss urban green spaces, but they differ primarily in their focus. Which choice best describes this difference?

- A) Passage 1 focuses on the design challenges of creating green spaces, while Passage 2 focuses on environmental impacts.
- B) Passage 1 describes the benefits of urban green spaces, while Passage 2 highlights the unequal distribution of those benefits.
- C) Passage 1 argues that green spaces are unnecessary luxuries, while Passage 2 argues that they are public health necessities.
- D) Passage 1 concentrates on the social benefits of green spaces, while Passage 2 argues that those social benefits have been exaggerated.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: Passage 1 is not labeled (the text begins without a 'Passage 1:' heading), while Passage 2 is labeled; minor inconsistency.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · medium-low · #16 (q00766)

> Proponents of universal basic income (UBI) argue that providing all citizens with a regular, unconditional cash payment would reduce poverty more effectively than targeted welfare programs. Because UBI does not require recipients to prove need or comply with restrictions, it eliminates the bureaucratic overhead and stigma associated with traditional assistance. Advocates contend this simplicity would ensure that support reaches everyone who needs it.
> Passage 2:
> Economists skeptical of universal basic income point out that universality is also its central weakness. Distributing payments to wealthy individuals who do not need assistance is an inefficient use of public funds. Targeted programs, despite their administrative complexity, direct resources to those in greatest need and can be calibrated to address specific forms of deprivation — such as housing instability or lack of healthcare — that a flat cash payment may not resolve.

**Q:** Based on the passages, the author of Passage 1 and the author of Passage 2 would most likely disagree about whether

- A) poverty is a serious problem requiring government intervention
- B) bureaucratic complexity in welfare programs creates challenges for recipients
- C) the universality of UBI is an advantage or a disadvantage
- D) cash payments are ever an appropriate form of public assistance

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (high)
- formatting: The first passage is not labeled 'Passage 1' (it appears only under the generic 'Passage / text:' heading), while the second is labeled 'Passage 2'. Meaning is still clear, but the labeling is inconsistent.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · medium-low · #17 (q00767)

> In the early twentieth century, the Bauhaus school revolutionized design education by insisting that art and industrial craft were not opposing disciplines. Students trained simultaneously in aesthetic theory and hands-on workshop practice, learning to create objects that were both beautiful and functional. The school's influence spread far beyond Germany, shaping modernist architecture, graphic design, and product design throughout the world.
> Passage 2:
> Admirers of the Bauhaus often celebrate its synthesis of art and craft, but some historians argue that this synthesis was never fully achieved. Workshop production at the Bauhaus remained largely oriented toward prototypes and one-of-a-kind pieces rather than genuine mass manufacture. The gap between Bauhaus ideals of democratizing design and the actual accessibility of Bauhaus-influenced objects suggests that the school's legacy is more aspirational than practical.

**Q:** How would the author of Passage 2 most likely qualify the claim in Passage 1 that the Bauhaus "revolutionized" design?

- A) By arguing that Bauhaus design principles were never adopted outside Germany
- B) By contending that the Bauhaus influenced architecture but had no lasting effect on graphic or product design
- C) By suggesting that while the Bauhaus was influential, its stated goal of making well-designed objects widely accessible was not fully realized
- D) By maintaining that Bauhaus students were trained in aesthetics but received no practical workshop instruction

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (high)
- formatting: The first passage is not explicitly labeled 'Passage 1' (only the header 'Passage / text:' appears before it), though the second is labeled 'Passage 2'. Minor, since the question references Passage 1 and context makes it clear.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · medium-low · #20 (q00770)

> The term "wilderness" carries an implicit assumption that undisturbed nature exists separately from human influence. This binary thinking has long shaped conservation policy in the United States, where protected wilderness areas are managed to exclude most human activity. Advocates argue that preserving landscapes untouched by modern development allows ecosystems to function according to their own ecological logic, free from the disruptions that agriculture, industry, and urbanization bring.
> Passage 2:
> Environmental historians have challenged the concept of pristine wilderness, pointing out that Indigenous peoples actively managed North American landscapes for thousands of years before European colonization. Through controlled burns, selective harvesting, and deliberate cultivation, these communities shaped forests, prairies, and wetlands in ways that European settlers mistook for untouched nature. Recognizing this history complicates the notion that setting aside land from human use is the only legitimate form of conservation.

**Q:** How does the information in Passage 2 relate to the argument made in Passage 1?

- A) It supports Passage 1 by confirming that North American ecosystems developed without significant human interference.
- B) It challenges the foundational assumption of Passage 1 by demonstrating that landscapes often considered pristine were actively shaped by human activity.
- C) It agrees with Passage 1 that excluding all human activity is the most effective conservation strategy.
- D) It argues that European settlers, not Indigenous peoples, were responsible for creating North American wilderness areas.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: The first text is not labeled 'Passage 1' even though the second is labeled 'Passage 2' and the question refers to 'Passage 1'; the label appears to have been dropped.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · medium-low · #21 (q00771)

> The placebo effect — in which patients experience real improvements in symptoms after receiving an inert treatment they believe to be active — has long been considered a nuisance variable in clinical trials. Researchers use control groups and blinding procedures specifically to filter out placebo responses, ensuring that observed benefits can be attributed to the treatment itself rather than to patients' expectations.
> Passage 2:
> Some medical researchers now argue that the placebo effect deserves to be taken seriously as a therapeutic mechanism in its own right. Studies have shown that even patients who are told they are receiving a placebo — so-called open-label placebos — sometimes experience meaningful symptom relief. This finding suggests that expectation and ritual may activate genuine neurobiological pathways, making the placebo effect a potential tool rather than merely a confound to be eliminated.

**Q:** Based on the passages, the author of Passage 2 would most likely challenge the view expressed in Passage 1 by arguing that

- A) control groups and blinding procedures are ineffective methods for conducting clinical trials
- B) the placebo effect should be studied and potentially harnessed as a treatment rather than simply minimized
- C) patients should not be informed whether they are receiving a placebo or an active treatment
- D) expectation plays no role in producing symptom relief in clinical settings

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: The first passage is not labeled 'Passage 1' (only 'Passage 2:' appears), which is inconsistent with standard paired-passage formatting, though the question still refers to 'Passage 1.'

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · medium-low · #22 (q00772)

> Proponents of charter schools argue that they increase educational equity by giving families in underserved communities more choice in where their children attend school. Because charters operate independently of traditional district bureaucracies, advocates say they can innovate more freely, tailoring curriculum and culture to the needs of specific student populations. Choice, in this view, is a mechanism for improving outcomes for children who might otherwise be trapped in low-performing schools.
> Passage 2:
> Critics of charter school expansion contend that choice-based reforms can fragment public school systems in ways that harm the students who remain in traditional public schools. When higher-performing students and engaged families migrate to charters, district schools may lose both funding — which is often tied to enrollment — and the social capital that involved parents bring. The promise of choice, critics argue, can mask a transfer of resources away from the most vulnerable students.

**Q:** Based on the passages, the authors of Passage 1 and Passage 2 would most likely disagree about whether

- A) charter schools are allowed to design their own curricula
- B) some students in urban areas attend low-performing schools
- C) expanding school choice improves outcomes for the most disadvantaged students overall
- D) charter schools receive any public funding

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (high)
- formatting: The first passage is not labeled 'Passage 1:' while the second is labeled 'Passage 2:', a minor inconsistency relative to standard SAT paired-passage formatting.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · medium-low · #24 (q00774)

> Astronomers have long used the brightness of Type Ia supernovae as "standard candles" to measure cosmic distances. Because these stellar explosions are thought to reach a consistent peak luminosity, their apparent brightness as seen from Earth can be used to calculate how far away they are. This method helped researchers discover in the 1990s that the universe's expansion is accelerating, a finding that led to the concept of dark energy.
> Passage 2:
> Recent studies have raised questions about whether Type Ia supernovae are as uniform in brightness as previously assumed. Researchers have found that the peak luminosity of these events can vary depending on the age of the stellar population in which they occur. If older stellar environments produce systematically dimmer supernovae, then distance measurements based on the standard candle assumption may contain systematic errors, potentially affecting conclusions about the rate of cosmic expansion.

**Q:** Based on the passages, how does the information in Passage 2 relate to the method described in Passage 1?

- A) It confirms that Type Ia supernovae are reliable standard candles by explaining the physics behind their consistent brightness.
- B) It introduces evidence that could undermine the reliability of the standard candle method by suggesting that supernova brightness may not be as uniform as assumed.
- C) It argues that the discovery of dark energy was based on a fundamentally different measurement method than the one described in Passage 1.
- D) It suggests that only supernovae occurring in young stellar populations should be used as distance markers.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: The first passage is not labeled 'Passage 1' (only 'Passage 2' is labeled), though the question refers to Passage 1. Minor, but inconsistent with College Board formatting.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · medium-low · #26 (q00776)

> The reintroduction of wolves to Yellowstone National Park in 1995 is frequently cited as a model of successful rewilding. After decades of absence, wolves reduced the overpopulation of elk, which in turn allowed streamside vegetation to recover. Restored plant cover stabilized riverbanks, reduced erosion, and ultimately altered the physical course of rivers — a cascading series of effects that ecologists call a trophic cascade.
> Passage 2:
> The Yellowstone wolf reintroduction has achieved near-mythological status among conservationists, but some ecologists urge caution about generalizing from it. The trophic cascade effects, while real, were smaller and slower than early reports suggested, and other factors — including drought, bear predation, and human hunting outside park boundaries — also influenced elk populations. Applying the Yellowstone model uncritically to other ecosystems risks oversimplifying the complex, site-specific dynamics that determine whether rewilding succeeds.

**Q:** Based on the passages, both authors would most likely agree with which of the following statements about wolf reintroduction at Yellowstone?

- A) The reintroduction had no meaningful ecological effects on elk populations or vegetation.
- B) The reintroduction produced some genuine ecological changes in the park.
- C) The Yellowstone model should be applied to all ecosystems where wolves once lived.
- D) Trophic cascades are too unpredictable to serve as a basis for conservation planning.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: The first passage lacks a "Passage 1" label (it appears only under "Passage / text:"), while the second is labeled "Passage 2"; this inconsistency is a minor presentation flaw but does not affect solvability.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · medium-low · #27 (q00777)

> The spread of social media has transformed how political information reaches citizens. Platforms that allow users to share, comment, and react to news stories have made political participation more accessible to ordinary people, enabling grassroots movements to organize and amplify their messages rapidly. Researchers who study civic engagement point to social media's capacity to lower the barriers to political involvement as a meaningful democratizing force.
> Passage 2:
> The democratizing potential of social media must be weighed against its tendency to create information environments in which users are exposed primarily to content that confirms their existing beliefs. Algorithmic curation rewards engagement, and emotionally charged or partisan content tends to generate more clicks and shares than nuanced reporting. Critics argue that this dynamic deepens political polarization rather than fostering the informed, cross-cutting dialogue that healthy democracy requires.

**Q:** How do the two passages differ in their evaluation of social media's effect on democracy?

- A) Passage 1 argues that social media harms democracy, while Passage 2 argues it has no effect.
- B) Passage 1 emphasizes social media's role in increasing political participation, while Passage 2 raises concerns about how its design may undermine informed civic discourse.
- C) Passage 1 focuses on how algorithms shape political content, while Passage 2 focuses on civic engagement.
- D) Both passages agree that social media strengthens democracy but disagree about which platforms are most effective.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: The first passage lacks its "Passage 1" heading (only "Passage 2" is labeled), though context makes it clear.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · medium-low · #28 (q00778)

> Minimalist architecture, popularized in the twentieth century by designers such as Ludwig Mies van der Rohe, prizes simplicity, clean lines, and the elimination of ornament. Its proponents argue that stripping away decorative excess reveals the essential beauty of form, material, and structure. In their view, a building should express honesty about its construction — letting steel, glass, and concrete speak without embellishment.
> Passage 2:
> Critics of architectural minimalism argue that its rejection of ornament reflects a culturally specific and historically contingent set of values, not a universal aesthetic truth. Many architectural traditions around the world — from Islamic geometric tile work to Gothic stone carving — treat ornament as an integral element of meaning, not superficial decoration to be discarded. Minimalism's equation of simplicity with honesty may say more about early twentieth-century European modernism than about architecture in general.

**Q:** Based on the passages, the author of Passage 2 would most likely challenge which assumption made in Passage 1?

- A) That Mies van der Rohe was among the designers who popularized minimalist architecture
- B) That steel, glass, and concrete are suitable building materials
- C) That eliminating ornament reveals a deeper and more universal architectural truth
- D) That minimalist buildings should be constructed using honest structural methods

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (high)
- formatting: The first passage is not labeled "Passage 1" (only "Passage 2" is labeled), though the question refers to Passage 1. Minor, but inconsistent with SAT paired-passage formatting.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · medium-low · #29 (q00779)

> The concept of emotional intelligence — the ability to recognize, understand, and manage one's own emotions and those of others — has gained considerable traction in education and organizational management. Advocates argue that emotional intelligence predicts success in collaborative environments more reliably than traditional cognitive measures such as IQ. Schools and corporations alike have invested in programs designed to build emotional intelligence skills in students and employees.
> Passage 2:
> Psychologists critical of emotional intelligence as a formal construct argue that the concept lacks the empirical rigor of established psychological measures. Many studies purporting to show emotional intelligence's predictive power rely on self-report questionnaires, which are susceptible to social desirability bias — respondents answer in ways they believe are favorable rather than truthfully. Until emotional intelligence can be measured with greater objectivity, critics contend its claimed benefits should be treated with skepticism.

**Q:** Based on the passages, what is the central point of disagreement between the two authors?

- A) Whether emotions play any role in workplace or educational success
- B) Whether emotional intelligence programs are more expensive than traditional educational approaches
- C) Whether emotional intelligence is a well-supported, reliable construct or a concept whose empirical foundation is questionable
- D) Whether IQ tests are an accurate measure of cognitive ability

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (high)
- formatting: The first text is not labeled (no "Text 1"/"Passage 1" heading), while the second is labeled "Passage 2"; also SAT convention uses "Text 1"/"Text 2." Minor, but inconsistent presentation.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · medium-low · #30 (q00780)

> Many linguists argue that the language we speak shapes the way we perceive and categorize reality. Studies have shown, for example, that speakers of languages with more precise color terminology can distinguish between shades more rapidly than speakers whose languages use fewer color categories. This line of research, associated with the Sapir-Whorf hypothesis, suggests that language is not merely a vehicle for expressing pre-existing thoughts but an active participant in forming them.
> Passage 2:
> The strong version of the Sapir-Whorf hypothesis — the idea that language determines thought — has largely been abandoned by cognitive scientists. While language may subtly influence certain perceptual tasks, as color recognition studies suggest, human beings across cultures demonstrate the capacity to think, reason, and solve problems independently of whether their language encodes specific distinctions. Thought, in this view, is not imprisoned by language but remains flexible and available to all people regardless of linguistic background.

**Q:** Based on the passages, both authors would most likely agree with which of the following claims?

- A) Language has absolutely no effect on human cognition or perception.
- B) The strong version of the Sapir-Whorf hypothesis accurately describes the relationship between language and thought.
- C) Research on color terminology provides some evidence that language can influence certain perceptual abilities.
- D) Human beings are incapable of thinking about concepts for which their language has no word.

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (high)
- formatting: The first text is not labeled 'Passage 1' (only 'Passage 2' is labeled), which is a minor formatting inconsistency; it does not affect answerability.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · medium-high · #1 (q00781)

> Psychological research on "ego depletion" suggests that willpower is a finite resource that diminishes with use. Studies in the early 2000s appeared to show that people who exerted self-control on one task subsequently performed worse on unrelated self-control tasks, as if drawing from the same limited mental reservoir. The finding was influential in popular psychology and was widely used to recommend behaviors such as making important decisions earlier in the day.
> Passage 2:
> The ego depletion effect has failed to replicate consistently in large-scale studies. A major pre-registered replication effort involving multiple laboratories found no significant depletion effect, and subsequent meta-analyses have suggested that early positive results may reflect publication bias toward studies with dramatic findings. Some researchers now argue that willpower is better understood as a motivational state influenced by beliefs and expectations rather than a physiological resource that runs out.

**Q:** Based on the texts, how does the evidence presented in Passage 2 affect the recommendations described in Passage 1?

- A) It confirms that people should make important decisions early in the day to conserve willpower.
- B) It undermines the empirical basis for those recommendations by casting doubt on the depletion model they were derived from.
- C) It suggests the recommendations are correct but were derived from the wrong explanation.
- D) It strengthens the case for ego depletion by providing additional replications of the original findings.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: The first text is not labeled (it appears as an unlabeled block followed by 'Passage 2:'), though the question refers to 'Passage 1.' The label should be present for parallel structure.
- difficulty_mismatch: For a stated medium-high difficulty, the distractors are implausibly weak: A and D directly contradict Passage 2, and C is self-contradictory given that Passage 2 offers no support for the timing advice. The item plays as easy.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · medium-high · #2 (q00782)

> The success of the Scandinavian social democratic model — combining high taxes, generous welfare states, and robust labor protections with strong economic performance — demonstrates that equality and prosperity are not in tension. Nordic countries consistently rank among the wealthiest, happiest, and most innovative in the world, suggesting that the trade-off between redistribution and growth is largely a myth.
> Passage 2:
> Attributing Nordic prosperity to social democratic policy requires controlling for a range of confounding factors that proponents of the model often overlook. Nordic countries had relatively high levels of social trust, low corruption, and strong institutional quality before the welfare state expanded significantly. It is plausible that these pre-existing conditions, rather than welfare policies themselves, are the primary drivers of prosperity — and that similar policies transplanted to countries without those conditions might produce different results.

**Q:** Based on the texts, the author of Passage 2 would most likely argue that the author of Passage 1's reasoning:

- A) Correctly identifies a causal relationship between welfare policy and prosperity
- B) Mistakes correlation for causation by ignoring pre-existing conditions that may explain both policy choices and economic outcomes
- C) Relies on inaccurate data about Nordic economic performance
- D) Overstates the generosity of Nordic welfare states compared to other European countries

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: The first text lacks a label ("Passage 1:"), while the second is labeled "Passage 2:" — the passages are presented inconsistently.
- difficulty_mismatch: The correct answer restates Passage 2's explicit confounding-variable argument almost verbatim, and the three distractors raise objections (bad data, overstated generosity, agreement) with no textual basis, making the item considerably easier than the stated medium-high tier.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · medium-high · #3 (q00783)

> The novelist argues in her essay that fiction's primary moral function is to cultivate the reader's imaginative identification with others. By living inside consciousnesses unlike our own, we become more capable of the kind of empathetic understanding that moral life requires. Great fiction does not instruct — it expands the self.
> Passage 2:
> Fiction's relationship to moral life is more complicated than the "empathy machine" model suggests. Research has shown that reading fiction does not reliably translate into more ethical behavior in practice — people can imaginatively inhabit a character's suffering while remaining unmoved in their actual moral conduct. The novel may expand the imaginative self without enlarging the ethical one.

**Q:** Based on the texts, both authors would most likely agree that:

- A) Fiction reliably causes readers to behave more ethically in their daily lives
- B) Imaginative identification with fictional characters is something reading can produce
- C) The moral function of fiction is well understood and uncontroversial
- D) Fiction that does not aim to instruct has no moral value

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- difficulty_mismatch: The distractors are all easily eliminated (A is explicitly contradicted by Text 2; C is contradicted by the framing of both texts; D is unsupported by either), making the item considerably easier than a medium-high Cross-Text Connections question.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · medium-high · #6 (q00786)

> The decline of Latin as a living language should be understood as a cultural impoverishment rather than a natural evolution. Latin was a language of extraordinary precision, flexibility, and philosophical depth. Its decline — driven largely by political fragmentation and the rise of vernacular nationalism — severed European civilization from a shared intellectual heritage that had sustained continuity across centuries of political change.
> Passage 2:
> The death of Latin as a spoken language was not a loss but a liberation. The dominance of Latin across medieval Europe was inseparable from ecclesiastical and institutional power structures that excluded vernacular expression — the language of peasants, women, merchants, and the unlettered. The rise of vernacular literature did not fragment European civilization; it expanded who could participate in it.

**Q:** Based on the texts, both authors would most likely agree that:

- A) The decline of Latin had no meaningful effect on European intellectual life
- B) The Catholic Church played a positive role in preserving Latin as a language of scholarship
- C) The transition away from Latin as a dominant language had significant cultural and political consequences
- D) Vernacular languages are linguistically inferior to Latin

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (high)
- difficulty_mismatch: Labeled medium-high, but the three wrong answers are transparently wrong (A contradicts both texts, B is mentioned only negatively in Text 2 and not in Text 1, D is contradicted by Text 2), making this an easy item.
- formatting: The first passage is introduced as 'Passage / text:' rather than 'Text 1'/'Passage 1', so the paired-text labeling is inconsistent with the second passage's label.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · medium-high · #7 (q00787)

> The growing movement to remove statues of historical figures associated with slavery, colonialism, or other forms of oppression reflects a healthy democratic impulse: communities have the right to decide whose memory they choose to honor in public space. Statues are not neutral historical records; they are acts of commemoration, and decisions about who is commemorated embody a community's values.
> Passage 2:
> Removing historical statues, however morally comprehensible, risks severing citizens from a complex past they need to understand rather than sanitize. The impulse to curate public space to reflect only approved values can slide toward a kind of historical amnesia. A more constructive approach would be to recontextualize statues — through accompanying text, counter-monuments, or museum relocation — rather than to erase their presence from the landscape.

**Q:** Based on the texts, on which point would the authors most likely agree?

- A) Historical statues should never be removed under any circumstances
- B) The decision about what to commemorate in public space is a genuinely important question with real cultural stakes
- C) Statues of all historical figures should be relocated to museums
- D) Citizens have no right to shape public commemorative landscapes

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: The first text lacks its "Passage 1:" (or "Text 1") label, while the second is labeled "Passage 2:", making the two-text structure visually inconsistent.
- difficulty_mismatch: For a medium-high item, the distractors are implausibly extreme (A, C, D use absolute language contradicted by both passages), so the question is easier than its stated tier.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · medium-high · #8 (q00788)

> The economist argues that universal basic income (UBI) — a regular unconditional cash transfer to all citizens — would reduce poverty and increase individual freedom by giving people the security to take risks, leave exploitative employment, and pursue education or creative work. Cash transfers, evidence from pilot programs suggests, are typically spent on necessities and productive investments rather than on frivolous consumption.
> Passage 2:
> UBI's appeal rests on an unduly individualistic conception of what people need from a social safety net. What the poor lack is not simply income but access to healthcare, childcare, housing, and education — services whose provision through markets is systematically inadequate. Replacing robust public services with a cash transfer risks dismantling the institutional infrastructure that delivers those services, leaving individuals with money but without the social goods money cannot reliably buy in unregulated markets.

**Q:** Based on the texts, the author of Passage 2 would most likely argue that the proposal in Passage 1 risks:

- A) Providing too small a cash transfer to make a meaningful difference in poverty rates
- B) Substituting a market-mediated solution for an institutional one, thereby dismantling social services that cash alone cannot replicate
- C) Creating inflation by increasing the money supply through universal payments
- D) Disincentivizing work by providing unconditional income to employed and unemployed people alike

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: The first text is not labeled 'Passage 1' (it begins 'The economist argues...'), while the second is labeled 'Passage 2'. The missing label is inconsistent and could momentarily confuse students.
- difficulty_mismatch: Choice B is almost a direct paraphrase of Passage 2's final sentence, and the three distractors raise objections (inflation, work disincentives, insufficient transfer size) never hinted at in either text, making the item easier than the stated medium-high difficulty.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · medium-high · #9 (q00789)

> The widespread adoption of open-plan office design was driven by genuine beliefs about collaboration and creativity. Removing walls between desks, the theory went, would increase spontaneous interaction, accelerate information sharing, and break down hierarchical barriers between employees and managers. Companies that made the switch reported feeling more dynamic and open.
> Passage 2:
> The research on open-plan offices tells a different story than the original theory predicted. Studies using sensor data have found that when companies removed private offices, face-to-face interaction actually declined — employees turned to headphones and screens to create private space, and communication shifted to email and messaging rather than conversation. The design intended to foster spontaneous collaboration produced the opposite: more isolation and more mediated interaction.

**Q:** Based on the texts, which of the following best describes the relationship between the theory described in Passage 1 and the findings described in Passage 2?

- A) The findings confirmed the theory by showing increased collaboration in open-plan offices.
- B) The findings revealed that the theory's predicted outcomes did not materialize and were in some cases reversed.
- C) The findings partially supported the theory but suggested that the design needed refinement.
- D) The two passages address different aspects of office design and do not bear on each other's claims.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: Passage 2 is labeled but the first text has no "Passage 1" heading, making the paired structure less clear.
- difficulty_mismatch: Passage 2 explicitly states the design "produced the opposite," so the correct answer is nearly paraphrased in the text; the distractors are weak (A contradicts the text, D is plainly false). This reads as an easy item rather than medium-high.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · medium-high · #12 (q00792)

> Ancient Stoic philosophers maintained that the good life depends entirely on virtue, which they defined as the correct use of reason in accordance with nature. External goods — wealth, health, reputation — were classified as "preferred indifferents": things a rational person might reasonably pursue but whose absence could never diminish true happiness. For the Stoics, a person living virtuously on a sickbed possessed the same quality of life, in the philosophically meaningful sense, as one who was prosperous and celebrated.
> Passage 2:
> Contemporary philosophers of wellbeing have largely rejected the Stoic position, arguing that subjective experience must factor into any adequate account of a good life. A theory that declares a suffering person equally well-off as a thriving one simply because both exercise reason correctly strikes many as deeply counterintuitive. Accounts that center desire satisfaction or positive hedonic states treat the person's own felt experience as morally and philosophically relevant in a way that classical virtue ethics tends to neglect.

**Q:** The authors of Passage 1 and Passage 2 would most likely disagree about which of the following?

- A) Whether ancient philosophical traditions have anything useful to contribute to contemporary debates about wellbeing
- B) Whether virtue can be meaningfully defined in relation to the use of reason
- C) Whether a person's subjective experience of suffering or flourishing is relevant to determining how well their life is going
- D) Whether wealth and reputation are the most important external goods a person can possess

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (high)
- formatting: The first passage lacks a "Passage 1" label while the second is labeled "Passage 2," a minor presentation inconsistency.
- other: Passage 1 reports the Stoics' position rather than stating the author's own view, so strictly the 'authors' disagreement' is inferred from attributed views; standard SAT items usually make the first author's endorsement clearer. This does not change the best answer.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · medium-high · #13 (q00793)

> Literary scholars have long debated whether an author's stated intentions should guide interpretation of a text. New Critics of the mid-twentieth century argued firmly against this view, proposing that a poem or novel, once published, belongs to the public and must be interpreted on the basis of its language alone. The author's private purposes — whatever they may have been — are neither recoverable with certainty nor relevant to what the text actually means in the hands of readers.
> Passage 2:
> Intentionalist critics counter that ignoring authorial purpose leads to interpretive anarchy. Without some anchor to the author's design, any reading becomes equally valid, however implausible, since readers can project onto texts meanings the words could support only through tortured logic. Knowing that a poet wrote in the context of a specific political crisis, for instance, can distinguish a reading that illuminates genuine textual features from one that imposes an anachronistic framework the work cannot legitimately sustain.

**Q:** Based on the passages, the New Critics described in Passage 1 and the intentionalist critics described in Passage 2 hold contrasting views on which of the following questions?

- A) Whether literary texts can ever be fully understood by readers who lack knowledge of historical context
- B) Whether authorial intent should serve as a constraint on which interpretations of a text are considered legitimate
- C) Whether the formal language of a poem or novel is more important than its narrative content
- D) Whether authors are generally aware of all the meanings their texts produce

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: The first passage lacks its "Passage 1:" label (only "Passage 2:" is marked), which is inconsistent with standard paired-passage formatting, though the question's reference to Passage 1 makes the intent clear.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · medium-high · #16 (q00796)

> Proponents of minimum wage increases often cite studies showing that modest hikes do not produce the unemployment effects that classical economic theory predicts. Research by economists Card and Krueger on fast-food employment in New Jersey following a 1992 wage increase found no significant job losses relative to neighboring Pennsylvania, which did not raise its minimum wage. This and subsequent studies have led many labor economists to revise their expectations about the elasticity of labor demand in low-wage sectors.
> Passage 2:
> The Card-Krueger study and its successors have not gone unchallenged. Critics argue that the restaurant industry, which is highly localized and cannot easily offshore labor, is not representative of broader labor markets. Furthermore, some economists contend that while immediate employment levels may hold steady after a wage increase, longer-term effects — reduced hiring of new workers, increased automation investment, and the consolidation of small businesses — are methodologically harder to capture and may account for the full disemployment effect the standard model predicts.

**Q:** Based on the passages, the authors would most likely agree that the Card-Krueger study and similar research:

- A) Conclusively demonstrate that minimum wage increases have no negative effects on employment under any economic conditions
- B) Are methodologically sound and should be accepted as the definitive basis for minimum wage policy
- C) Show outcomes that differ from what classical economic theory predicts, even if the interpretation and generalizability of those outcomes remain subjects of debate
- D) Are primarily valuable for what they reveal about the fast-food industry rather than broader labor market dynamics

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (high)
- formatting: The first text is not labeled 'Passage 1' while the second is labeled 'Passage 2'; minor inconsistency, though the two-text structure remains clear.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · medium-high · #21 (q00801)

> Rewilding — the large-scale restoration of ecosystems through the reintroduction of extirpated species — has gained significant traction among conservation biologists. The reintroduction of wolves to Yellowstone National Park in 1995 is frequently cited as a paradigm case: wolf predation altered elk behavior, which reduced overgrazing along riverbanks, which in turn allowed vegetation to stabilize stream banks and change hydrology in ways that benefited numerous species. Proponents argue that restoring apex predators can trigger cascading ecological benefits through so-called trophic cascades.
> Passage 2:
> The Yellowstone wolf reintroduction has become something of a conservation myth, its effects amplified well beyond what the data support. Subsequent ecological analyses have found that the "landscape of fear" effect — the idea that elk changed their behavior dramatically due to predation risk — is more limited geographically and temporally than early accounts suggested. Riverbank vegetation recovery in Yellowstone has been influenced by drought cycles, human management, and elk population dynamics that predate wolf reintroduction. Attributing the ecosystem changes primarily to wolves oversimplifies a complex, multi-causal system.

**Q:** Based on the passages, the authors would most likely disagree about which of the following claims?

- A) Wolves were reintroduced to Yellowstone National Park in 1995.
- B) The Yellowstone wolf reintroduction provides strong and sufficient evidence that rewilding through apex predator reintroduction reliably produces cascading ecosystem benefits.
- C) Ecological systems are influenced by multiple interacting variables including climate, human management, and species populations.
- D) Conservation biology as a field has increasingly focused on large-scale ecosystem restoration efforts.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: The first text is not labeled (no "Passage 1" / "Text 1" header) while the second is labeled "Passage 2," which is inconsistent with standard SAT paired-text formatting.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · medium-high · #23 (q00803)

> The transition from hunter-gatherer societies to agricultural ones has traditionally been described as a progressive advance — a development that freed humans from the uncertainty of foraging and enabled the cultural and technological achievements that followed. Stable food surpluses allowed for specialization of labor, the accumulation of knowledge, and ultimately the rise of civilization. On this reading, agriculture was the precondition for everything we recognize as distinctively human achievement.
> Passage 2:
> Anthropologists and archaeologists have increasingly challenged the narrative of agricultural progress. Skeletal evidence suggests that early agricultural populations were shorter, more disease-prone, and nutritionally worse off than the hunter-gatherers they replaced. The agricultural transition concentrated resources and power in ways that generated hierarchy and coercion. Many researchers now argue that agriculture was adopted not because it was superior but because expanding populations left communities with few alternatives — necessity rather than innovation.

**Q:** The authors of the two passages hold different views about the agricultural transition. Which of the following best characterizes the nature of their disagreement?

- A) Passage 1 argues that agriculture developed first in the Fertile Crescent, while Passage 2 argues for multiple independent origins.
- B) Passage 1 treats the shift to agriculture as a beneficial development that enabled human achievement, while Passage 2 presents evidence that the shift may have reduced individual wellbeing and resulted from constraint rather than choice.
- C) Passage 1 emphasizes the spiritual and cultural benefits of agriculture, while Passage 2 focuses exclusively on its economic disadvantages.
- D) Passage 1 argues that hunter-gatherers were unable to develop complex societies, while Passage 2 contends they were capable of significant cultural achievement.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: The first passage is not labeled (no "Text 1"/"Passage 1" header), while the second is labeled "Passage 2"; SAT convention also uses "Text 1"/"Text 2."
- difficulty_mismatch: For a medium-high item, the distractors are implausible: A introduces geographic claims absent from both texts, C misstates Passage 1 entirely, and D attributes a claim to Passage 2 it never makes. The correct answer is identifiable almost immediately, making this closer to an easy item.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · medium-high · #26 (q00806)

> The widespread adoption of the forty-hour workweek in the twentieth century was not simply a humanitarian reform — it was, in part, a productivity insight. Henry Ford discovered that reducing his workers' hours from nine to eight per day actually increased output, as fatigue-related errors declined and worker morale improved. Subsequent research in industrial psychology confirmed that beyond certain thresholds, additional hours produce diminishing returns, and that well-rested workers consistently outperform overworked ones on tasks requiring sustained attention and judgment.
> Passage 2:
> The relationship between working hours and productivity is more contingent than reform advocates typically acknowledge. Evidence that shorter hours improve output tends to come from manufacturing environments involving repetitive, physical, or error-sensitive tasks where fatigue is the primary limiting variable. In knowledge work — law, software development, strategic consulting — the relationship is less linear. Bursts of intensive effort during critical project phases may yield breakthroughs that could not be achieved under a strictly regulated schedule, and the costs of coordination and interruption inherent in rigid work-hour policies can offset productivity gains in cognitively complex roles.

**Q:** How would the author of Passage 2 most likely qualify the claim made in Passage 1 that "well-rested workers consistently outperform overworked ones on tasks requiring sustained attention and judgment"?

- A) By pointing out that Ford's experiment was conducted under conditions that cannot be replicated in modern workplaces
- B) By arguing that sustained attention and judgment are not actually the skills that determine productivity in most professional contexts
- C) By suggesting that the productivity benefits of reduced hours may not apply uniformly across all types of work, and may be less significant in knowledge-intensive roles
- D) By contending that worker morale, not reduced fatigue, is the true driver of the productivity gains associated with shorter hours

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (high)
- formatting: The first text is not labeled "Passage 1" (only the second is labeled "Passage 2"), though the question refers to "Passage 1." Minor, but inconsistent with standard dual-text presentation.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · medium-high · #30 (q00810)

> Neuroimaging studies conducted over the past two decades have enabled researchers to identify patterns of brain activity associated with various cognitive and emotional states. Proponents of this approach argue that it offers an objective window into mental life that self-report measures cannot provide: brain scans do not depend on participants' willingness or ability to describe their inner states accurately, and activation patterns can reveal processes that occur below the threshold of conscious awareness. This, advocates contend, makes neuroimaging a uniquely powerful tool for psychology.
> Passage 2:
> The claim that brain imaging provides an "objective" alternative to self-report is philosophically and empirically problematic. Neuroimaging data do not speak for themselves: interpreting a BOLD signal as evidence of, say, "fear" or "decision-making" requires mapping activation onto psychological constructs that are themselves contested and culturally variable. The apparent objectivity of a brain scan is partially an artifact of the inferential steps required to get from raw data to psychological meaning — steps that import all the assumptions and measurement problems that neuroimaging was supposed to transcend.

**Q:** The author of Passage 2 would most likely challenge the claim in Passage 1 that brain scans "do not depend on participants' willingness or ability to describe their inner states accurately" by arguing that:

- A) Participants in neuroimaging studies frequently move during scanning, producing motion artifacts that reduce the reliability of BOLD signal data
- B) The apparent independence from self-report is undermined by the fact that converting raw neural data into psychological conclusions requires interpretive frameworks that are no less assumption-laden than self-report measures
- C) Brain scans are less useful for studying emotional states than for studying perceptual and motor processes because emotional constructs are too diffuse to produce localized activation patterns
- D) The willingness of participants to remain still and follow task instructions during scans constitutes a form of behavioral self-report that neuroimaging studies cannot eliminate

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: The first passage is not labeled 'Passage 1' (only 'Passage 2' is labeled), though the question refers to Passage 1. Minor, since the division is still inferable.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · hard · #3 (q00813)

> Linguist Noam Chomsky's theory of universal grammar holds that human beings are born with an innate capacity for language — a set of abstract structural principles common to all human languages that children use to acquire their native tongue. The speed and uniformity with which children across all cultures acquire language, despite limited and often imperfect input, argues for a biological foundation that cannot be explained by learning alone.
> Passage 2:
> The universalist claims of Chomsky's theory have come under increasing pressure from the study of linguistic diversity. Languages vary more radically in their structural properties than universal grammar predicts — some lack features the theory designates as universal. Researchers working in the usage-based tradition argue that children acquire language through domain-general cognitive capacities — pattern recognition, social learning, and statistical inference — without requiring a dedicated linguistic module. The data from cross-linguistic diversity suggests the universal grammar framework is more a product of its limited empirical base than a window onto innate cognitive architecture.

**Q:** Based on the texts, the author of Passage 2 would most likely argue that the evidence cited in Passage 1 for universal grammar is:

- A) Conclusive proof that an innate language faculty exists in all human beings
- B) Consistent with Chomsky's theory but equally consistent with domain-general cognitive explanations, and therefore not uniquely supportive of innate grammar
- C) Based on fabricated data that does not reflect actual patterns of child language acquisition
- D) Relevant only to Indo-European languages and inapplicable to non-Western linguistic traditions

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: The first passage lacks a 'Passage 1' (or 'Text 1') label, while the second is labeled; the question refers to 'Passage 1,' so the label should be present for consistency.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · hard · #4 (q00814)

> Historian Dipesh Chakrabarty argues that the discipline of history is structured around assumptions of secular, rational time — a linear progression in which the past is categorically different from the present and the supernatural has no causal role. This framework, he contends, emerged from a specifically European Enlightenment tradition and is ill-equipped to account for the experiences and self-understandings of people for whom ancestors, spirits, and the sacred actively shape present-day life. To write subaltern history fully, historians must work at the limits of their discipline.
> Passage 2:
> The call to expand history beyond secular rationalist frameworks, while well-intentioned, risks abandoning the critical tools that make historical inquiry distinct from other forms of cultural representation. The discipline's commitment to evidence, skepticism, and the falsifiability of claims about the past is not merely a Eurocentric imposition — it is what allows historians to challenge official narratives, expose state falsifications, and recover marginalized voices from the archive. Relativizing the epistemological foundations of history may serve no one less than the very subaltern subjects the critique seeks to protect.

**Q:** Based on the texts, the author of Passage 2 would most likely argue that Chakrabarty's position, despite its stated intentions, risks:

- A) Reinforcing Eurocentric narratives by focusing too narrowly on secular rationalism
- B) Undermining the methodological foundations that enable historians to challenge power and recover marginalized voices
- C) Overemphasizing the role of religion in non-Western societies
- D) Reducing historical inquiry to a form of literary criticism

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: The first text is not labeled (it begins 'Historian Dipesh Chakrabarty argues...') while the second is labeled 'Passage 2'; the missing 'Passage 1' header breaks the standard paired-text presentation.
- difficulty_mismatch: Stated as 'hard,' but choice B is a near-verbatim paraphrase of Passage 2's final sentences ('challenge official narratives... recover marginalized voices'), while the distractors introduce ideas absent from either text. This is an easy item.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · hard · #5 (q00815)

> The philosopher argues that moral progress is real and follows a recognizable pattern: the circle of moral concern expands over time to include beings and groups previously excluded. The abolition of slavery, the extension of political rights to women and formerly colonized peoples, and the growing recognition of animal suffering all trace this arc. What drives moral progress is not changing circumstances but the pressure of philosophical argument — reason forcing us to confront the inconsistencies in our existing moral commitments.
> Passage 2:
> The narrative of moral progress as expanding circles of reason-driven concern is too tidy. Historical expansions of rights were rarely the product of philosophical argument alone — they emerged from the political struggles, material interests, and contingent alliances of social movements. The abolition of slavery, for example, was as much the product of enslaved people's resistance, economic shifts, and geopolitical calculation as it was of abolitionist philosophy. Attributing moral change primarily to reason risks flattering the role of intellectuals while obscuring the messy, interest-laden processes through which it actually occurs.

**Q:** Based on the texts, the central disagreement between the two authors concerns:

- A) Whether slavery was morally wrong
- B) Whether moral progress has occurred at all in human history
- C) Whether philosophical reasoning is the primary driver of moral progress, or whether material and political forces play a more fundamental role
- D) Whether the expansion of voting rights represents genuine moral progress

**Key:** C · **Reviewer:** C (high)
- difficulty_mismatch: Although the passages are dense, the question itself is very easy: option C restates the disagreement almost verbatim and the three distractors are transparently wrong (neither author disputes slavery's wrongness, the existence of some moral change, or voting rights). This is not a 'hard' cross-text item; a hard item would require distinguishing among plausible characterizations of the disagreement.
- formatting: The first passage is introduced as 'The philosopher argues...' with no 'Text 1' label while the second is labeled 'Passage 2,' creating inconsistent labeling. SAT convention is 'Text 1'/'Text 2' with an introductory note.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · hard · #7 (q00817)

> The political theorist argues that deliberative democracy — a model in which legitimate political decisions emerge from inclusive, reason-giving public discourse — rests on an idealized and exclusionary conception of rational argument. What counts as a "reasonable" contribution to political deliberation is itself shaped by class, education, and cultural capital. Forms of expression that fall outside the norms of formal argumentation — testimony, narrative, emotional speech — are systematically devalued in deliberative theory, effectively silencing those whose political experience cannot be easily translated into the idiom the theory demands.
> Passage 2:
> The critique of deliberative democracy as exclusionary does not follow from its premises. Deliberative theory, properly understood, does not demand that participants speak in formal philosophical prose — it requires only that contributions be offered in good faith and with openness to the force of better reasons. A personal narrative that conveys injustice can be a legitimate deliberative contribution if it expands the shared understanding from which collective decisions are made. The problem is not deliberation per se but the impoverished institutional settings in which it is often practiced.

**Q:** Based on the texts, the author of Passage 2 would most likely argue that the author of Passage 1 has:

- A) Correctly identified a flaw inherent in deliberative democratic theory that cannot be remedied
- B) Confused the theory's requirements with the inadequate institutional forms through which deliberation is often practiced
- C) Underestimated the degree to which formal argumentation dominates all political contexts
- D) Misattributed the exclusionary effects of deliberation to class rather than to race and gender

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: The first text is not labeled (it begins "The political theorist argues..." with no "Text 1"/"Passage 1" header), while the second is labeled "Passage 2" — the mismatch is slightly confusing, though the content makes the structure clear.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · hard · #9 (q00819)

> The poet argues in her essay that poetry's value lies precisely in its resistance to paraphrase. To say what a poem "means" in prose is always to lose something essential — the particular arrangement of sounds, rhythms, and silences through which the meaning is made. Poetry is not a roundabout way of saying something that could be said more directly; it is a form of knowing that cannot be translated out of its form without remainder.
> Passage 2:
> The claim that poetry is irreducibly untranslatable has been used to insulate it from criticism and accountability. If no prose statement can capture what a poem does, then no prose argument can challenge it either. This creates a protected aesthetic space in which vagueness masquerades as profundity, obscurity substitutes for depth, and the critic who points to confusion is dismissed as insufficiently sensitive to the poem's ineffable qualities. The resistance-to-paraphrase doctrine protects bad poetry as effectively as good.

**Q:** Based on the texts, the author of Passage 2 would most likely argue that the position described in Passage 1, while potentially true of some poetry, functions in practice as:

- A) A useful critical tool for distinguishing between high-quality and low-quality verse
- B) A doctrine that, by making poetry immune to prose critique, also makes it immune to accountability for its failures
- C) An accurate description of how all readers actually experience poetry
- D) A position that strengthens the case for teaching poetry interpretation in schools

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: Passage 1 is not labeled (it appears under 'Passage / text' while Passage 2 is explicitly labeled), which is a minor inconsistency but does not impede comprehension.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · hard · #10 (q00820)

> The philosopher argues that personal identity over time cannot be grounded in bodily continuity alone. The cells that make up a human body are almost entirely replaced over the course of years; the physical substrate changes even as the person persists. What grounds identity is psychological continuity — overlapping chains of memory, intention, belief, and character that connect earlier and later stages of a life. On this view, a person just is a certain pattern of psychological connectedness.
> Passage 2:
> Psychological continuity theories of personal identity face a challenge that their proponents tend to underestimate: the problem of fission. If the psychological pattern constituting a person were duplicated — as in certain thought experiments involving brain bisection and transplant — the theory generates two persons with equal claim to being the original. Yet it is incoherent to say that one person has become two. This suggests that psychological continuity is not sufficient for identity, and that personal identity may be, in the end, what some philosophers have called an "empty question" — one that does not admit of a determinate answer because there may be no deep fact of the matter.

**Q:** Based on the texts, the author of Passage 2 would most likely argue that the psychological continuity theory in Passage 1:

- A) Correctly identifies the basis for personal identity and should be accepted without qualification
- B) Provides a necessary condition for personal identity but fails to address cases where continuity cannot individuate between competing identity claims
- C) Is internally inconsistent because psychological patterns cannot be described in physical terms
- D) Successfully resolves the problem of bodily change but ignores the moral implications of personal identity

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: The first text is not labeled 'Passage 1' (it begins with 'The philosopher argues...'), while the second is labeled 'Passage 2'. The question refers to 'Passage 1,' so the missing label is a minor formatting inconsistency.
- other: Choice B asserts that psychological continuity is a 'necessary condition,' which Passage 2 never explicitly grants (it only denies sufficiency). The second half of B still clearly matches the fission objection, so B remains the best answer, but the first clause is slightly beyond the text.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · hard · #12 (q00822)

> Economist Daron Acemoglu contends that the long-run wealth of nations is best explained by the quality of their institutions — specifically, whether those institutions are "inclusive," distributing political and economic power broadly, or "extractive," concentrating it in the hands of a narrow elite. Inclusive institutions, he argues, create incentives for innovation and investment because individuals can expect to capture the returns on their efforts. Geography and culture, factors that rival theories emphasize, may shape initial conditions, but institutions determine whether those conditions translate into sustained growth or stagnation.
> Passage 2:
> Anthropologist James Scott is skeptical of institution-centered accounts of development, arguing that they tend to treat formal organizations as self-sufficient engines of prosperity while undervaluing the tacit, locally embedded knowledge that enables communities to survive and adapt. Scott's concept of "metis" — practical wisdom accumulated through experience — captures what centralized institutional reforms routinely destroy when they override customary practices in the name of legibility and efficiency. Development imposed from above, however well-intentioned and institutionally sophisticated, often unravels precisely because it cannot replicate or accommodate metis.

**Q:** Which choice best describes a central tension between the two passages?

- A) Acemoglu focuses on political institutions while Scott focuses on economic ones, producing incompatible frameworks for measuring national prosperity.
- B) Acemoglu treats formal institutional design as the primary driver of development, while Scott warns that such top-down design may undermine the local knowledge systems that development depends on.
- C) Acemoglu argues that inclusive institutions naturally generate tacit knowledge, a claim Scott rejects by citing historical cases of institutional failure.
- D) Acemoglu and Scott agree that geography is an insufficient explanation for development but disagree about whether institutions or culture provides the stronger alternative account.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: The first text is unlabeled while the second is labeled "Passage 2," so the passages are inconsistently presented (missing "Passage 1" header).

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · hard · #13 (q00823)

> Virginia Woolf argued that the novel, more than any other literary form, is capable of rendering the texture of consciousness — not merely what characters think, but how thought moves, interrupts itself, and dissolves into sensation. Her own stream-of-consciousness technique was a deliberate formal response to what she saw as the failure of Edwardian novelists like Arnold Bennett, who, she charged, furnished their fictional worlds with exhaustive material detail while leaving the interior life of their characters essentially unexamined. To catalogue a character's possessions, Woolf insisted, is not to know that character.
> Passage 2:
> Literary critic Franco Moretti has argued that the rise of the novel in the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries is inseparable from the emergence of new practices of accounting, inventory, and description — what he terms the culture of the "serious and the detailed." Far from being a limitation on the novel's capacity for depth, the enumeration of objects and domestic particulars was the form through which novelists trained readers to understand character through environment and circumstance. The Bennetts of literary history, in Moretti's view, were not avoiding interiority; they were constructing it through a different semiotic register.

**Q:** Based on the passages, Woolf and Moretti would most likely disagree about which of the following?

- A) Whether stream-of-consciousness technique represents a meaningful formal innovation in the history of the novel
- B) Whether the detailed enumeration of material circumstances can serve as a legitimate means of revealing a character's inner life
- C) Whether the novel is a more psychologically capacious form than drama or poetry
- D) Whether Edwardian novelists were conscious of the artistic choices they were making in favor of material description over interior monologue

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: The first passage is not labeled 'Text 1'/'Passage 1' (only 'Passage 2:' appears), which is slightly inconsistent with standard dual-text formatting, though the division is still clear.

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · hard · #19 (q00829)

> In her analysis of nineteenth-century domestic fiction, critic Nancy Armstrong argues that the novel did not merely reflect existing gender arrangements but actively produced them. By constructing idealized models of femininity centered on interiority, emotional refinement, and household management, novels like those of Jane Austen or Charlotte Brontë helped constitute the very category of the "domestic woman" as natural and desirable. Fiction, on Armstrong's account, was a technology of subject formation — it made certain ways of being a woman seem inevitable rather than historically contingent.
> Passage 2:
> Literary historian Mary Poovey, while sharing Armstrong's interest in the ideological work of Victorian culture, cautions against accounts that treat cultural texts as seamlessly effective instruments of social reproduction. Texts, Poovey argues, are internally contradictory: they expose the tensions and instabilities in the ideologies they appear to endorse, and it is precisely these contradictions that provide readers — especially women readers — with resources for critical distance and resistance. A novel that idealizes the domestic woman simultaneously reveals, through its formal strains and narrative anxieties, how constructed and precarious that ideal is.

**Q:** Based on the passages, Armstrong and Poovey would most likely disagree about which of the following?

- A) Whether nineteenth-century novels engaged substantially with questions of femininity and domestic life
- B) Whether cultural texts participate in the construction and dissemination of ideological norms related to gender
- C) Whether the domestic ideal promoted in nineteenth-century fiction was historically produced rather than naturally given
- D) Whether the ideological work performed by domestic fiction was as coherent and effective as its cultural dominance might suggest

**Key:** D · **Reviewer:** D (high)
- formatting: The first passage is not labeled 'Passage 1' while the second is labeled 'Passage 2,' which is inconsistent with standard dual-text presentation (though the structure remains inferable).

### R&W · Cross-Text Connections · hard · #22 (q00832)

> Proponents of universal basic income (UBI) often argue that the policy would liberate workers from the compulsion to accept degrading or unsafe employment, effectively raising the floor of acceptable working conditions. Because workers would have a guaranteed income baseline, employers would be forced to improve job quality to attract labor. Advocates see this as a correction of a fundamental power imbalance that has long favored capital over labor in wage negotiations.
> Passage 2:
> Critics of universal basic income worry that its effects on labor markets may be precisely the opposite of what advocates envision. If workers receive an unconditional income, some employers — particularly in sectors requiring low-skill labor — may respond not by improving conditions but by automating positions that had previously been filled by workers who had little choice but to accept poor conditions. The result, critics contend, could be a contraction of the very labor market that UBI was meant to improve.

**Q:** Which choice best describes a central tension between the two passages?

- A) Passage 1 argues that UBI would increase government spending, while Passage 2 contends that the spending would be offset by gains in productivity.
- B) Passage 1 claims UBI would strengthen workers' bargaining position, while Passage 2 suggests the policy might ultimately reduce the number of jobs available to workers.
- C) Passage 1 presents UBI as a form of wealth redistribution, while Passage 2 argues that redistribution is an ineffective means of addressing labor inequality.
- D) Passage 1 assumes that employers will respond rationally to labor scarcity, while Passage 2 argues that employers routinely behave irrationally in labor markets.

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- difficulty_mismatch: Labeled 'hard,' but the correct choice is a near-direct paraphrase of each passage's thesis while the three distractors introduce claims (government spending, wealth redistribution, employer irrationality) that appear nowhere in either text. A genuinely hard cross-text item would require closer discrimination among plausible restatements.

### R&W · Transitions · medium-high · #12 (q00912)

> The philosopher Simone Weil argued that genuine attention — a complete suspension of one's own thoughts in order to truly receive another person — is among the rarest and most demanding human acts. Most people, she contended, mistake sympathy or emotional reaction for real attentiveness. Weil believed that schools, properly organized, could train students in this capacity by requiring sustained engagement with difficult problems whose answers are not immediately apparent. _______ the goal of such exercises was never mere intellectual development but the cultivation of a moral disposition toward others.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text with the most logical transition?

- A) In contrast,
- B) For her,
- C) As a result,
- D) On the other hand,

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- other: "For her," functions as an attributive phrase rather than a true logical transition, which is somewhat atypical of College Board transition items; however, the elimination logic (two synonymous contrast options, and an illogical cause-effect option) makes B clearly the intended and only workable answer.

### R&W · Transitions · hard · #2 (q00932)

> Economic models of rational choice assume that individuals make decisions by calculating expected utility — weighing the probability and magnitude of potential outcomes to select the option that maximizes their benefit. ________, decades of behavioral research have documented systematic patterns in which people's actual choices deviate from this model in predictable ways: they weight losses more heavily than equivalent gains, prefer certain smaller rewards to uncertain larger ones, and make different choices depending on how options are framed, even when the underlying mathematics are identical.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text with the most logical transition?

- A) Similarly
- B) A substantial body of evidence has since demonstrated, however, that
- C) In other words
- D) As a result of this framework

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- formatting: Option B is not a transition word/phrase but a full clause ending in "that," so inserting it leaves a stray comma: "...has since demonstrated, however, that, decades of behavioral research have documented..." The sentence also becomes redundant ("evidence has since demonstrated that decades of research have documented"). SAT transition items normally offer parallel, grammatically interchangeable transitional words/phrases.
- difficulty_mismatch: Only one choice expresses contrast (A = similarity, C = restatement, D = consequence), so the item is solvable by elimination almost instantly; it is easier than the stated 'hard' tier.

### R&W · Transitions · hard · #3 (q00933)

> For much of the twentieth century, historians treated the archive as a neutral repository of facts — raw material awaiting interpretation. ________, scholars in the archival turn have argued that archives are themselves constructed objects: decisions about what to preserve, how to classify, and who has access reflect the priorities and power relations of the institutions that created them.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text with the most logical transition?

- A) Likewise
- B) More recently, however
- C) As a result
- D) For example

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- difficulty_mismatch: The contrast between 'For much of the twentieth century' and the later scholarly revision is strongly signposted, and only one choice conveys both contrast and time; this reads closer to easy/medium than 'hard.'

### R&W · Transitions · hard · #16 (q00946)

> The architect Louis Sullivan is celebrated for the dictum "form follows function," a phrase typically interpreted as a call for buildings whose exterior shapes should be dictated by their internal uses. This reading, _______, distorts Sullivan's actual position. His 1896 essay makes clear that he regarded ornament as spiritually essential to architecture, not as decorative excess to be eliminated. For Sullivan, "function" encompassed the expressive and emotional purposes a building serves, not merely its utilitarian operations — a distinction his modernist successors largely chose to ignore.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text with the most logical transition?

- A) therefore,
- B) however,
- C) moreover,
- D) in particular,

**Key:** B · **Reviewer:** B (high)
- difficulty_mismatch: The sentence contains an explicit contrast signal ('This reading... distorts Sullivan's actual position'), making 'however' obvious despite the sophisticated passage content; this plays as an easy/medium transitions item rather than hard.

### R&W · Transitions · hard · #23 (q00953)

> The development of written legal codes in ancient Mesopotamia is often celebrated as a triumph of rationalized governance. Hammurabi's Code, inscribed around 1754 BCE, enumerated penalties for offenses ranging from property theft to bodily injury, establishing a publicly visible standard to which rulers and subjects alike were nominally accountable. Scholars have noted, however, that enforcement of such codes depended heavily on local administrators whose own interests frequently diverged from the written statutes. Archaeological evidence from provincial sites reveals widespread inconsistency between codified punishments and those actually imposed. _______ the codes may have functioned more as ideological proclamations of royal authority than as operational guidelines for judicial practice.

**Q:** Which choice completes the text with the most logical transition?

- A) Nonetheless,
- B) Similarly,
- C) In other words,
- D) Thus,

**Key:** D · **Reviewer:** D (high)
- difficulty_mismatch: Labeled 'hard,' but the logic is a straightforward evidence-to-conclusion relationship; 'Thus' is readily identified and the distractors are weak (Nonetheless and Similarly are plainly wrong, and the final sentence is clearly an inference rather than a restatement).

### R&W · Rhetorical Synthesis · medium-low · #28 (q01017)

> - Biomimicry is an approach to design and engineering that draws inspiration from structures and processes found in nature.
